# Title

->**Giovanni Boccaccio**<-
->***Concerning Famous Women*** <note>original Latin: *De praeclaris mulieribus*. Written around 1361–62, this was the first collection of biographies in Western literature devoted entirely to women, featuring both historical and mythological figures.</note><-
->(Without year and place of publication) <note>original Latin: *absque anno et loco*. This phrase is used by bibliographers to indicate that the book does not state where or when it was printed, a common occurrence in the earliest days of the printing press.</note><-

<meta>This is the opening page of Giovanni Boccaccio's "De Mulieribus Claris" (Concerning Famous Women), a collection of biographies of historical and mythological women. This specific section is the dedication to Queen Joanna I of Naples.</meta>

1

**P**<note>The manuscript begins with a large, decorative initial letter 'P' painted in vibrant green and pink pigments.</note>reviously, having withdrawn a little from the common crowd and being almost free from other cares, I wrote a small book <note>original: "libellum"</note> in praise of the exceptional female sex and for the comfort of my friends, rather than for the great benefit of the state. However, while I was turning it over in my mind to whom I should first send it—so that it might not wither away in idleness in my possession, and so that, supported by the favor of another, it might go out into the public more securely—I realized that it was not suitable for a prince. Rather, since it speaks about women, it should be destined for some distinguished woman. As I was searching for one more worthy than the others, there came to my mind that shining Italian light <note>original: "italicum jubar"</note>, that most brilliant glory not only of women but also of kings: Joanna, the most serene Queen of Jerusalem and Sicily <note>Joanna I (1325–1382), the first woman to rule the Kingdom of Naples in her own right.</note>. Considering both the brilliance of her renowned lineage and ancestors, as well as the new praises she has sought for herself with a brave heart, I felt a desire to send it...

<summary>This page contains the opening dedication of Boccaccio's "Famous Women," where the author explains his decision to dedicate the work to Queen Joanna I of Naples instead of a male ruler.</summary>
<keywords>Boccaccio, Famous Women, Queen Joanna I, Naples, Sicily, dedication, female virtue, manuscript</keywords>

--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page concludes the dedication to Andrea Acciaioli and begins the general preface of Boccaccio's "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women). It continues the transition from personal address to the formal introduction of the work.</meta>

...indeed, you will be soothed in your leisure by your own favorable judgment, while you delight in feminine virtue and the charm of these histories. Nor do I think this reading will be in vain; if you emulate the outstanding deeds of these women, you will rouse your excellent mind toward better things. And though you may sometimes find wanton stories mixed with sacred ones—as the nature of storytelling requires—do not pass them over. Rather, like one in a garden of the mind, stretch out your ivory hands to gather the flowers, leaving the sharp thorns behind. Collect the things worthy of praise and set aside the shameful. 

And whenever you read something in a pagan woman <note>original: "gentali muliere"; referring to the non-Christian women of Greek and Roman antiquity</note> that is worthy of your professed Christian religion, and you realize you have not reached that standard, let a blush of shame stir your mind and reprove yourself. For you, who have been anointed with the holy chrism <note>the sacred oil used in Christian baptism and confirmation, signifying a set-apart life</note> of Christ, should surpass them in honor, modesty, and virtue. With your intellect spurred to its full strength, you should strive not only to avoid being surpassed, but to exceed all others with your outstanding virtue. Just as you are conspicuous for your joyful youth and the flowering beauty of your body, you should become even more distinguished than others—not only those of your own time but those of old—by the integrity of your soul. 

Remember that beauty is not truly adorned by pigments, as most women do, but by honor, holiness, and good works. Thus, while you give thanks to the One who granted you such grace, you will not only be radiant among women in this fleeting mortality, but when you cast off the form of humanity, you may be received into eternal love by that same Giver of Graces. Furthermore, if you deem it worthy, most excellent of women, grant this work the boldness to go forth into the public. It will go, I believe, under your protection, safe from the insults of the malicious; and it will carry your name, shining as one among the other illustrious women. Your presence, known to those here now through your merits—even where I cannot personally offer my work—will make you known to them and preserve your name for an eternal posterity. Farewell.

->**The book of Giovanni Boccaccio of Certaldo on famous women, dedicated to Andrea Acciaioli of Florence, Countess of Altavilla, begins happily.**<-

### Preface

Long ago, several of the ancients wrote short books about famous men. And in our own time, that distinguished man and outstanding poet, Francesco Petrarch <note>The famous Italian scholar and poet (1304–1374) who was Boccaccio’s close friend and mentor</note>, our teacher, is writing on the same subject with a broader scope and a sharper style—and rightly so. For those who surpassed others through their famous deeds, and who devoted all their effort, blood, and life to such opportunities, truly deserved to have their names preserved for the memory of posterity. 

Indeed, I have wondered very much why women have had so little influence among such writers that they have not earned the favor of a special record in any specific book, even though it is clear from more extensive histories that some women have acted quite vigorously. And if men are to be praised when they perform great deeds with the strength granted to them, how much more should women be honored, since by nature they are... <note>The text continues on the next page, likely discussing the perceived natural weakness women must overcome to achieve greatness.</note>

<summary>Boccaccio completes his dedication to Andrea Acciaioli, urging her to use the lives of pagan women as a moral mirror, and begins his preface by noting that while many have written of famous men, women's achievements have been unjustly neglected.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Boccaccio, Andrea Acciaioli, Francesco Petrarch, Famous Women, Christian virtue, pagan antiquity, gender roles in history</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the definition of "fame" or "renown" (claritas) and the author's intent to include both virtuous and wicked figures in this historical collection.</meta>

...to take the name of renown <note>original: "claritatis nomen." The author is debating whether "fame" should only apply to the virtuous.</note> so strictly that it always seems to result in virtue. Rather, with the reader's kind permission, I will bend it toward a broader meaning, and consider as "famous" those whom I know to be most well-known to the world through common talk, regardless of the nature of their deeds. 

For I remember having often read, among the likes of Leonidas, <note>The Spartan king famous for his stand at Thermopylae.</note> the Scipios, <note>A celebrated Roman family of generals and statesmen.</note> the Catos, and the Fabricii—illustrious men—also of the most infamous: the shifty <term>Greeks</term> <note>The author likely uses "Graecos versipelles" as a trope for cunning or deceitful characters in classical literature.</note>, the traitor Hannibal, Jugurtha, <note>A Numidian king who became a famous enemy of the Roman Republic.</note> the blood-stained Sulla and Marius (steeped in the blood of civil war), and the equally rich and greedy Crassus, and others like them. 

Truly, since extolling worthy things with praise and sometimes rebuking wicked things with reproaches will not only drive the noble toward glory and pull back the cowardly from unlucky paths with a bit of a rein, but will also restore that beauty which seems to have been taken away from this little work <note>original: "opusculo"</note> by the foulness of certain people; I have therefore decided to occasionally insert into these histories some pleasant charms of virtue, and to add stings to drive away and cause detestation of crimes. And here...

<summary>The author argues for a broad definition of "fame" that includes both heroes and villains, explaining that recording the deeds of the wicked serves as a moral deterrent and restores the balance of his historical work.</summary>
<keywords>fame, renown, virtue, vice, Leonidas, Scipio, Cato, Hannibal, Roman history, morality in history</keywords>

--- Page 6 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion on the moral endurance of holy figures, specifically transitioning from the philosophical endurance of the ancients to the spiritual constancy of holy women (likely saints or martyrs).</meta>

They were compelled toward human tolerance <note>original: "humanitati tollerantiā coegere"</note>. Imitating the commands as well as the footsteps of the sacrosanct precepts, where those men—whether by some gift of nature or instinct, or rather by a desire for this fleeting mortal brilliance—endured at times the most grievous hardships, not without a sharp strength of mind, against the impulses of <term>Venus<note>The author uses "Venus" as a classical metaphor for lust or carnal desire.</note></term> or the shifts of wandering fortune.

Furthermore, these true and shining lights in the failing darkness live most gloriously in unconquered eternity. This is through their virginity, chastity, holiness, and virtue, and through their unconquered <term>constancy<note>In this context, "constancy" (constantiam) refers to the steadfastness of faith under pressure or torture.</note></term> in overcoming both the <term>concupiscence<note>Strong carnal desires or lusts of the flesh.</note></term> of the flesh and the tortures of tyrants. 

We know that their merits have been described in individual volumes by men of old, who were distinguished for their sacred writings and venerable majesty. However, their merits are described in this present edited volume in a specific way—as has already been mentioned—that has been demonstrated by no one else until now.

<summary>This passage contrasts the worldly endurance of ancient philosophers with the eternal spiritual triumph of holy women, who overcame both carnal temptation and physical torture. The author asserts that while their lives were recorded by great men of the past, this specific volume offers a unique perspective on their merits.</summary>
<keywords>constancy, virginity, martyrs, carnal desire, tyrants, hagiography, virtue, ancient philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 7 ---
<meta>This page continues the introductory discussion of famous women, transitioning from the theological archetype of Eve to the historical/legendary figure of Semiramis. The text is from Giovanni Boccaccio’s "De Mulieribus Claris" (On Famous Women).</meta>

Nevertheless, because beauty is numbered among the primary gifts of women, and through it they have already attained much glory in the uncritical judgment of mortals, it has been placed here not redundantly among their excellences, like a primary brilliance, and it will be included in the accounts that follow. 

She <note>The author is referring to Eve, though she is not named in this specific passage.</note>, moreover, was made a citizen of Paradise both by right of her origin and her habitation, and was clothed in a splendor unknown to us while she eagerly enjoyed the delights of that place with her husband. The envious enemy of her happiness <note>The serpent or Satan</note> thrust into her mind, through wicked persuasion, the idea that she could reach even greater glory by going against the single law imposed on her by God. When she believed him with a feminine lightheartedness <note>original: "levitate feminea" — a common medieval trope suggesting that women were more easily swayed or fickle than men</note> more than was fitting for her or for us, and foolishly thought she would ascend to higher things, she first—through a certain flattering suggestion—drew her flexible husband into her opinion. 

And acting against the law of the tree of the knowledge of good and evil, once they had tasted the fruit with reckless daring, they thrust themselves and their entire future race out of peace and eternity into anxious labors and miserable death; they were cast out from a delightful homeland into the midst of briars, soil, and rocks. For when the shimmering light in which they walked went away, after they were rebuked by their blessed Creator and stripped of all their ornaments, they all came as exiles from that place of delights, driven into the fields of Hebron. 

There this remarkable woman—famous for these deeds—became the first, as some believe, to help her husband as he turned the stubborn soil with hoes, while she worked the distaff <note>A tool used in spinning thread, here symbolizing the beginning of domestic labor</note>. She frequently experienced the pains of childbirth and was equally distressed by the anxieties caused by the deaths of her sons and grandsons. Having endured cold and heat, hunger and other hardships, she reached old age, exhausted by labors and destined to die.

->**Of Semiramis, Queen of the Assyrians**<-

**Semiramis** was a distinguished and most ancient queen of the Assyrians. Time has taken away the knowledge of which parents she was born from, although it pleases the ancients to tell the fable that she was the daughter of **Neptune** <note>The Roman god of the sea</note>, whom they firmly held to be the son of Saturn and god of the sea by an erroneous belief. Even if this should not be believed, it is proof that she was born of noble parents. 

She married **Ninus**, the outstanding king of the Assyrians, and bore him a single son, also named Ninus. Truly, after Ninus had subdued all of Asia and finally the Bactrians, he died from the strike of an arrow. Since she was still a young woman and her son was a mere boy, she did not think it safe to commit the reins of so great and vast an Eastern empire to such a tender age. She possessed such a great spirit that what a fierce man [would have done] with arms...

<summary>This page recounts the Fall of Eve, detailing her transition from the glory of Paradise to a life of labor and mortality, before introducing the legendary Assyrian Queen Semiramis.</summary>
<keywords>Eve, Paradise, Fall of Man, Semiramis, Assyria, Ninus, gender roles, origin myths, labor</keywords>

--- Page 8 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Semiramis, the legendary Queen of Assyria, following the death of her husband, King Ninus.</meta>

...she had subdued nations and restrained great forces with her strength, skill, and talent—deeds that a woman would hardly dare to take upon herself. For, having devised a deception with a certain womanly cunning, she first of all deceived the armies of her great, deceased husband. It was not surprising, as Semiramis bore a very close facial resemblance to her son. Because of this, the cheeks of both were suited to a youthful age; her voice was feminine <note>original: "feminea vox" — the author suggests that a woman’s voice and a young boy’s voice were indistinguishable to the soldiers</note>, and in her physical stature, she—being only slightly larger—differed little from her son.

To assist this—so that her fraud would not be discovered as things progressed—she covered her head with a <term>tiara: a high, peaked headdress or turban worn by ancient Near Eastern monarchs</term> and hid her arms and legs with long, flowing robes. And since this style was unheard of among the Assyrians at that time, she ensured that the novelty of the clothing would not cause suspicion among the inhabitants by decreeing that the whole population should wear a similar style of dress <note>This explains the historical origin of the long, sleeved robes associated with Median and Assyrian fashion</note>. 

Thus, she who was once a wife now pretended to be a son, and a woman feigned to be a boy. Having attained royal majesty with remarkable care, she maintained it along with strict military discipline. Having disguised her sex, she performed great deeds and achieved things outstanding even for the strongest of men. And while n...

<summary>This page describes how Queen Semiramis disguised herself as her young son to seize control of the Assyrian Empire after the death of King Ninus. She successfully deceived the army and the public by introducing a new style of concealing dress that became the national fashion.</summary>

Semiramis was famously interrupted while dressing her hair and swore not to finish the braid until the city was conquered.</note> until she had forced that most potent city—which had been affected by no prior siege—into surrender, bringing it back under her dominion through the force of arms. 

A massive statue cast in bronze long stood as a testament to such a spirited deed; erected in Babylon, it depicted a woman with her hair flowing loose on one side and intricately braided on the other. Furthermore, she founded many cities from scratch and performed great deeds, which antiquity has so swallowed up that almost nothing remains of her legacy for us to praise, except for what has been recounted here. 

However, all these achievements—which would be considered marvelous, praiseworthy, and worthy of perpetual memory not only in a woman but in any vigorous man—were defiled by a single obscene lure <note>This refers to the scandalous rumors of her sexual appetites and her alleged incestuous relationship with her son, Ninyas.</note>; for when among...

## On Ops, the Wife of Saturn

Ops—or Opis, or Rhea <note>The Latin text uses "thea," likely a variant for the Greek goddess Rhea, who was equated with the Roman Ops</note> if we trust the ancients—shone with the highest renown amidst both prosperity and adversity. For she was the daughter of Uranus—who was a most powerful man among the Greeks while they were still in a primitive state—and of his wife, Vesta.

She was both the sister and the wife of King Saturn. She distinguished herself by no crime that has come down to us, but rather by her womanly cunning <term>womanly cunning: a common literary trope in historical texts referring to a woman's strategic intelligence or "mother wit"</term>. She rescued her sons Jupiter, Neptune, and Pluto from death, after a pact had been made between Saturn and his brother Titan <note>The Latin "Tutone" is a scribal variation for "Titane" (Titan). In the myth, Titan allowed Saturn to rule on the condition that he raise no male heirs</note>. 

Through the justice of that first age, and later through the madness of the men of that era, these people were elevated to the renown of supreme divinity. Nor did she only gain honor through her lineage; through the error of mortals, she was held to be a distinguished goddess and the "Mother of the Gods." Her temples, priests, and sacred rites were established by public decree. To such an extent did this enormous evil <note>The author, likely writing from a medieval Christian perspective, refers to the spread of pagan worship as an "enormous evil" or a grand delusion</note> grow strong...

<summary>This page describes the origins of the goddess Ops (Rhea), identifying her as a mortal woman of great cunning who saved her children from Saturn and was later deified by the Greeks.</summary>
<keywords>Ops, Rhea, Saturn, Uranus, Jupiter, Neptune, Pluto, Titan, Vesta, Euhemerism, Mother of the Gods, Greek Mythology</keywords>

<meta>The previous page likely discussed the birth or lineage of the Roman gods, specifically focusing on the children of Saturn and Ops, leading into this description of Juno’s upbringing on the island of Samos.</meta>

Born from the same birth as the god <note>original: "deum eodem edita partu"; referring to her brother and eventual husband Jupiter</note> and sent from infancy to Samos. There she was diligently raised until she reached puberty, and finally, she was married to her brother Jove <note>Jupiter, the king of the Roman gods</note>. This was believed for many centuries, as evidenced by a statue in the temple of Samos. For the Samians <note>the inhabitants of the Greek island of Samos</note>, thinking it brought no small glory to themselves and their descendants that Juno was prepared and betrothed while in their care—since they considered her a queen and a goddess—built a massive temple, more marvelous than any other in the world, so that this memory would not easily fade. They dedicated it to their deity and had her image sculpted from marble in the attire of a bride, placing it before their temple. Having finally married the great king, as his empire grew daily and his fame spread his name far and wide, she herself also attained no small amount of splendor. Indeed, after she was made Queen of Heaven by poetic fictions and the notorious license <note>original: "liberalitate"; here referring to the creative freedom or lack of restraint used by ancient writers</note> of the ancients...

Providing many occasions for these fables. There was, moreover, another Ceres <note>The author follows a tradition—common in the Renaissance and seen in Boccaccio’s *Concerning Famous Women*—that distinguishes between an earlier Egyptian "Ceres" (Isis) and the later Greek "Ceres" (Demeter) of Eleusis.</note> at Eleusis, a city of the Attic region, who was famous among her people for these same merits; they say that Triptolemus <note>In Greek mythology, Triptolemus was the prince of Eleusis whom Ceres/Demeter taught the art of agriculture so he could spread the knowledge across the world.</note> was obedient to her. Because antiquity elevated both women equally with divine status and honors, it seemed sufficient to record the innovations of both under a single name. By Pollux! <note>original: "edepol" — an ancient Roman oath.</note> I do not know whether to praise or curse the works of these women. For who would condemn those who once wandered in the wild? Who would condemn men led out from the forests and into the city? Who would condemn those living after the manner of wild beasts, now summoned to a better harvest? Who would condemn the changing of acorns <note>In classical literature, the "acorn-eater" was a symbol of primitive, pre-civilized humanity. The transition from acorns to grain represents the birth of civilization.</note> into crops, which provide more vigorous limbs and nourishment better suited for human use? Who would condemn a world—once blocked by wild growth, briars, and unmanaged thickets—now turned toward cultivation, beauty, and the public good? Who would condemn a crude age turned into a civil one? Who would condemn minds stirred from sloth into contemplation? Who would condemn sluggish strength, once hidden in caves, now drawn into the labors of the city or the field? By which so many cities were enlarged...

...the cultivated lands respond with anger. Immediately there follows a shortage of grain, and fasts harsher than those of ancient times arise. A savage hunger, never before known in the woods, has entered the <note>original: "gorgusciolos," likely meaning small huts or hovels</note> hovels of the poor—and not without frequent danger to the rich as well. From this comes a foul and wild thinness, a hellish paleness, and a weakness walking with a staggering step; many causes of disease and hurried death arise. Looking at these things, along with many others, I hardly know what to say. But first, I know that those former times, though they were rough and rustic, are to be preferred to these iron ages of ours <note>The "Iron Age" is a traditional term for a period of hardship and moral decline, contrasted with the "Golden Age."</note>.

# On Minerva

**Minerva**, who is also called **Pallas**, was a maiden of such conspicuous clarity and brilliance that foolish men believed she did not have a mortal origin. Some say she was first seen and known on earth in the time of King Ogygus <note>A legendary king of early Greece, often associated with a great flood.</note> at Lake Tritonis, not far from the Lesser Syrtis <note>A gulf on the coast of Libya, known today as the Gulf of Gabès.</note>. And because they had seen her doing many things over a span of time that had not been seen before, it was believed—not only among the unrefined Africans but also among the Greeks, who at that time surpassed others in wisdom—that she was born from the brain of **Jupiter** without a mother and had fallen from heaven.

This ridiculous error was given more credit because her origin was so hidden. They wished her, above all others, to have flourished in perpetual virginity; to make this more believable, they devised a story that **Vulcan**, the god of fire, struggled long with her in the heat of carnal lust but was overcome. Furthermore, they claim that before her, the art of <term>wool-working|The process of cleaning, combing, and spinning wool into yarn</term> was entirely unknown to everyone. For she showed the order in which wool, cleansed of its impurities and softened by the teeth of iron <note>Referring to wool combs or cards</note>, should be placed on the <term>distaff|A staff used in spinning to hold the unspun wool</term> and finally drawn into thread by the fingers. 

She devised the craft of weaving and taught how the threads should be intertwined and joined by the stroke of the comb and solidified into cloth by the <note>original: "calce," literally "heel," referring to the part of the loom used to pack the threads together</note> beater. In praise of this workmanship, the famous contest between her and **Arachne of Colophon** is told. Beyond this, she discovered the olive tree, which was previously unheard of by mortals; she found it and taught them to grind the berry-bearing fruit in a mill to produce oil. Because this seemed to bring so much utility, victory was attributed to her over **Neptune** in the naming of Athens. 

She also wished it to be her work when she first invented four-horse chariots, and turned iron into weapons. She taught how to cover the body with armor, how to arrange the battle line of warriors, and all the laws by which one goes into battle. They say, moreover, that she invented numbers and brought them into the order we still enjoy today. Furthermore, from the bone of a... <note>The text cuts off here, likely referring to the invention of the flute (tibia) from bone.</note>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Minerva, goddess of wisdom, detailing her iconography and the transition of her cult to Rome, followed by the beginning of the entry for Venus, Queen of Cyprus.</meta>

...reeds, or rather from marsh stalks, they believed she first invented flutes or pastoral pipes <note>original: "tibias seu pastorales fistulas"</note>, and that she threw them down from heaven to earth because the act of blowing into them made the puffer's throat swell and the face become deformed. Why say more? Because of so many discovered marvels, antiquity—the bestower of godhood—attributed the name of **Wisdom** to her. Moved by this consideration, the Athenians were named after her; and because their city seemed suited for studies through which everyone might become prudent and wise, they took her under their protection and dedicated the arts to her. Having constructed a huge temple consecrated to her divinity, they fashioned her image there with **slanting eyes** <note>original: "oculis curva." This likely refers to a side-long or piercing gaze, symbolizing the hidden nature of a wise person's thoughts.</note> because it is rarely known toward what end a wise person's intention tends. She was depicted **helmeted**, wishing by this to signify that the counsels of the wise are covered and armed; she is dressed in a **breastplate** <term>lorica: a piece of Roman body armor</term> because a wise man is always armed against whatever blows of fortune may come. She is equipped with a very long **spear**, to show that the wise person strikes with a dart at a distance. 

Furthermore, they represented her protected by a crystalline **aegis** <note>The aegis was the shield or goatskin cloak of Athena, often featuring the head of the Gorgon Medusa.</note> with the Gorgon’s head fixed upon it, intending by this to show that all coverings are transparent to a wise person. They are always so fortified by serpentine cunning that the ignorant seem **turned to stone** <note>original: "larei," likely a transcription error for "lapidei" (stony), referring to the Gorgon's power to petrify.</note> at their sight. In her protection they placed an **owl**, asserting that just as in the light, so also in the dark should the prudent be able to see. 

Finally, the fame of this woman and the reverence for her divinity spread so far and wide—and indeed the course of events favored her—that temples were constructed in her honor and sacred rites celebrated almost throughout the whole world. She rose so high that in the Capitol, next to **Jupiter Optimus Maximus** <note>The supreme Roman deity; his title translates as "Jupiter, the Best and Greatest."</note>, they dedicated a shrine to her, and among the most powerful gods of the Romans, she held equal sovereignty with Queen Juno herself. However, there are some very serious men who assert that there was not just one Minerva, but many more than have been mentioned. I would willingly agree with this, so that the number of famous women might be even greater.

### Concerning Venus, Queen of the Cypriots.

It is the opinion of some that the **Cyprian Venus** was a mortal woman. There is doubt among some concerning her parents. For some will have it that she was the daughter of a certain Cyprus and Syria; others, of Cyprus and Dione, a Cyprian woman. Some, to extol the brilliance of her beauty, assert she was fathered by Jupiter and the aforementioned Dione. Truly, from whomever she may have been born, I have judged that she should be described among famous women rather for her illustrious beauty than for any **shameful invention** <note>The author likely refers to the tradition that Venus "invented" prostitution or promoted loose morality among women.</note>. She shone with such beauty of face and grace of the whole body that the belief of those looking upon her was often deceived. For some...

--- Page 15 ---
<meta>This page continues a historical and mythological examination of the life of Venus, treating her not as a literal goddess but as a mortal woman whose life became the basis for later myths (a perspective known as Euhemerism).</meta>

They called her that very star of heaven which we name Venus. Others said she was a celestial woman who fell to earth from the lap of Jupiter. In short, all of them—darkened by a blind mist—even though they knew she was born of a mortal woman, asserted that she was an immortal goddess. They affirmed with all their might that she was the mother of that ill-fated love which they called Cupid. Nor did she lack the arts of deceiving the minds of foolish onlookers with various gestures. Through these deeds, it came to pass that because of their [the people's] lack of merit, they were unable to resist many obscenities (not all of which I am about to write down), and she was held to be a daughter of Jupiter and even one of the most venerable goddesses.

Nor was she appeased by incense alone at Paphos <note>An ancient city on the island of Cyprus, famous for its temple to Venus.</note>, the most ancient town of the Cypriots. For they thought that a dead and incestuous woman delighted in that scent—she who, while living, wallowed in the filth and pleasures of brothels. This was also true among other nations and the Romans, who long ago built temples to her under the title of **Venus Genitrix** <note>original: "veneris genitricis"; Venus the Mother, ancestress of the Roman people</note> and **Venus Verticordia** <term>Verticordia: "The Turner of Hearts," a title given to Venus because she was believed to turn women's hearts toward virtue and chastity.</term> and other designations. 

But because it is widely believed she married two men—though it is not quite certain to whom she was first married—it pleases some that she first married Vulcan, king of the Lemnians and son of the Cretan Jupiter <note>The author distinguishes the "Cretan Jupiter" from the sky-god, following the theory that the gods were originally human kings from Crete.</note>. After he was gone, she married Adonis, son of Cinyras and Myrrha, who was king of the Cypriots. This seems more likely to me; for if we said Adonis was her first husband, then—whether through a fault of her own temperament, or the infectious nature of the region where lewdness seems to hold great power, or the malice of a corrupt mind—it happened that after Adonis died, she fell into such a great itch of lust that she seemed to have stained all the brightness of her beauty with frequent and undisguised fornications. 

Since it was known in the adjacent regions that she was caught by Vulcan, her first husband, with a soldier, from this began the believed fable of the adultery of Mars and herself. Finally, however, so that she might seem to have wiped away a little of the shame from her shameless brow and gained for herself a greater license for lewdness, having devised an unspeakable depravity, she first (as they say) invented public prostitution and established brothels, and even compelled matrons to enter them. This is the cursed custom of the Cypriots, which many passing centuries have witnessed. They long observed the practice of sending their virgins to the shores so that they might engage in intercourse with strangers, and so [become] future...

...they were seen to have offered up the first fruits of their chastity, as if seeking the gifts of Venus through their marriages. This abominable folly eventually reached as far as the Italians, as we read that the Locrians <note>A people of southern Italy known for the Epizephyrian Locri colony</note> once practiced the same custom.

# X. Concerning Isis, Queen and Goddess of the Egyptians

->**Isis**, whose name was formerly **Io**, was a most illustrious woman. She was not only a queen of the Egyptians but eventually became their most holy and venerable deity <term>numen; a spiritual force or divinity presiding over a place or people</term>. However, among famous historians, there is much debate regarding the era in which she lived or who her parents were.<-

There are those who claim she was the daughter of Inachus, the first king of the Argives <note>Inhabitants of Argos in Greece</note>, and the sister of Phoroneus. These figures are known to have lived during the lifetime of Jacob, the son of Isaac <note>A reference to the biblical patriarchs used here to synchronize classical mythology with sacred history</note>. Others assert that she was the daughter of Prometheus and lived while Phorbas reigned in Argos, which occurred long after the first period mentioned. Some say she lived during the time of Cecrops, the king of Athens, and flourished during the era of the Argives. Because these differing opinions among celebrated men are well-supported by various arguments, it is at least clear that she was an extraordinary woman among those of her own age.

<summary>This page concludes a discussion on the spread of pagan marriage customs to Italy before beginning a biography of Isis, exploring her origins and attempting to place her within a historical timeline alongside biblical figures and Greek kings.</summary>
<keywords>Isis, Io, Egypt, Argos, Inachus, Jacob, Prometheus, Locrians, Goddesses, Ancient Chronology</keywords>

--- Page 17 ---
...to sow seeds in cultivated fields, and finally to process the gathered crops into food in due season. Moreover, she brought together those who were wandering and living almost like wild animals, giving them laws to live by in a civilized manner. She also—which is far more remarkable for a woman—by applying the powers of her intellect, showed them an alphabet <note>original: "litterarum idiomati"; the author refers here to the invention of Egyptian hieroglyphs or script</note> for the language, so that they might be bound together by law. These achievements appeared so miraculous to people unaccustomed to such things that they easily believed Isis had not come from Greece, but had fallen from heaven. Because of this, they established divine honors for her while she was still living. Indeed, her divinity—with the devil deceiving the ignorant <note>The author uses a common medieval and Renaissance explanation that pagan "gods" were actually historical figures whose memory was later corrupted by demonic influence into false worship.</note>—grew into such great and famous veneration after her death that a massive temple was built for her in Rome, now the mistress of the world, and a solemn annual rite in the Egyptian style was established. There is no doubt that this error penetrated even to the western and barbarian nations. Furthermore, the husband of this most famous woman was Apis, whom mistaken antiquity believed to be the son of Jupiter and Niobe, the daughter of Phoroneus. They say that after yielding the kingdom of Achaia to his brother Aegialeus and reigning in Argos for thirty-five years, he withdrew to Egypt; there he ruled together with Isis, was regarded as a god, and was called Osiris or Serapis <note>Serapis was a Graeco-Egyptian deity; the author is synchronizing various names for the same mythological/historical figure.</note>. Let it be that there are some who say Isis had a certain "theologian" as a husband, and from him bore Epaphus, who later ruled the Egyptians and was esteemed as the son of Jupiter by her.

[space]

->**Concerning Europa, Queen of the Cretans**<-

Some believe Europa was the daughter of Phoenix, but far more say she was the daughter of Agenor, King of the Phoenicians. She was said to possess such miraculous beauty that Jupiter <note>The author treats Jupiter here as a powerful "Cretan ruler" rather than a literal god.</note> was captured by love for her. When this powerful man was planning her abduction, he intended it to be done through the smooth talk of a certain person, so that the playful maiden would follow her father's herds from the mountains to the Phoenician shore. From there, she was suddenly snatched away and placed upon a ship that had a white bull as its figurehead <note>original: "albus thaurus erat inſigne"; a rationalized explanation of the myth where Zeus turns into a bull.</note>, and was carried off to Crete. I judge that allowing young women too much freedom to wander and to lend a ready ear to anyone's words is not at all to be praised. For I have often read that when these things happen, shameful marks are sometimes stamped upon one's honor, which even the lasting glory of subsequent chastity could not later wipe away.

--- Page 18 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of famous women from antiquity, likely from a version of Giovanni Boccaccio's "De Mulieribus Claris" (On Famous Women). The previous context would have introduced Europa, daughter of Agenor.</meta>

From these accounts, it clearly appears that the fable took its origin—the one which reads that Mercury <note>The Roman messenger god, often depicted driving the cattle of the gods</note> drove the herds to the shore with him, and that Jove <note>Jupiter or Zeus</note>, turned into a bull, carried away the maiden Europa while she was at leisure to the island of Crete. 

Ancient writers disagree, however, on the time of this abduction. For those who place it in an earlier age say it happened during the reign of Danaus at Argos; others prefer a second opinion, that it occurred while Ocasius reigned; and finally, there are those who place it under King Pandion's rule over the Athenians. This last date seems to coincide with the era of Minos <note>Original: "Mynops", a variant spelling for the legendary King Minos of Crete</note>, the son of Europa. Some simply maintain that she was ravished by Jove, and that she afterwards married Asterius, King of the Cretans, bearing him three sons: Minos, Rhadamanthus, and Sarpedon. Most say these were the sons of Jove, though some assert that Asterius and Jove were the same person.

While this dispute is for others to settle, many wish to see Europa made famous by her marriage to such a great god. They further affirm that she was of extraordinary nobility, for the Phoenicians, through many merits, were more renowned than others for the distinction of their ancestors. Whether by reason of her dominion and husband, or by the grace of her royal sons, or by Europa's own exceptional virtue, the third part of the world <note>In ancient and medieval geography, the world was divided into three parts: Asia, Africa (then often called Libya), and Europe.</note> was forever named "Europe" after her. I certainly regard her as a woman distinguished by her virtues, not only because her name was granted to a continent, but because of a remarkable bronze statue dedicated by the illustrious philosopher Pythagoras <note>Original: "pictagora illustri phō". Pythagoras was frequently cited in medieval texts as an authority on ancient monuments and symbols.</note>, which, though lacking the name, was dedicated to Europa.

### Concerning Thisbe, the Babylonian maiden.

**T**hisbe, a Babylonian maiden, became more famous among mortals for the outcome of her unhappy love than for any other deed. It has not even been recorded by our ancestors who her parents were; nevertheless, it is sufficiently believed that she lived within Babylon in a house adjoining that of Pyramus, a boy of her own age. Since they were neighbors by right, and because of that constant proximity, a unique chance drove a childish affection in them while they were still children. As the years grew, and since both were most beautiful, that childhood love increased into a great fire <note>The author uses the metaphor of an "incendium" or great fire to describe the intensity of their adolescent passion.</note>.

And they revealed this between themselves at least by nods sometimes, as they were now approaching the age of puberty. Indeed, when Thisbe became a bit older, she began to be kept at home by her parents for future marriage rites <note>original: "hymeneos"</note>. When both bore this most heavily, and they sought anxiously for some way they might at least speak sometimes, they found a small crack—previously seen by no one—located in the common wall. When they had met there secretly many times, and by the habit of speaking a little, the wall acting as a barrier making them blush less, they increased the freedom of expressing their affections. Often they revealed their sighs, tears, fervors, desires, and all their passions; sometimes they even prayed to each other for peace of mind, for embraces and kisses, and for devotion, faith, and perpetual love. 

Finally, as the fire of their love grew, they decided to make a plan for flight on the following night, so that each might first deceive their own household and leave their homes. If one should escape first, they would go to the grove nearest the city and wait for the slower one near the fountain by the tomb of King Ninus <note>Ninus was the mythical founder of Nineveh, often associated in these legends with the husband of Semiramis and the builder of Babylon’s monuments.</note>. By chance, the more burning promise of Thisbe deceived her own family, and wrapped in a cloak in the dead of night, she left her father’s house alone. With the moon showing the way, she went trembling into the grove; and while she waited by the fountain, at every movement of a thing—

<meta>This page concludes a discussion on the tragic nature of young love (likely following the story of Pyramus and Thisbe) and begins a new chapter on the life of Hypermestra, one of the fifty daughters of Danaus.</meta>

Who would not grant at least one small tear to these youths for their so unhappy end? He would be made of stone. The children loved; they did not deserve a bloody fate because of this misfortune. It is a fault of their flowering age, yet not a horrific one for those who are free. This fault can even lead toward marriage. Indeed, perhaps the worst of luck was to blame, or perhaps their wretched parents sinned. Truly, the impulses of the young should be restrained gradually, lest while we wish to oppose them with a sudden barrier, we drive those in despair over a precipice. The passion of desire is of immoderate strength and is a plague—a common disgrace—among almost all adolescents. By Hercules <note>original: "edepol," an archaic Latin oath</note>, it must be tolerated with a patient mind, because it happens by the will of nature that while we are strong in age, we are naturally inclined toward it, lest the human race fall into decline if mating were deferred until old age.

### Concerning Hypermestra, Queen of the Argives <note>The Argives were the people of the ancient Greek city of Argos.</note>

**H**ypermestra was noble in lineage and dignity, the daughter of Danaus, King of the Argives, and the wife of Lynceus. It is gathered from the histories of the ancients that there were once two brothers in Egypt, the sons of King Belus, who were preeminent in their remarkable but wicked power. One of them was Danaus; the other was named Aegyptus <note>original: "egistus," a common medieval variant for Aegyptus</note>. Fortune was not equal to both regarding their offspring, even if the number was equal. For Danaus had fifty daughters, and Aegyptus had just as many sons. Truly, when Danaus had received an oracle stating that he would be killed by the hand of a nephew (a son of his brother), he was secretly tormented by fear; at first, he did not know whose hands he should hold suspect among such a vast multitude. It happened that when the children of both were reaching maturity, Aegyptus requested that all the daughters of Danaus be joined in marriage to his sons. Danaus, having devised a cruel crime, granted this of his own accord. Once the daughters had been betrothed to his nephews and the marriage rites were being prepared, he warned them all most urgently that if they wished for their own safety, each one should slay her husband with the blade on the first night, while he was soaked with wine and feasting and bound in heavy sleep. All of them, having secretly carried knives into their bedchambers, killed the young men as they were failing from their excessive drinking, according to their father's command. But Hypermestra alone abstained; for the maiden had already set her heart upon Lynceus <note>original: "linu" or "linteu," likely a scribal corruption of her husband's name, Lynceus</note>, her husband, as is the custom... <margin>The rest of the marginal note is cut off.</margin>

...of the young women, immediately upon seeing her groom, she began to love him. For this reason, moved by compassion for him—much to her great honor—she held back from the abominable slaughter. She urged the youth to flee to a place where he might be safe. 

Truly, when the cruel father <note>King Danaus of Argos, who commanded his fifty daughters to kill their husbands, the sons of Aegyptus, on their wedding night.</note> had applauded the others in the morning for the crime they had perpetrated, Hypermnestra alone was scolded and locked in a dungeon, where she wept for some time over her dutiful act. <note>original: *pium opus*. The author highlights the irony that she is punished for "piety" or "duty" toward her husband and the gods, which her father viewed as a crime.</note> 

Alas, miserable mortals, with what covetous and fervent spirits we grasp for things that are destined to pass away. Refusing to look upon our own downfall, through what execrable ways do we scale the heights (if we can reach them). By what crimes do we then maintain the status we have climbed to! It is as if we imagine that fickle Fortune <note>The Roman goddess Fortuna, often depicted with a wheel to show how quickly life can change.</note> can be made stable by shameful riches. And how absurd it is: through what sins and villainous deeds do we try—I will not say to extend, but to make permanent—this flying and fragile "little day" <note>original: *dieculam*, a word emphasizing the pitifully short duration of human life.</note> of our life. Especially as we see others passing into death...

[He] reserved for himself the settled ages of his shameful crime. And he who by right could have had fifty sons-in-law, preserved only one, who served as an ill-deserved enemy. Finally, by the just judgment of God's hand, the fierce old man could not escape; he poured out that harmful blood which he had redeemed with the blood of so many grandsons <note>original: "nepotum." While Danaus was the uncle and father-in-law of the murdered men, medieval Latin often uses this term for nephews or descendants generally.</note>. 

Whether driven out, or a fugitive, or summoned, he finally crossed the sea into Greece and held the kingdom of the **Argives** <term>Argives</term> <note>The people of Argos, a major power in ancient Greek mythology and history.</note>, having seized it by both his wit and his strength. He was the only son of Aegyptus to survive the wedding night.</note>, a man of grim memory. And in his place, Linus himself reigned over the Argives.

--- Page 25 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of Niobe’s downfall and begins the biography of Hypsipyle, following the typical structure of Giovanni Boccaccio’s "Of Famous Women" (De Mulieribus Claris).</meta>

While she watched, a deadly plague consumed all her children, who were then blooming in beautiful youth; within a short space of time, they were taken, down to the last one. And Amphion <note>King of Thebes and husband of Niobe</note>, who had suddenly been bereft of the fourteen children he had fathered, was driven by grief to pierce himself with a sword by his own hand. The Thebans believed these things happened because of the gods' vengeance for her <term>countless insults</term> <note>original: "innummis iniuriarum" — referring to Niobe’s boasting that she was superior to the goddess Latona because she had more children</note>. 

Niobe, however, surviving so many funerals as a mournful widow, fell into such a great and stubborn silence that she seemed more like an immovable rock than a woman. For this reason, it was later written by poets that at Mount Sipylus <note>A mountain in Lydia, modern-day Turkey, where a rock formation resembling a weeping woman still exists</note> (where her children had been buried), she was turned into a stone statue. 

It is a hard and hateful thing first of all to tolerate, let alone look upon, proud men. But in women, pride is loathsome and unbearable. For nature produced men with a boiling and elevated spirit, but it brought forth women of a mild disposition and gentle virtue, better suited to elegance <note>original: "lautitiis"</note> than to the exercise of power <note>original: "imperiis"</note>. Therefore, it is less surprising if the wrath and judgment of God falls upon proud women whenever they happen to exceed the boundaries of their natural weakness, as the foolish Niobe did. She was deceived by the trickery of fortune and was ignorant that being the parent of a large family was not the merit of the mother, but the work of nature turning the benevolence of heaven toward her. 

It would have been enough, therefore, for her first to have given thanks to God with her eyes <note>meaning through devout prayer or humble tears</note> rather than seeking divine honors for herself, as if it had been her own doing to have birthed such a numerous and conspicuous offspring. Because she acted with pride rather than prudence, she brought it about that she wept for her misfortune while alive, and after many centuries, her name would be hated by posterity.

### Of Hypsipyle, Queen of Lemnos

Hypsipyle was a woman distinguished both for her devotion to her father and for her unhappy exile, as well as for the death of her charge, Archemorus <note>The text reads "Artheniorum," likely a scribal error for the infant Archemorus, whom Hypsipyle was nursing when he was killed by a serpent</note>, and the timely recovery of her sons. For she was the daughter of Thoas, King of the Lemnians, reigning at that time when a madness entered the minds of the women of the island to withdraw their untamed necks entirely from the yoke of men <note>A reference to the mythological massacre where the women of Lemnos murdered all the men on the island</note>. 

For, having weighed the rule of the aged king as being of little value, and having brought Hypsipyle in with them, they came to a unanimous decision: that on the following night they would rage with swords against every male. Nor was the deed lacking from their purpose. Indeed, while they were softening <insert>their hearts</insert>...

A milder plan occurred to the mind of Hypsipyle <note>Hypsipyle was the Queen of Lemnos; according to myth, the women of the island killed all the men, but she secretly saved her father.</note> than to the others. For thinking it would be inhuman to be defiled by her father’s blood, she revealed the crime of the other women to her father and placed him on a ship so that he might escape the public's anger to Chios <note>original: "chium"; an island in the Aegean Sea.</note>. Immediately, having constructed a massive pyre, she pretended to perform her final duty to her father <note>By building a funeral pyre, she tricked the other women into believing she had killed him as they had killed their own male relatives.</note>. Since this was believed by everyone, she was placed upon her father's throne in the place of the king, and was appointed queen over those wicked women.

Indeed, the <term>piety</term> <note>original: "pietas"; here meaning the moral duty, loyalty, and natural affection owed by children to their parents.</note> of children toward their parents is a most holy thing. For what is more fitting, what is more just, or what is more praiseworthy than to return the favor with kindness and honor to those by whose labor we, when we were weak, received nourishment? By their skill we were protected, and by their unceasing love we were guided into adulthood. We were instructed in morals and learning, and enriched with honors and resources; we are strong in both talent and character because of them. Since all these things were bestowed with such care by Hypsipyle upon her parent, she is not undeservedly added to the ranks of <term>illustrious women</term> <note>original: "illustribus... mulieribus"; this text is likely from or based on Giovanni Boccaccio’s 14th-century work "De Mulieribus Claris" (On Famous Women).</note>.

Therefore, while she was reigning—whether driven by the force of the winds or having turned aside by design—Jason <note>The leader of the Argonauts on the quest for the Golden Fleece.</note> arrived with the Argonauts returning from Colchis <note>A region on the coast of the Black Sea.</note>, despite the efforts of those who tried in vain to prevent it...

# On Medea, Queen of the Colchians.

[Large initial M is missing] Medea is a most cruel example of ancient treachery. She was the daughter of Aeetes, the most famous King of the Colchians <note>Colchis was an ancient kingdom on the eastern coast of the Black Sea, in modern-day Georgia.</note>, and his wife Perse. She was beautiful enough, and by far the most learned in the dark arts; for by whatever master she was instructed, she held such a familiar grasp of the powers of herbs that no one knew them better. She clearly knew how to disturb the heavens with a chanted incantation, to summon the winds from their caves, to stir up storms, to stop the flow of rivers, to concoct poisons, and to prepare elaborate fires for any conflagration—and to accomplish all things of this sort. Nor was her spirit (which was far worse) discordant from her arts. For when her magic failed, she was prepared to use the sword.

She ardently loved Jason the Thessalian, a youth conspicuous for his virtue in that age, who had been sent to Colchis by his uncle Pelias—who was plotting against Jason’s integrity—under the pretext of a most glorious expedition to steal the Golden Fleece <note>The Golden Fleece was the fleece of a golden-haired winged ram, sought by Jason and the Argonauts as a symbol of kingship.</note>. Having been captured by Jason’s excellence, she acted to earn his favor by first stirring up a rebellion among the inhabitants, leading to a war that would provide Jason the time and space to achieve his vow. Who, even a sensible man, would think that from a single glance of the eyes, the destruction of a most wealthy king would follow?

Therefore, once that crime was committed and she had earned the embrace of the beloved youth, she secretly took flight with him, dragging away all her father’s wealth. Not content with such a great crime, she turned her fierce mind toward worse things. Thinking that Aeetes would follow the fugitives, she decided to stop him at the island of Tomis <note>original: "Thomitania"</note> (through which the pursuer would have to pass). She ordered that Absyrtus (or Aegialeus), a young boy and her own brother—whom she had brought with her as a companion in this flight—be butchered and his small limbs scattered here and there. Her plan was that while the father was wretchedly gathering the scattered pieces to give them tears and a tomb, he would also be providing the fugitives time for their escape. Nor did her calculated opinion fail her; so it was done.

At last, when after many wanderings she had arrived in Thessaly with her Jason, she filled her father-in-law, Aeson, with such great joy—both from the return of his son and from the celebrated victory, the booty, and the illustrious marriage—that he seemed restored to his flourishing youth. To prepare the kingdom for Jason, she used her arts to sow discord <term>zizania</term> <note>The word "zizania" refers to tares or weeds, a biblical metaphor for sowing discord or strife.</note> between the daughters of Pelias and Pelias himself. She miserably armed the daughters against their father <note>According to myth, Medea tricked the daughters into killing and boiling their father Pelias by promising it would make him young again.</note>. However, as the years slipped by, she became hateful to Jason, and in her place, he took Creusa, the daughter of Creon, King of the Corinthians.

<summary>This page recounts the mythological life of Medea, detailing her mastery of sorcery and the ruthless crimes she committed—including the murder of her brother and her uncle-in-law—to aid Jason and secure power.</summary>
<keywords>Medea, Jason, Golden Fleece, sorcery, Colchis, Pelias, Absyrtus, Aeetes, mythology, dark arts</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the mythological sorceress Medea, following her betrayal of her father for Jason and leading into a moral discourse on the dangers of the wandering eye.</meta>

Impatient and raging, after she had devised many schemes against Jason, she broke out into such a fury that by her own cunning <note>The original Latin "ingenio suo" implies both her innate nature and her skill in magic.</note> she consumed Creusa, and all of King Creon’s palace, with a flying fire. While Jason watched, she slaughtered the sons she had borne him and fled to Athens. There, she was married to King Aegeus; and when she had borne him a son named Medus, and had unsuccessfully tried to kill the returning Theseus <note>Theseus was Aegeus's first son; Medea tried to poison him to ensure her own son Medus would inherit the throne.</note> with poison, she took flight for a third time. And when she had returned to Jason's favor, after they were both driven out of all Thessalia by Aegialeus, the son of Pelias, she returned home to Colchis and restored her aged and exiled father to his kingdom. What she finally did, or under what sky or by what kind of death she ended her days, I do not remember reading nor hearing.

But lest I should overlook it, one must not grant every liberty to the eye. For it is through their looking that we recognize splendors, we introduce them into the world, and we attract all desires. Through their action, greed is excited; beauty is praised; filth and poverty are unworthily condemned. And since the eyes are unlearned judges <note>The author uses a common medieval trope: the eyes "see" but do not "understand" truth, making them dangerous guides.</note> and believe only in the outward appearance of things, they very often prefer shameful fictions to the sacred, true, and serious things. And while they commend things that should be rejected, and within a short span of time entice the soul, they sometimes infect minds with the foulest stain. 

These ignorant eyes are captured, dragged, seized, and held by beauty—even dishonest beauty—by lascivious gestures, and by youthful insolence in biting waves. And since they are the door of the heart, through them lust sends its messages to the mind; through them desire breathes out many sighs and kindles all its fires; through them the heart sends forth its groans and shows its seductive passions. 

If anyone were truly wise, they would either close them, or raise them to heaven, or sink them into the earth; for there is no safe path for the eyes between these two extremes. But if they must be used at all, they must be restrained by a sharp bridle lest they run wild. Nature provided them with doors <note>This refers to the eyelids.</note>—not only to be closed in sleep, but to resist harmful things. Indeed, if the powerful Medea had closed them, or turned them elsewhere while she gazed greedily upon Jason, her father’s power would have stood longer, her brother’s life would have been saved, and the honor of her virginity would have remained unbroken—all of which perished through the immodesty of these eyes.

<summary>This page concludes the story of Medea's vengeful acts and subsequent travels before transitioning into a moralizing lecture on how the eyes act as a dangerous gateway for lust and sin. The author argues that Medea's tragic downfall began simply because she allowed her eyes to look greedily upon Jason.</summary>

### Concerning Arachne, the woman of Colophon.

Arachne was a woman of Asia and of common birth, being the daughter of Idmon of Colophon <note>Colophon was an ancient Greek city in Ionia, famous in antiquity for its luxury and its production of resins and dyes.</note>, a dyer of wool. Although she was of humble origin, she is nevertheless to be extolled for several merits. Certain ancient writers assert that the use of linen was her invention. They also believe she was the first to devise nets for fowling or fishing and to cast them upon the ground. And since her son, whose name was Closter <term>Closter</term> <note>From the Greek word for "spinner" or "spindle."</note>, had discovered the spindles suitable for wool-working, some believe that she held the leadership of the art of weaving in her age. She was of such great talent in this regard that she performed with her fingers, threads, and the <term>spatula</term> <note>A weaving tool, likely a weaving-slat or batten used to compress the weft threads.</note> and other tools appropriate to that office what a painter does with a brush—truly a skill not to be despised in a woman. 

Indeed, while she not only lived among weavers <note>original: "ypheis," a Latinization of the Greek word for weavers.</note> but heard her fame celebrated everywhere, she became so puffed up with pride that she dared to enter into a contest against Pallas <note>Pallas Athena, the goddess of wisdom, weaving, and crafts.</note>, the very discoverer of this art. And when she could not bear with an even mind being surpassed, she ended her life with a noose. From this, an opportunity was given to those who create fables; for since the name for the insect, the spider <note>original: "aranea"</note>, is similar to Arachne, and since it hangs by a thread just as she herself hung in the noose, they said that out of the mercy of the gods, Arachne was turned into a spider, and that she might continue her former care in her perpetual labor. 

Others truly say that although she put on the noose intending to die, she did not actually die, as she was saved by the help of her family; however, having set aside her grief, she spent her time in her skillful craft. Now, if there is anyone, I pray, who believes they surpass others in anything, let them say if they think Arachne herself could have turned the heavens or drawn all honors into herself. Or rather, could she have made God himself, the giver of all things, so kind to her through her prayers and merits that he would be forced to pour all his graces upon her from his generous heart, ignoring all others? 

But why do I ask this? For it seems, by Hercules, a most foolish thing to believe. Nature, by an eternal law, unites the heavens and provides talents suited for various things to all people. These talents, I believe, remain sluggish through idleness and sloth, but are made bright through study and practice, becoming capable of the greatest things. Driven by that same nature, people are carried by a desire for the knowledge of all things, even if they do not possess the same cleverness or fortune. And if this is so, what is there to prevent many people from being made equals in the same field? And for that reason, why should anyone think themselves the only one of importance among such an innumerable multitude of mortals?

<summary>This page recounts the legend of Arachne, a commoner credited with inventing linen and nets, whose pride in her weaving led to a tragic contest with the goddess Pallas Athena. The author uses her story to argue that talent is a product of nature and practice, warning against the vanity of thinking oneself uniquely gifted.</summary>
<keywords>Arachne, weaving, Pallas Athena, Idmon of Colophon, invention of linen, spinning, spiders, human pride, nature and talent</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the critique of Arachne's pride and transitions into the biographies of famous women from antiquity, specifically focusing on the Amazonian queens Orithia and Antiope, followed by the Erythraean Sibyl. This text is consistent with the style of Boccaccio's "Famous Women."</meta>

...to believe that one prevails over others in the race for glory is the mark of a dull mind. I would truly wish that Arachne were our only laughingstock in this regard, yet countless others are ensnared in such great madness. While they elevate themselves toward the precipice of dull presumption, they make Arachne appear less ridiculous by comparison.

### Of Orithia and Antiope, Queens of the Amazons

**Orithia** was the daughter of Marpesia. Together with **Antiope**, whom some believe to have been her sister, she became queen of the Amazons after Marpesia. Above all else, she was distinguished and highly commendable for her perpetual virginity. However, alongside her co-regent Antiope, she excelled so greatly in warfare that she expanded the Amazonian empire with many honors. She elevated the reputation of her military discipline to such an extent that **Eurystheus**, the king of Mycenae, judged that her belt <note>original: *baltheum*. The quest for the Amazonian belt was the ninth labor of Hercules.</note> could only be won through war. For this reason, it is said he commanded the indebted Hercules, as his greatest task, to bring that belt back to him.

Indeed, it is an extraordinary glory for a woman that **Hercules**, who conquered all things, was set against her because of the splendor of her courage in arms. When he had entered upon his expedition and his fleet <note>original: *longis navibus*, literally "long ships," referring to warships</note> reached the shore of the Amazons while Orithia was away, the Amazons—thrown into confusion by their small numbers and lack of preparation—provided him an easy victory. Both **Menalippe** and **Hippolyte**, the sisters of Antiope, were captured. Once the queen's belt was surrendered, Menalippe was restored.

But when Orithia heard that **Theseus**, Hercules’ companion on the expedition, had carried Hippolyte away, she dared to stir up war against all of Greece, having gathered allies from every side. Yet, abandoned by her allies due to disagreement and separated by the Athenians, she returned to her kingdom. I do not recall finding anything further regarding her deeds.

### Of the Erythraean or Eriphila, a Sibyl

**The Erythraean**, or **Eriphila**, was a woman and one of the most distinguished of the Sibyls <note>A Sibyl was an ancient female prophet believed to be possessed by a deity.</note>. Some believe there were ten such Sibyls in number. They designate them by their proper names, and because they all excelled greatly in prophecy, they gave them this common name. For in the "celestial language" <note>This refers to a medieval folk etymology of Greek.</note>, *sios* sounds like "God" <note>original: *deus*</note> in Latin, and they said *biles* means "mind." Therefore, "Sibyls" are named as if they were "divine in mind" or "bearing God in their mind." Among all these venerable women, it is asserted that this one was the most famous, and that her origin was among the Babylonians some time before the Trojan War. Although some...

Her name (as some say) was Eriphila <note>original: "Eriphila," likely a variant of Herophile, a name often associated with the Sibyls</note> or Erythraea, so named because she stayed for a long time at the island of Erythraea <note>Erythrae was actually a city in Ionia (modern Turkey), but was sometimes referred to as an island in older traditions</note>, and many of her songs were found there. 

Therefore, such was the merit of her talent, her speech, or her devotion in the sight of God that, through watchful study and not without a divine gift, she deserved (if what is read was truly said by her) to describe things poured forth with such clarity that it seems more like a Gospel <note>The author suggests her prophecies of Christ were so accurate they read like eyewitness accounts</note> than a prophecy. And—what is far greater in my judgment—she revealed the secrets of the divine mind, though only through figures...

<meta>This page continues the biography of Medusa, transitioning from a description of her physical presence to a rationalized historical account of her myth, followed by a moralizing essay on the dangers of wealth, and concluding with the beginning of the life of Iole.</meta>

...she had a noble body of worthy height. But among her other great qualities, there was such a calm power in her eyes that those upon whom she looked kindly were rendered nearly motionless and lost to themselves. <note>The author is providing a "rational" explanation for the myth of Medusa turning men to stone; here, it is attributed to her beauty and commanding presence rather than magic.</note> Furthermore, some assert that she was highly skilled in agriculture. It is said she acquired the nickname **Gorgon** from this expertise. <note>original: "gorgonis." The author likely derives this from a Greek root related to husbandry or perhaps a misunderstood regional term.</note> Through her work and with wondrous shrewdness, she not only preserved her father’s wealth but increased it so immensely that those who made vows to her believed she surpassed all western kings in her treasures. And so, because of her exceptional beauty as well as her opulence and wisdom, her fame spread even to remote nations.

Indeed, among others, this celebrated rumor reached the <term>Argives</term>—the people of Argos in Greece. Among them was **Perseus**, the most flourishing of the Greek youth; upon hearing these reports, he was seized by a desire both to see this most beautiful woman and to take possession of her treasures. And so, having boarded a ship—which had a horse named **Pegasus** as its figurehead—he was carried to the west with marvelous speed. There, through planning, experience, and force of arms, he overcame the queen; loaded with gold and rich plunder, he returned to his people.

From these events, poetic fiction found its inspiration. In these myths, we read that Medusa the Gorgon was accustomed to turn those she looked upon into stone; that her hair was turned into snakes by the wrath of Minerva because she had defiled Minerva’s temple by lying with Neptune; and that she had given birth to Pegasus, and that Perseus flew into her kingdom seated on a winged horse and made use of the <term>Aegis</term> of Pallas. <note>The Aegis was the mythological shield or breastplate associated with the goddess Athena (Pallas).</note>

The possession of gold is an unhappy state. If it is hidden, it is of no benefit to the owner; if it shines, a thousand traps are born from those who lust for it. Even if the hands of violent men are held back, the anxious cares of the owner do not cease. For peace of mind is put to flight, sleep is stolen away, and fear enters. Trust is diminished, suspicion is increased, and in short, every enjoyment of life is hindered for the wretched man. If indeed, by any chance, that wealth is lost, the man is tormented by anxieties. Having become poor, the greedy man praises [wealth], the generous man laughs, the envious man is consoled, the needy man is relieved, and the whole common crowd turns the suffering of the grieving man into a story.

# On Deianira, the wife of Hercules.

D Eyanira was the daughter of Oeneus, king of the Aetolians <note>The OCR reads "Etholozuz," likely a corruption of "Aetolorum."</note> (as some assert),
and the sister of Meleager, a maiden of such remarkable beauty
that a contest arose between Achelous <note>original: "Atheolum"; the river god who fought Hercules for Deianira's hand.</note> and Hercules to
win her in marriage. When she had fallen to the victor, Hercules, 
she was loved by Nessus the centaur. And when Hercules was 
transporting her from Calydon to his homeland, he met 
the waiting Nessus at the river Evenus in Calydon. Since 
Nessus was a horseman <note>Referring to his nature as a centaur, half-man and half-horse.</note>, he offered his services to Hercules 
to carry Deianira across. When Hercules had granted this, 
intending to swim after his wife, Nessus—as if having achieved 
his desire—once he had crossed the river, took flight 
with his beloved. Since Hercules could not overtake him on foot, he 
struck the fugitive with an arrow tainted with <term>Lernean poison</term> <note>Poison from the blood of the Hydra, a multi-headed monster slain by Hercules.</note>. 
Sensing this and realizing he was dying, Nessus immediately 
gave Deianira a garment stained with his own blood, 
asserting that if she kept it bloody, she could restrain 
Hercules from all outside loves and hold him in her own love. 
Deianira, being credulous and taking it as a most significant gift, 
kept it hidden for some time. When Hercules was in love with Omphale 
or Iole, she cautiously sent it to him via the servant Lichas. 
But once the sweat had dissolved the blood tainted with poison 
and it was absorbed by his skin, he turned to madness and 
proceeded of his own accord to burn himself in fire. And thus 
Deianira, widowed of such a great husband while she 
hoped to win him back, lost him, and even paid the price for Nessus's revenge.

-># Concerning Jocasta, Queen of Thebes.<-

**J**ocasta <note>original: "yocasta"</note>, the queen of Thebes, was more famous for her misfortune than for her merits or her kingdom. Indeed, since she traced her splendid origin from the first founders of Thebes, she married Laius, the king of the Thebans, while she was still a young woman. When she had conceived a son by him, because of a hostile response given to Laius from an oracle, she—distressed—handed the infant over with orders that he be thrown to the wild beasts. Although she believed he had been devoured immediately, he was raised by the king of the Corinthians as a son. When he was grown, after he had killed Laius in the land of Phocis, she—now a widow—took this stranger as her husband. By him, she gave birth to sons, Eteocles and Polynices, and just as many daughters, Ismene and Antigone. And when she seemed happy in both her kingdom and her offspring, she learned by the response of the gods that the man she considered her lawful husband was actually her son. Though she bore this most bitterly, he <note>Oedipus</note> bore it even more so; to the point that while he desired eternal death out of shame for the crime committed, he tore out his eyes. Taking up this discord, the sons went to war, having broken their pacts; and though they often descended into combat with great sadness, she received news that they had killed each other with mutual wounds in a duel. Unable to endure this grief, the wretched mother and grandmother <note>She was both mother and grandmother to her children/grandchildren due to the incestuous nature of her marriage to Oedipus.</note>—seeing her brother Creon now in power, her son and husband a captive to his fate, and her daughters Ismene and Antigone entangled in falling fortune—drove out her soul, weary of evils, with a blade, though she was by then an old woman; and so she ended her anxieties with her life. There are those, however, who claim she could not bear her guilty errors for so long, and that as soon as she saw Oedipus <note>original: "Epidum"</note> tearing out his eyes, she immediately vented her rage upon herself.

->## Concerning Amalthea, or the Sibyl Eryphile.<-

**A**malthea <note>original: "Almathea"</note> the virgin (whom some call Eryphile, daughter of Glaucus) is believed to have drawn her origin from Cumae, an ancient town of Chalcidian Campania. Since she was one of the <term>Sibyls</term> <note>Prophetic women of the ancient world who were said to speak the oracles of the gods.</note>, some believe she flourished at the time of the destruction of Troy and reached such a great age that she lived until the time of Tarquinius the Elder, king of the Romans. She possessed such a character that she never allowed herself to be defiled by the contact of a man. And although the fables of the poets testify that she was loved by <term>Phoebus</term> <note>The god Apollo, patron of prophecy.</note> and by his gift received both long-lasting years and—
