--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues a biographical and historical account of a Sibyl, likely the Cumaean Sibyl, discussing her prophetic powers, her oracle at Lake Avernus, and the legend of the Sibylline Books sold to King Tarquin.</meta>

...attained divinity. I indeed believe that by the merit of her virginity she received the light of prophecy from the true sun (who illuminates every person coming into this world <note>original: "qui illuminat omnem hominem venientem in hunc mundum" — a reference to the Gospel of John 1:9</note>). Through this light, she predicted many things and wrote of things to come. Furthermore, they say that on the Baian shore, near Lake Avernus, there was a famous oracle of hers. This I have indeed seen and heard of; it retains her name even to this day. Although it is worn away by great age and its structure is half-ruined, it still preserves its ancient divine majesty and offers wonder at its magnitude to those who look upon it.

There are some who say she provided guidance to the fugitive Aeneas to the underworld <note>A reference to Virgil’s Aeneid, where the Sibyl leads Aeneas into the land of the dead</note>, but I do not believe this; I will speak of this elsewhere. Those who claim she lived for many centuries assert that she came to Rome and brought nine books to Tarquin the Elder <note>Tarquinius Priscus, the legendary fifth king of Rome</note>. When Tarquin refused to pay the requested price for them, she burned three while he watched. On the following day, she demanded the same price for the remaining six (the same price she had previously asked for nine) and asserted that if it were not given, she would burn three more immediately. On the day after that, she received the price she asked for from Tarquin for the remaining books. After these were preserved, it was discovered by later generations that they contained all the fates of the Romans. For this reason, the Romans thereafter kept them with the greatest care, and as the need arose, they would turn to them as to an oracle to consult about the future.

To me, it is hard to believe that she was the same person as Deiphobe <note>The name given to the Sibyl in Roman mythology</note>. However, we read that she ended her days among the Sicilians, and for a long time her tomb was pointed out there by the inhabitants. Therefore, we are made illustrious through study and divine grace, which are denied to no one who makes themselves worthy. But if we look on while numbed by sloth, we would clearly feel that with time wasted from the womb, even if we die at a great age, we are merely being carried to the grave. Finally, if women, ever watchful through talent and divinity, are so powerful, what must be thought of wretched men? For them, there is a readier aptitude for all things; if laziness were driven away, they would surely rise to divinity itself. Let those, therefore, weep and waste away, from whom so great a gift has been taken by inertia, and let them admit that they are mere stones among living men—which will happen when, speechless, they confess their own crime.

### About Nicostrata, also known as Carmenta, daughter of King Poneus

Nicostrata (who was later named Carmenta by the Italians) was the daughter of Poneus, King of the Arcadians <note>Arcadia was a mountainous region in ancient Greece, often associated with rustic simplicity and myths</note>. According to some accounts, she married Pallas of the Arcadians; according to others, she was his daughter-in-law. She was not only distinguished by the splendor of her royal status, but was also highly learned in Greek literature. Her mind was so versatile that through constant, vigilant study, she reached the power of prophecy <term>vaticination|the act of foretelling the future through divine inspiration</term> and became a very famous seer. Because she would reveal the future to those who asked—and sometimes spontaneously—through song <note>original: "carmine"</note>, the Latins called her **Carmenta**, effectively discarding her original name, Nicostrata. 

She was the mother of Evander, King of the Arcadians. Ancient myths claim he was conceived by Mercury <note>The messenger god of eloquence and wit</note>, either because he was such a gifted and eloquent speaker or because he was particularly clever. As some tell it, Evander accidentally killed his real father; others say he was driven from his kingdom due to a civil uprising. At the urging of his mother Carmenta—who promised him great things through her prophecy if he sought the land she pointed out—she joined him in his exile. They set sail with their people and, with a favorable wind, reached the mouth of the Tiber River from the Peloponnese <note>The southern peninsula of Greece</note>. 

Guided by his mother, they settled on the Palatine Hill (which he named after his father Pallas, or his son Pallas), the very site where the great city of **Rome** was later established. There, they settled with their people and built the town of Pallanteum. 

When Carmenta encountered the local inhabitants, who lived almost like wild men of the forest—even though they had recently learned how to sow crops as a gift from King Latinus <note>A legendary king of the Latins</note>—she saw they had no knowledge of writing, or at most a slight familiarity with Greek script. Foreseeing with her divine mind the future fame of these people and this region, she felt it was unworthy for their future great deeds to be recorded using foreign letters. She applied all her intellectual strength to create a unique alphabet for these people, entirely different from those of other nations. 

God did not fail this undertaking. By His grace, she invented new characters suited to the Italian speech and taught how to join them. She was content to devise only sixteen shapes <note>The original Latin alphabet is traditionally said to have had fewer letters than the modern version, which grew over centuries</note>, just as Cadmus, the founder of Thebes, had long ago invented for the Greeks. We call these "Latin" letters to this day, and we still possess the gift she gave...

<summary>This page recounts the legend of Nicostrata (Carmenta), an Arcadian prophetess who migrated to the future site of Rome and invented the Latin alphabet. It emphasizes her role in transitioning the local people from a "wild" state to a literate civilization.</summary>

<keywords>Nicostrata, Carmenta, Evander, Palatine Hill, Latin alphabet, origins of writing, Roman mythology, prophecy, Pallanteum</keywords>

Wherefore, since they had celebrated her with divine honors while she was living, after her death they built a shrine to her name at the lowest part of the **Capitoline Hill** (where she had spent her life); and for her perpetual memory, they called the adjacent places the *Carmentalia* after her name. Indeed, not even Rome, now grown great, allowed this to become obsolete; on the contrary, for many centuries, they called a gate of the city (which the citizens had built there out of pressing necessity) the **Carmental Gate** after the name of Carmenta. 

Italy was once flourishing with many gifts above the other regions of the world, and gleaming with almost celestial light; nor was such a splendid brightness sought only under its own sky. <meta>This page continues a rhetorical defense of the Latin language and the written word, likely from a Renaissance-era treatise. [A paperclip is attached to the left edge of the page.]

We transmit our own thoughts and faithfully receive those of others. Through these <note>The author is referring to letters or written documents</note>, we join friendships across great distances and preserve them through mutual exchanges. These describe God to us, as far as it is possible; they signify the heavens, the lands, the seas, and all living creatures.

Nor is there anything you might seek that you cannot perceive through these, provided you are vigilant. Whatever cannot be grasped or held by the breadth of the mind, the work of these writings briefly and most faithfully commits to safe-keeping. 

Yet even if some of these benefits reach others, it takes nothing away from what is commendable in our own. Moreover, of such excellent gifts, some we have lost, some we have given away, and some we still hold by name alone rather than in actual effect. 

Truly, whatever has become of the rest—whether by our own fault or by fortune—neither German rapacity, nor Gallic fury <note>original: "gallic⁹ furor"; a common Renaissance rhetorical trope regarding the perceived temperament of the French</note>, nor English cunning, nor Spanish ferocity, nor the unrefined barbarity or insults of any other nation, [can] snatch away this so grand, so spectacular, and so excellent glory of the Latin name... <note>The text cuts off mid-word at "surrip[ere]," meaning to steal or snatch away</note>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Cephalus and Procris, specifically the moment where Aurora (the goddess of Dawn) attempts to sow doubt in Cephalus's mind regarding his wife's fidelity.</meta>

...she tried to draw him into her way of thinking with her prayers. Because of this, she said, "Unworthy man, you will repent, Cephalus, of having loved Procris so fervently; you will find out soon enough—if there is someone to test her—that she has preferred gold to your love." <note>The speaker here is the goddess Aurora, who had kidnapped Cephalus and was frustrated by his devotion to his wife.</note> 

Hearing this, the young man, eager to put it to the test, went away pretending to go on a long journey abroad. Having turned his steps back toward his homeland in disguise, he tested his wife's constancy by offering gifts through an intermediary. No matter how great the things promised, they were unable to move her at the first impulse. 

However, as he finally persevered and increased the offer of jewels, her wavering mind finally bent. To him, the night and the desired embrace were promised, provided the pledged gold was given. Then Cephalus revealed himself, overcome with grief, because he had intercepted Procris's fickle love through this trickery. 

She, covered in blushes and driven by the consciousness of her crime, immediately fled into the woods and gave herself to solitude. However, the young man, impatient in his love, called her back into favor with prayers, even though she initially rejected him, and he offered her his own forgiveness. 

But what does it matter? The power of forgiveness has no strength against the stings of conscience <note>original: "consciencie morsus," a common medieval metaphor for the pain of guilt</note>. Procris was driven by various impulses of the mind. And, divided by jealousy—lest perhaps her husband should do to her through the charms of love exactly what she herself had bartered for gold—she began to follow the hunter <note>Cephalus was a renowned hunter</note> secretly through crags, steep mountain ridges, and the secrets of the valleys. 

While she was doing this, it happened that as Procris was hiding among the grassy valleys and marshy reeds, she moved. Her husband, believing her to be a wild beast, pierced her with a thrown arrow, and she died. 

I do not know what I should say is more powerful: whether nothing on earth is more powerful than gold, or whether it is more foolish to seek out that which you do not wish to lose. While looking into both of these things, this woman earned for herself an indelible stain and found the death she was least seeking. 

But to end this discussion on the immoderate desire for gold (by which nearly all of us fools are drawn), I ask those seized by such stubborn jealousy to say what profit they feel they gain from it, what honor, what praise, or what glory they achieve. Indeed, in my judgment, this is a ridiculous sickness of the mind <term>egritudo mentis</term> <note>a philosophical term for a disordered emotional state</note> taking its origin from the faint-heartedness of the sufferer. For we see this jealousy nowhere else than among those who consider themselves of such lowly virtue that they easily believe anyone else would be preferred over them.

# Concerning Argia, wife of Polynices, and daughter of King Adrastus.

<margin>Argia was a Greek woman who traced her noble lineage back to the ancient kings of the Argives; she was the daughter of King Adrastus.</margin>

And just as she offered a joyful sight to those of her own time through her remarkable beauty, so too she left to those who followed an eternal and most illustrious testimony of her devotion to her husband. Because of this, her name has reached our own days shining with an exceptional brilliance. This woman, then, was married to Polynices <note>original: "Pollicinio"</note>, the son of Oedipus <note>The tragic King of Thebes who unknowingly killed his father and married his mother.</note> and an exile. When she had already borne him a son named Thersander <note>original: "Tessandrum"</note>, she noticed that her husband was being tormented by biting cares due to the treachery of his brother. Seized by anxiety herself, she did not only move her father—now an old man—to pity with her tears and prayers, but she even stirred him to take up arms against Eteocles <note>original: "ethyoclem"</note>, who was occupying the kingdom of Thebes in violation of the legal agreements made with his brother <note>Polynices and Eteocles had agreed to share the throne of Thebes by alternating years, but Eteocles refused to step down when his term ended.</note>.

<summary>This page introduces Argia, daughter of King Adrastus, praising her beauty and her loyalty to her exiled husband, Polynices. It describes how she persuaded her father to go to war against Eteocles to reclaim Polynices' rightful throne.</summary>

<keywords>Argia, Polynices, Adrastus, Thebes, Eteocles, Thersander, marital devotion, Greek mythology</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Argia's devotion to her fallen husband Polynices, then transitions to the life of the prophetess Manto.</meta>

...could not be hidden from her spouse. Neither the filth of his stained face could drive away the wife’s kisses, nor could cries, nor tears, nor the great command of Creon <note>Creon was the King of Thebes who had forbidden the burial of the rebel army.</note> stop her. For when she had often sought the breath of life through kisses on his mouth, had washed his foul limbs with tears, and had often called the lifeless man back into her embraces with her voice, she handed him over to the now-fragrant flames (lest any part of her pious duty be left undone). Once he was consumed, she buried him together with the others. Nor, terrified by the fire, did she fear to undergo the sword and chains of the severe king for her "crime." 

Many women have often wept over their husbands' illnesses, imprisonments, poverty, and many misfortunes; yet they did so while the hope of better fortune remained, or out of fear of a harsher love <note>The author suggests other women act out of social pressure or hope for reward, rather than pure devotion.</note>. While that might seem praiseworthy, it cannot be called the ultimate judgment of affection in the way Argia's devotion can be. She sought out hostile fields when she could have wept in her own homeland; she handled a foul corpse when she could have anointed herself; she bestowed the honor of kings upon the flames when (considering the danger of the time) it would have been enough to bury him secretly. She let out womanly wails where she could have passed through in silence. Nor did she have anything to hope for from a slain exile, yet she had everything to fear from the enemy king. Thus true love, thus wholehearted faith, thus the sanctity of marriage and unsullied chastity were able to persuade her. By this merit, Argia comes to be praised, honored, and extolled with splendid proclamation.

### Concerning Manto, daughter of Tiresias.

**Manto** was the daughter of **Tiresias**, the greatest prophet of the **Thebans**, and was famous in the time of King **Oedipus** and his sons. She was of such ready and capable talent under her father’s guidance that she learned <term>pyromancy</term> <note>original: "piromanciam"; divination by observing flames</note>, a most ancient invention of the **Chaldeans** (or as others wish, of **Nimrod** <note>A biblical figure often associated in the Middle Ages with the invention of magic and the Tower of Babel.</note>), quite excellently. 

And in her age, no one better understood the movements, colors, and murmurs of the flames, in which (by I know not what diabolical aid) the manifestations of the spirits are said to be present. Furthermore, she understood the entrails of cattle and the livers of bulls and the innards of any animals with a sharp intuition. It was very often believed that by her arts she compelled unclean spirits and the ghosts of the underworld to speak and give answers to those who questioned her. Truly, when the Argive kings who had besieged **Thebes** had already fallen in battle, and **Creon** had seized the city...

<meta>This page continues the biography of Penthesilea, Queen of the Amazons, and begins the account of Polyxena. The narrative maintains the celebratory yet tragic tone regarding the heroines of the Trojan War found in Boccaccio’s "Of Famous Women."</meta>

...It is well established that she did not lack a strong character. It is read that the use of the battle-axe <note>original: "securis"</note> was unknown until her era. This woman (as some like to say), having heard of the virtue of Hector of Troy <note>Hector was the greatest hero of Troy and the eldest son of King Priam.</note>, loved him ardently. Out of a desire to bear noble offspring from him for the succession of her kingdom, she was easily persuaded to come to his aid against the Greeks with a very large number of her people. 

The bright fame of the Greek princes did not terrify her; instead, desiring to please Hector with her skill in arms and her courage rather than her beauty, she very often entered into the thick of armed combat. Sometimes she would strike men down with a spear, sometimes open a path through those resisting with a sword, and very often drive retreating troops into flight with her bow. She acted so grandly and with such manly spirit that she sometimes moved Hector himself to look on in admiration. 

Finally, while this warrior-woman <note>original: "virago," a term used here to describe a woman who possesses the strength and spirit traditionally associated with men.</note> was battling against the fiercest enemies one day and showing herself worthy of such a lover beyond her usual manner—many of her own people having already been killed—she received a lethal wound. She fell pitiably in the midst of the Greeks she herself had laid low. Others, however, claim that she reached Troy after Hector was already dead and was killed there in sharp combat, as is written. 

Some might wonder how women, however well-armed, could ever run against the spirits of men, if they did not set aside their wonder when they realize that practice turns into a second nature; for this reason, these women and those like them are made much more capable in arms than many of those whom nature created as males, but whom cowardice and pleasure turn into women or **"helmeted hares."** <note>A derogatory term for men who wear the armor of a soldier but possess the fearful heart of a rabbit.</note>

# Concerning Polyxena, daughter of King Priam

**Polyxena**, a maiden and daughter of King Priam of Troy and Hecuba, was a young girl of such blooming beauty that she was able to ignite the flames of desire in the stern heart of Achilles, son of Peleus. Through the deception of her mother Hecuba, she drew him alone into the temple of Apollo to his death, under the pretext of a brief meeting. Oh, how much less than her due—after the Trojan forces had fallen and Troy was overthrown—she was led by Neoptolemus <note>The son of Achilles, also known as Pyrrhus.</note> to his father's tomb as a sacrificial offering <note>original: "piaculum," a sacrifice intended to appease the spirits or make amends.</note>, by the hand of her father's enemy. There (if any faith can be placed in the writings of the ancients), seeing that the fierce youth had drawn his sword while the others standing around were weeping...

<summary>This page recounts the Amazon Queen Penthesilea's combat prowess and death at Troy, followed by the tragic story of Polyxena, who was used to lure Achilles to his death and was later sacrificed by his son.</summary>
<keywords>Penthesilea, Amazons, Hector, battle-axe, Polyxena, Achilles, Neoptolemus, Trojan War, sacrifice</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the fall of Troy, transitioning from the sacrificial death of Polyxena to the biography of her mother, Queen Hecuba. The text appears to be from a version of Giovanni Boccaccio’s "Of Famous Women" (De Mulieribus Claris).</meta>

The innocent girl offered her throat with such a steady heart and fearless face that admiration for her bravery moves the soul no less than pity for her parent's grief. It is indeed a great and memorable thing that her tender age, her female sex, her royal luxury, and her changed fortune were unable to crush the maiden's great spirit—especially when facing the sword of a victorious enemy. Beneath such a blade, the courageous hearts of even distinguished men are sometimes changed and often fail. I would easily believe this was the work of a noble nature, demonstrating by this brief display of character what kind of woman she would have become, had her fortune not been so quickly snatched away by the enemy.

## On Hecuba, Queen of the Trojans

Hecuba was the most famous queen of the Trojans, serving as both an extraordinary flash of fleeting splendor and the most certain evidence of human misery. According to some, she was the daughter of Dymas <note>original: "Dyamantitis"</note> and Thoas. Others, however, claim she was the daughter of Cisseus <note>original: "Cipsei"</note>, the King of Thrace; I myself lean toward this view, as it is the opinion held by many. While still a maiden, she married Priam, the illustrious King of the Trojans, and by him she conceived a great number <note>The transcription "mirtim" likely refers to "multitudinem" or a "multitude."</note> of children of both sexes. She gave birth to sons...

## Concerning Cassandra, daughter of Priam, King of the Trojans

<margin>C</margin>assandra was the daughter of Priam, King of the Trojans. To her indeed (as the beauty of the old stories claims) belonged a prophetic mind. Whether this was acquired through her own studies, or given as a gift from a god, or rather through demonic <margin>deception, is not</margin> entirely certain. However, many people affirm that long before the abduction of Helen, she often and with a clear voice foretold the audacity of Paris, the arrival of the daughter of Tyndareus <note>referring to Helen of Troy</note>, the long siege of the city, and the final desolation of Priam and Ilium <note>Troy</note>. Because of this, even though she offered no credit to her words... <note>The text ends mid-sentence; it likely continues to explain that no one believed her prophecies.</note>

<meta>This page begins a biography of Clytemnestra, likely from a version of Giovanni Boccaccio's "Concerning Famous Women" (De Mulieribus Claris).</meta>

->### On Clytemnestra, Queen of Mycenae.<-

**Clytemnestra** was the daughter of **Tyndareus**, the King of Oebalia <note>original: "Oebalie"; an ancient name for Sparta</note>, born of **Leda**. She was the sister of **Castor**, **Pollux**, and **Helen**. While still a young maiden, she married **Agamemnon**, the King of Mycenae. Although she was already noble enough by birth and by her marriage, she became even more famous for her wicked daring. 

For while her husband Agamemnon was commanding the Greek forces at **Troy**, and after she had already borne many children by him, she fell into lust for an idle and lazy young man named **Aegisthus**. He was the son of **Thyestes** and **Pelopia** <note>In Greek mythology, Aegisthus was the product of an incestuous union intended to produce an avenger for the house of Thyestes</note> and, because he held a priesthood, he had abstained from going to war. As some authors believe, she was drawn into his embrace and his bed through the persuasions of the old man **Nauplius**, the father of the late **Palamedes** <note>Nauplius sought revenge against the Greek leaders because they had unjustly executed his son, Palamedes, during the Trojan War</note>.

From this crime, it followed that—whether out of fear of Agamemnon’s return after her misdeed, or by the persuasion of her lover and a desire for the kingdom, or perhaps out of a web of resentment regarding **Cassandra** (whom Agamemnon was bringing back to Mycenae)—this spirited woman, with a hardened mind and through deceptions, rose up against her husband with reckless daring. When he returned as the victor of Ilium <note>Troy</note>, weary from the storms of the sea, she received him into the palace with feigned joy upon her face. 

As some tell the story, she ordered him to be struck down by her lover, who emerged from an ambush while the King was dining and perhaps already soaked with wine. Others, however, say that when he was lying down, having put on his "garments of victory" as if for a Greek festival, his calm but unfaithful wife persuaded him to instead put on the clothes of his ancestors, which she had prepared beforehand for this very purpose. These garments—which <term>lacked an opening for the head</term>—the bold woman offered to him. When the man had already thrust his arms into the sleeves and, being wrapped in the cloth, was searching for where he could poke out his head, he was trapped and helpless. In this semi-bound state, he was handed over to the assassin by the queen, who urged the lover on. Thus, while the King could see no one, he was struck down.

Once this was done, she seized the entire kingdom and ruled for seven years with her lover Aegisthus. However, in the meantime, **Orestes** (Agamemnon’s son by her) had grown up. Friends had secretly saved him from his mother’s rage, and when he reached an age to avenge his father’s death, he seized the opportunity and killed her along with her lover. 

I know not what to marvel at more: the crime or the audacity. First, that a man so renowned had not feared such a heavy evil; second, by how much... <note>The text ends mid-sentence: "Secundu quatomm?" likely intended as "Secundum quanto magis," meaning "secondly, by how much more..."</note>

<summary>This page recounts the betrayal and murder of Agamemnon by his wife Clytemnestra and her lover Aegisthus, detailing the different versions of the King's assassination and his eventual vengeance at the hands of his son, Orestes.</summary>
<keywords>Clytemnestra, Agamemnon, Aegisthus, Mycenae, Trojan War, Orestes, regicide, Greek mythology, betrayal</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative regarding the aftermath of Agamemnon's murder and the revenge of his son Orestes, transitioning into the biography of Helen of Troy.</meta>

It was fitting for such a treacherous woman; I find her all the more abominable. I have, however, something to praise: the honest virtue of a son <note>Orestes, the son of Agamemnon and Clytemnestra</note> who, having endured for a long time, did not allow himself to be held back by devotion to an unchaste mother. Instead, as a bold avenger, he rushed upon those responsible for his father's undeserved death. The son acted against his ill-deserving mother just as his undeserving father had suffered at the hands of the adulterous priest <note>original: "adultero sacerdote" – referring to Aegisthus, Clytemnestra’s lover, who helped murder Agamemnon</note> under the command of that unchaste woman. He turned the crime back upon its authors so that, by the shedding of their blood, he might atone for the father's blood which had been poured out by their command and help.

# Concerning Helen, wife of King Menelaus

**Helen** was a woman most famous throughout the whole world, as much for her observed wantonness—as it seemed to many—as for the long war <note>The Trojan War</note> that followed because of her. She was the daughter of Tyndareus, King of Oebalia <note>An ancient name for the region of Laconia or Sparta</note>, and Leda, a most beautiful woman, and she was the wife of Menelaus, King of the Spartans. Her beauty was so celebrated (as all the ancient Greeks say, and the Latins after them) that she is easily placed above all others. For (to set aside the rest) she exhausted the man of divine genius, **Homer**, before he could adequately describe her in verse according to the rules of poetry. 

Moreover, numerous excellent painters and sculptors all undertook the same labor, so that they might leave to posterity at least an image of such extraordinary grace. Among these, **Zeuxis of Heraclea**—the most famous painter of that age and superior to all others—was hired by the people of Croton for a vast sum of money. He applied his whole genius and the full strength of his art to depicting her with his brush <term>pinniculo: a small brush or feather used by ancient painters</term>. And since he had no other model besides Homer's poem and her great fame everywhere, he could only conceive of her face and general stature in his mind through those two sources. He devised a plan whereby he might first grasp that divine image of Helen through other beautiful forms, show the design to those who requested it, and leave it to posterity once revealed. 

First, he selected the most beautiful boys, and then their sisters, and from them chose five maidens who were particularly remarkable for their grace. Having gathered together a single form from the beauty of them all, and having exerted all the powers of his celebrated genius, it is scarcely believed that he was able to achieve what he desired through art with sufficient fullness. Nor do I wonder that...

For neither painting nor statue, by means of the brush or color, could ever truly depict:
the joy within the eyes, the peaceful charm of the whole mouth, the heavenly
smile, and the various and graceful expressions of the face that shift according to the quality of words
and actions—since this is the duty of nature alone. He did, therefore,
what he was able to do; and what he had painted, he left to posterity as the heavenly beauty of an image.
From this, more clever wits <note>original: "acutiores"; referring to insightful poets or philosophers who interpret deeper meanings</note> devised a fable. On account of the starry
glow of her eyes, on account of a light unseen by mortals, and on account of the
remarkable whiteness of her face and the abundance of her flowing golden hair, which
fell here and there over her shoulders with playful tinklings, and the witty
and sonorous sweetness of her voice, as well as certain gestures of both her cinnamon
and rosy mouth as of her splendid brow and ivory throat, and from
the snowy, heavenly charms of her rising breast... they derived her name from the breathy sounds <note>original: "ex aspiratis"; likely a reference to the grammatical 'aspiration' or the letter 'H' in a name like Helen</note> conceived

The author may be using a specific allegorical tradition or this may be a rare variant.</note>, and described her as his daughter. They did this so that
they might understand that beyond the beauty she could have inherited from her mother, there was a
divine power infused within her, which the brush <note>The text cuts off here, likely continuing with "the brush could not capture."</note>

Taking a great part of the treasures of Menelaus by night from the Laconian shore—or, as others prefer, from the nearby island of Cythera—he snatched the watchful Helen from a certain temple during the performance of a sacred ancestral rite and placed her upon his prepared fleet. After many dangers, he arrived with her at Troy, where she was received with exceptional honor by Priam. <meta>This page continues the discussion of the mythological sorceress Circe and begins the biography of the warrior-queen Camilla.</meta>

...not valuing it much, provided she achieved what she desired, [claiming] she had kept her chastity unblemished by scandal <note>The author is suggesting Circe was more concerned with her public reputation for purity than her actual behavior</note>. And thus, she drew many of those who approached her into her enticements through flatteries and ornate speech. Many others she inflated with pride because of her singular affection for them. And so, those from whom human reason seemed to be stripped by the works of this ill-fated woman were rightly believed to have been turned by her into the beasts of their own crimes. From this, we can well enough understand, by observing the morals of men and women, that there are many "Circes" everywhere, and far more men turned into beasts by their own lust and crime. And because he led his life according to the actions of the woodpecker, he was said to have been turned into a woodpecker. When, or by what kind of death, or where this Circe died, I do not have a record.

->**Concerning Camilla, Queen of the Volscians**<-

Camilla <note>The initial letter "C" is missing in the manuscript, as it was intended for a large, decorative hand-painted initial</note> was a distinguished maiden and most worthy of remembrance. Born to the most ancient King of the Volscians and his wife Casmilla, her birth was the cause of her mother’s death. For when the mother died after giving birth to the little girl, she was named Camilla by her father Metabus—taking her name from her mother's with only one letter removed—for his own consolation. The fortune of this maiden was harsh from the day of her birth. For a little while after the mother's funeral, Metabus was driven from his kingdom by a sudden sedition <note>A violent uprising or rebellion</note> of his citizens. Taking flight, he could carry away nothing into exile but this little girl, his daughter, beloved by him above all other things...

Alone and on foot, the wretched man fled, carrying his companion <note>original: "sociam" — referring to his daughter, Camilla.</note> Camilla in his arms. He arrived at the River Amasenus <note>A river in the Lazio region of central Italy.</note>, which was swollen by the previous day’s rain. Because he was hindered by the burden of the infant, he was unable to swim across. However, he arrived at a timely plan through the grace of God <note>original: "porrigente deo" — while the story is pagan, the medieval scribe uses the singular "God" or "a god" to suggest divine providence.</note>, who did not wish for this maiden—destined for such future fame—to be destroyed by so lowly a fate.

    Therefore, he wrapped her in the bark of a cork tree and bound her to a spear <note>original: "iaculo"</note> that he happened to be carrying. He then dedicated her to Diana <note>The Roman goddess of the hunt and wild places.</note>, vowing that she would serve the goddess if she were kept safe. With his arm vibrating with all his strength, he hurled the spear, along with his daughter, across to the opposite bank, and he immediately followed her by swimming. 
    
    When he found her unharmed by the gift of God, he was joyful even in his misery and sought out the secret hiding places of the woods. There, with great effort, he raised the little girl on the milk of wild beasts. When she had reached a stronger age, she began to cover her body with the skins of wild animals and to brandish weapons with her arms. She learned to swing slings <note>The OCR reads "fidas," likely a transcription of "fundas" (slings).</note>, to bend bows, and to carry quivers. In the chase, she pursued stags and wild goats, and she came to look down upon all feminine labors. Above all else, she preserved her virginity inviolate.

...what is fitting for them in their father's house, in the temples, and in the theaters (places where a multitude of spectators and the strictest judges of morality <note>Latin: *morū censores*. It is indeed necessary for them to close their ears to things that are not honorable, to restrain their speech with silence, to steady their eyes with a serious gaze, and to control all their gestures with the weight of honor. They should avoid idleness, rowdy banquets, excessive luxuries, dances, and the company of young men. Let them understand that neither desiring whatever they wish nor doing whatever is merely permitted is holy or consistent with chastity. In this way, having become more prudent and flourishing with a praiseworthy dignity, they may arrive at the state of sacred marriage in good time, following the standards of their character.

### Concerning Penelope, the Wife of Ulysses

The strength of her virtue was fiercely tested by fortune (though in vain). ...freed from the suitors. When at last she was able to recognize her husband, she was filled with the greatest joy and received the man she had long desired. However, a certain Lycophron <note>Lycophron was a Greek poet known for his obscure and often dark versions of myths; his work "Alexandra" contains many alternative traditions.</note>, one of the more recent Greek poets, claims that because of the persuasions of the aged Nauplius <note>Nauplius sought revenge against the Greek leaders because they had unjustly executed his son, Palamedes, during the Trojan War. Far be it from me to believe such a thing about a woman so celebrated for her chastity in the writings of many authors. These authors assert the contrary: that Penelope remained exceptionally chaste. Indeed, her virtue is all the more brilliant and commendable because it is found so rarely, and because she persevered with a more unshaken constancy while being driven by a greater struggle.

## About Lavinia, Queen of the Laurentians.

Lavinia, Queen of the Laurentians <note>The Laurentians were an ancient people of Latium, the region in Italy where Rome was later founded.</note>, tracing her lineage back to Saturn of Crete <note>In this tradition, the Roman gods were often viewed as ancient kings who were later deified; Saturn was said to have fled to Italy after being deposed in Crete.</note>, was the only daughter of King Latinus and his beloved wife. She eventually became the wife of Aeneas, the most vigorous leader of the Trojans, and she is famous more for being the cause of the war between Aeneas and Turnus of the Rutulians than for other <note>original: "al[ia]"—the text cuts off here at the end of the page.</note>...

She preserved it until she could resign it to her son when he reached puberty, with nothing having been diminished. However, some claim that she was recalled from the woods and married a certain **Melampus**, and that Silvius was raised by Ascanius with brotherly benevolence.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Elissa (more commonly known as Dido), the legendary founder of Carthage. Having discovered that her brother Pygmalion murdered her husband for his wealth, she begins her plan to escape Tyre.</meta>

Elissa <note>original: "Elpissa"; a variant of Elissa, the Phoenician name for Dido</note> bore this so impatiently that she could scarcely refrain from death. Indeed, after she had spent much time in tears—having often called in vain for her most beloved husband and cast out all manner of bitter and dire curses upon her brother—she deliberated on taking flight. This was prompted either by a warning in her dreams (as some believe) or by her own counsel, lest she herself should perhaps be drawn to her death by her brother’s greed. Later, having set aside feminine softness and strengthened her spirit with a masculine resolve—for which she afterwards earned the name **Dido** in the Phoenician language, meaning "heroine" or "man-like woman" in Latin <note>original: "virago"; used here to denote a woman of great strength and courage</note>—she first brought over to her side several of the city's leading men, whom she knew hated Pygmalion for various reasons. 

Having seized her brother’s fleet—whether it had been intended for transport or prepared for some other purpose—she ordered it to be quickly filled with companions at the shipyards. At night, she secretly had all the treasures she knew were her husband's, and those she could take from her brother, placed upon the ships. Then, with a clever trick, she loaded many bundles filled only with sand; under the pretense that these were the actual treasures, she did this in the sight of everyone. 

Once they were on the high sea, while the ignorant sailors watched in wonder, she ordered the sand-filled bundles to be thrown into the water. With many tears, she declared that the death she had long desired had now come to her through the bitter loss of the treasures. She pretended to pity her companions, saying she did not doubt that if they returned to Pygmalion, they would be butchered along with her by that most greedy and fierce king through dire tortures. However, she asserted that if they were willing to take flight with her, she would not be lacking in providing for them and their needs. 

Hearing this, the wretched sailors—though they left their native soil and ancestral homes with heavy hearts—were nonetheless terrified by the fear of a cruel death and easily agreed to exile. With the prows turned and Dido as their leader, they came to **Cyprus**. There, she seized young women on the shore who were performing offerings to Venus according to their custom, taking them along to provide comfort for the young men and to ensure the procreation of their people. She also took as a companion for their pilgrimage a priest of Jupiter with his whole family; he had been forewarned and prophesied that great things would follow from this flight. 

Now, with Crete behind them and Sicily left on the right, she turned the course toward Africa. Grazing the coast of the <term>Massylians</term> <note>A Berber tribe of eastern Numidia in North Africa</note>, she entered the bay. It is well known where she finally decided to give a little rest to those weary from rowing, having judged it a safe harbor for the ships. There, as the neighbors arrived with a desire to see the foreigners...

<meta>This page continues a moralizing discourse on Dido, the legendary founder of Carthage. The author is using Dido as a "virtuous pagan" example to shame Christian women who do not maintain the same level of chastity in their widowhood.</meta>

...if she had commanded her own unbridled desires, all the aforementioned things would have been frustrated by a single denial. Dido was able to die rather than live unchastely. This woman <note>Referring to a typical Christian widow of the author's time</note>, so that she might live [seemingly] chastely, could not refuse marriage. One more clever than the rest will appear at her judgment and say: "I was young. Youth burns (as you know); I could not contain myself," quoting the <term>Teacher of the Gentiles</term> <note>original: "doctoris gencium", referring to Saint Paul</note> saying: "It is better to marry than to burn" <note>A reference to 1 Corinthians 7:9</note>, following his counsel.

Oh, how well said! As if I were to command chastity to little old ladies! Or as if Dido were not a young girl when she confirmed her chastity in her heart. Oh, wicked deed! That counsel <note>St. Paul's advice on marriage</note> is not given so holily by Paul without it being very often shamefully bound to the defense of a crime. We can gradually restore exhausted strength with food; we cannot <note>The author suggests that physical abstinence alone is insufficient to suppress the "superfluous" energy of lust if the will is not also firm.</note> diminish superfluous strength through abstinence. A pagan woman was able to command her fervor for the sake of empty glory and impose laws; but can a Christian woman, so that she may attain eternal glory, not command herself?

Woe is me! While we think to deceive God with such excuses, we withdraw ourselves from eternal life for the sake of fleeting honor, and we drive ourselves into the abyss of eternal damnation. Let them blush, therefore, looking upon the lifeless corpse of Dido; and while they inquire into the cause of her death, let them cast down their faces, grieving that the <term>worshippers of Christ</term> <note>original: "cristicole"</note> are surpassed in chastity by a <term>member of the devil</term> <note>original: "membro dyaboli", a medieval term for a pagan.</note>.

Let them not think that by wiping away tears and putting on mourning clothes for the deceased they have fulfilled everything. Love must be preserved until the end if they wish to fulfill the duty of widowhood. Nor should they think to move on to further vows (which many often do) rather to satisfy their "itching" <note>original: "prurigini", referring to carnal desire</note> under the false name of marriage than to obey sacred wedlock and remain free from the stain of unchastity. For what else is it to seek the embraces of so many men and so many [admirers] than to enter brothels and vaults like <term>Valeria Messalina</term> <note>The wife of the Roman Emperor Claudius, notorious in historical literature for her promiscuity.</note>?

But of this, more elsewhere. I confess, however, that I have exceeded the limits of the work I began. But who is so much in control of himself that he is not sometimes carried away by impulse beyond his purpose? Let those who read this forgive me, I pray, and let us return to where we diverted. Therefore, with public tears and sorrow, the citizens bore out the bloodless Dido, celebrating a magnificent funeral not only with human but also with divine honors.

**On Pamphile, daughter of Platea.**

I find that Pamphile was once a Greek woman. Although antiquity has erased the knowledge of which country she came from, it has kindly left us the name of her father <note>original: "patris"</note>; for it is found that she was the daughter of a certain Platea. Even if she cannot be decorated with the most magnificent titles, because she added something of value to the common good, she ought not be cheated of her portion of praise by silence. For nothing new—however easy it may seem after it has been done—is discovered by anyone without it being a proof of a clever mind, and depending on the quality of the discovery, it must sometimes be counted as a matter of glory. 

Therefore, authors (to whom a certain amount of credit is given) claim that she was the first to collect the fluttering **silkworm** <term>silkworm (bombyx): the larva of the silk moth</term> cocoons from small trees and to have cleaned them of impurities with a comb. Once cleaned, she placed them on a **distaff** <term>distaff (colus): a tool used in spinning to hold the unspun fibers</term> and also taught how to draw a thread from them and then to weave from that thread. In this way, she established the use of silk as it has been known ever since. The logic she devised for this process easily demonstrates how much Pamphile must have excelled in her other affairs.

Rhea Ilia <note>Also known as Rhea Silvia, the mythical mother of the twins Romulus and Remus</note> once shone among mortals with the particular brilliance of her noble blood. For through her sons...

...shameful exclusions and flights; and finally, dishonor <note>original: *dehonestas*. This refers to the loss of social standing or personal integrity when a person is forced into a life they did not choose.</note> seeks to nourish those whom an avaricious man might have joined in honorable marriage. 

Let the mad ones feel, therefore—if they do not wish to measure the strength of others by their own—that virgins are not to be dedicated to God out of ignorance, nor as small children, nor by coercion. Rather, they should first be holily nourished from infancy in their father's house, imbued with integrity and proven character. They should be advanced in age, knowing what they are doing and knowing their own minds, undergoing the yoke of perpetual <term>virginity: a lifelong vow of celibacy and religious devotion</term> of their own accord and not by coercion. I judge that such women are found very rarely; but it is far better for there to be a small number of such women than to profane the sanctuary of God with an enticing multitude <note>original: *multitudine illecebri*. The author argues that a large number of nuns who are there against their will brings worldly temptation and "allurements" into the sacred space.</note>.

# Sappho of Lesbos

**S**appho <note>The famous Greek lyric poet from the island of Lesbos, known for her emotional poetry and mastery of the lyre.</note> was a young woman from the island of Lesbos. <margin>Sappho, the poetess</margin> Through her dedication to study, she climbed the heights of **Parnassus** <note>original: "pnasī" for "Parnasi." This mountain in Greece was the legendary home of the Muses and the god Apollo, symbolizing the highest achievement in poetry and the arts.</note> [...] having struck <note>original: "pulsa"</note> the lyre's strings [...] she was welcomed among the sacred <note>original: "sacris"</note> songs <insert>and verses</insert> of the Muses. 

<note>The scribe uses various abbreviations common in the period: the 'rum' ending (ꝝ), the Tironian 'and' (⁊), and the 'us' abbreviation (⁹).</note>

<meta>This page concludes the biography of the Greek poet Sappho, specifically discussing the legendary origins of the Sapphic meter as an expression of her grief, before beginning the famous account of the Roman matron Lucretia.</meta>

...might be suitable. Groaning at his obstinate hardness <note>This refers to Phaon, the ferryman with whom Sappho was legendarily and unrequitedly in love</note>, they say she sang mournful verses. I would have thought these were elegies <note>traditional poems of lamentation</note>, especially since I have read that such a style is often used for this purpose. However, it was as if she despised the forms of poems past and invented a new kind that moved with "feet" <note>the rhythmic units of poetry</note> different from all others; this style is still called the <term>Sapphic meter</term> after her name. But why should the Muses <note>original: "Pierides," a title for the nine Muses</note> be blamed? They were able to move the Ogygian stones <note>The stones of the ancient city of Thebes, which were said to have leaped into place to the music of Amphion</note> when Amphion touched his lyre, yet they were unwilling to soften the young man's heart while Sappho sang.

# Concerning Lucretia, the Wife of Collatinus

Lucretia, an outstanding leader of Roman chastity and a most sacred ornament of her ancient lineage, was the daughter of Spurius Lucretius Tricipitinus—a man of great distinction among the Romans—and the wife of Tarquinius of Collatia <note>known as Collatinus</note>. Among the matrons of Rome, it remains uncertain whether she was considered more beautiful for the loveliness of her face or for the integrity of her character. Because of her loyalty...

--- Page 71 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative following the contest of the wives' virtues, where Lucretia was found to be the most virtuous. The previous page likely described the arrival of the noblemen at Collatinus's house in Collatia.</meta>

...was seen. Collatinus, however, kindly received the other young men into his home, where they were treated with honor. Sextus, the son of [Tarquin] the Proud <note>Tarquinius Superbus, the legendary seventh and final King of Rome</note>, cast unchaste eyes upon the virtue <note>original: "inhonestatem"—likely a scribal error or OCR misreading for "honestatem" (virtue/honor), as it contrasts with "formositatem" (beauty)</note> and beauty of this chaste woman. Inflamed by a wicked fire, he resolved in his silent thoughts to overpower her loveliness by force, if it were not otherwise surrendered. 

Not many days later, driven by his madness, he secretly left the camp and came at night to the house of Collatinus. There, because he was a kinsman, he was courteously received and honored by Lucretia. After he perceived the whole house was silent and judged everyone to be asleep, he drew his sword and entered Lucretia's bedroom. He revealed who he was and threatened her with death if she made a sound or if she did not submit to his will. When he saw that she resisted his desire and was unafraid of death, he turned to a damnable trickery, saying that he would kill her alongside a servant and tell everyone he had caught them in the act of adultery. 

Hearing this, the trembling woman stopped; terrified by such foul infamy and fearing that if she were killed in that manner, there would be no one to defend her innocence, she yielded her body to the adulterer with a loathing mind. When he had satisfied his seductive pleasure and departed, a victor in his own judgment, Lucretia—sickened by such a wicked <note>original: "celesti"—likely a scribal error for "scelesti" (wicked/criminal)</note> crime—ordered her father Tricipitinus and Brutus, a kinsman of Collatinus who had been held in high regard until that day, and other relatives to be summoned immediately. 

When they arrived, she related with tears and in order what Sextus had done to her in the dead of night. And while her relatives were miserably consoling her as she wept, she drew out a dagger she had hidden under her garment and said: "Though I absolve myself of the sin, I do not free myself from the punishment; nor shall any unchaste woman live hereafter by the example of Lucretia." 

Having said this, she plunged the blade into her innocent breast and, falling upon the wound, she collapsed dying in the sight of her husband and father; it was not long before she poured out her life with her blood. Her beauty was indeed unhappy, yet her chastity—which can never be sufficiently praised—must be extolled with worthy proclamations, all the more so because the disgrace forced upon her was expiated with such sharpness. From this act, not only was the honor restored that the incestuous youth had weakened by the foulness of his crime, but Roman liberty followed <note>Lucretia’s suicide sparked the revolution that overthrew the monarchy and established the Roman Republic</note>.

# Concerning Tomyris, Queen of the Scythians

Tomyris <note>original: "Thamiris"; also known as Tomyris, a massagetean queen of the 6th century BCE</note> was an illustrious queen of the Scythians. Although the Scythians live on barren soil and in a freezing region of the sky, bordered by the Riphean and Hyperborean mountains <note>mythical mountains believed by ancient Greeks and Romans to exist in the far north of Eurasia</note> and were for that reason known almost only to themselves. It is unknown from which parents Tomyris was born, or to whom she was joined in marriage. And for this reason, she is notable and conspicuous for her noble distinction chiefly because she ruled over wild and untamable peoples.

At the time when Cyrus <note>Cyrus the Great, founder of the Achaemenid Empire</note> already held the kingdoms of Asia, he perhaps desired to become more famously known, and so conceived a longing for the Scythian kingdom, more to exalt his own glory than to increase his empire. For he had heard that the Scythians were a poor and forest-dwelling people, yet remained undefeated by the greatest kings until his own time; drawn by this greed, he led his army against Queen Tomyris. 

When Tomyris learned of his arrival—even though he was formidable to all Asia and almost the whole world due to the scale of his deeds—she did not seek hiding places like a terrified woman, nor did she demand terms of peace through a herald <term>caduceator</term> <note>original: "caduceatore"; a peace-messenger or diplomat carrying a staff to signal neutrality</note>. On the contrary, having gathered her forces and become a leader in war, although she could have resisted him with naval works, she allowed him to cross the Araxes <note>a major river in Central Asia, often associated with the Syr Darya or Volga in ancient texts</note> with his entire army and enter her borders; the wise woman judged that Cyrus's madness could be far better defeated within her own boundaries than outside them. 

And I believe this happened: once he had penetrated the interior of the kingdom, she granted a third of her forces to her only young son and ordered him to go forth to meet Cyrus in battle. Cyrus, however, having considered both the nature of the terrain and the customs of the people he had heard of, and sensing the youth coming with an army, decided to conquer him more by trickery than by arms. He feigned a flight, leaving the camp filled with wine—which the Scythians did not yet know <note>The Scythians typically drank fermented mare's milk; undiluted wine was a potent trap for them</note>—along with feasts and other delicacies. 

When the youth entered the empty camp as if he were the victor and the enemy had been put to flight, he and the Scythians, joyful and as if invited not to war but to a banquet, began to gorge themselves on the abundant food and unknown drinks. As a result, military discipline was abandoned, and sleep followed. While they were buried in sleep, Cyrus arrived and put the youth and the rest of his men to death, and as if certain...

<summary>This page recounts the beginning of the conflict between Queen Tomyris of Scythia and Cyrus the Great, detailing how Cyrus used a deceptive feast of wine to ambush Tomyris's son.</summary>
<keywords>Tomyris, Scythia, Cyrus the Great, Araxes River, military strategy, ancient history, Boccaccio, Famous Women</keywords>

--- Page 73 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the war between Cyrus the Great and Tomyris, Queen of the Massagetae, following Cyrus's initial victory through trickery.</meta>

Cyrus proceeded further in his victory. However, when Tomyris heard of the slaughter of her people—and though, as a widow, she was deeply moved by the death of her only son—she did not give herself over to tears in the usual manner of women. Instead, with those tears stilled by anger and a desire for revenge, she turned to the remainder of her forces. Using the same kind of trickery by which she had heard her son was captured—believing that even if the camps were not left filled with wine cups, the enemy (as clever as he was) could still be caught—she used her knowledge of the terrain to feign a retreat. She led her greedy pursuer among barren and frost-chilled mountains, drawing him along a path until she had him where she wanted. There, in a helpless position amidst the ruggedness and mountain passes, she turned on him and almost entirely destroyed Cyrus and his army. Nor did Cyrus himself escape; the widow sated her anger with his bloody death.

Tomyris, her mind made wild with rage, ordered Cyrus’s body to be sought out among the corpses. When it was found, she commanded that his head be cut off and cast into a wineskin full of the blood of his own people. As if she had provided a worthy tomb for the proud king, she said: "Satiate yourself with the blood for which you thirsted." <note>original: "saciare sanguine quem sitti." According to legend, Cyrus had famously declared his "thirst" for conquest and blood.</note> But why say more? We have no other record of Tomyris’s deeds besides this one, which is all the more famous because Cyrus’s empire had been so great.

## Concerning Leena the Courtesan

I believe Leena <note>Also known as Leaena, a famous Athenian courtesan associated with the tyrannicides Harmodius and Aristogeiton.</note> was a Greek woman. Although she was less than chaste, I would still like to describe her among famous women—with the kind permission of honorable matrons and illustrious queens. For (as was said previously) I promised to include women who became famous for *any* deed, not only those who were chaste. Furthermore, we are so indebted to virtue <term>virtue: in this context, the Latin 'virtus' implies both moral goodness and strength of character or courage</term> that we ought not only to elevate those we see established in a noble position, but we must also strive to bring into the light of merit a woman who is buried under a shameful reputation. 

For virtue is precious everywhere; it is no more defiled by the contagion of crimes than a sunbeam is stained by being mixed with filth. <note>The author uses a striking metaphor here: just as light remains pure even when it touches a dirty surface, a person's good qualities are not negated by their low social status.</note> If, therefore, we sometimes see virtue seated in a heart devoted to a detestable trade, we ought to so detest the trade that the praise of her virtue is not diminished. Indeed, virtue is all the more wonderful and worthy in such a person, as it was thought to be further removed from them. Because...

<meta>This page continues the discussion of virtuous women from history, transitioning from perhaps more "noble" figures to those of lower social status who nonetheless exhibited extraordinary character.</meta>

[A metal clip is attached to the left edge of the page.]

For this reason, the memory of prostitutes is not always to be despised; rather, whenever they have made themselves worthy of remembrance through some merit of virtue, they should be extolled with wider praise for readers. In such women, a discovered virtue acts in a way that strikes a blush of shame into decadent kingdoms, since the idleness of those kingdoms serves as an excuse for their own slippery luxuries. Furthermore, so it may appear that great spirits are not always connected solely to illustrious titles and that virtue disdains no one who is willing to seek her, the woman Leena <note>Greek: Leaena (Λέαινα), meaning "Lioness." She was a famous Athenian courtesan associated with the group that overthrew the tyrants.</note> must be joined to such a celebrated assembly of women. Thus, even in that part where she acted with such vigor, she may be praised as one who has served well. This Leena, therefore, given over to shameful prostitution, performed such detestable service that her origins and her homeland are unknown. 

However, during the time when Amyntas <note>The text mentions Amyntas of Macedon, though historical accounts usually place these events in Athens under the tyrant Hippias.</note> was reigning among the Macedonians, Harmodius and Aristogiton <note>Original: "Armormis & Ariston." These are the famous "Tyrannicides" of Athens.</note>—noble young men driven either by the need to erase the shame of a homeland subjected to a foul tyranny or by some other cause—had killed the cruel tyrant Hipparchus. Among others, Leena was captured as one conscious of their deeds, because she enjoyed their intimate companionship. 

And when she was being driven by dire tortures to betray her comrades-in-arms and fellow conspirators, this "slippery" woman <note>A play on her status as a courtesan, contrasted with her solid resolve.</note> reflected within herself on how holy and venerable the name of friendship was. Wishing, through pious consideration, that she might not bring any violence upon that friendship just to spare herself, she first commanded her own mind with wonderful constancy for a long time, so that she would not say what the torturers sought. 

Finally, as the tortures grew stronger and her bodily strength failed, this woman of manly spirit <note>Original: "virilis femina." A common trope in Latin literature where a woman's courage is described as "masculine" to emphasize its strength.</note> feared that if her physical virtue were weakened, her resolve might also be unnerved. She rose to a greater strength: she acted so that her power of speech was taken away as surely as her physical strength. With a sharp bite, she cut off her own tongue and spat it out. By this single but most illustrious act, she snatched away from her torturers all hope of learning what they required of her. 

Who would say that Leena inhabited the brothels except by the crime of fortune? Surely he did not know her, who said that women only keep silent about what they do not know. <note>This is a jab at a common ancient proverb suggesting women are only silent when they are ignorant.</note> Alas for me! Sometimes the wanton opulence of a home and the excessive indulgence of parents has led young women into slippery paths. Their frisky ease, unless restrained by austere bridles and held back by the most vigilant observance of mortals, sometimes slips even without being pushed—and once they have fallen, the honor of their former honesty is trampled by despair.

## Concerning Athalia, Queen of Jerusalem

Athaliah’s savage mind made her such a stepmother to the Syrians and Egyptians that she was seen as an opportunity for the Davidic line <note>The "Davidic line" refers to the royal house of King David, from which the Kings of Judah descended. Athaliah sought to destroy this line to secure her own power.</note>; let it be said that her house, foul on all sides with the blood of her kin and with manifold slaughter, added no less ill-omened fame to her name than the shining crowns of kings. She was, therefore, the daughter of Ahab, King of the Israelites, and of Queen Jezebel, a most wicked woman. She was married to Joram <note>Also known as Jehoram</note>, the son of Jehoshaphat, King of Jerusalem. 

At length, after Jehoshaphat and the eldest son Ahaziah <note>original: "Ozia"; the succession of these biblical kings is often complex in medieval Latin transcriptions</note>—to whom the succession of the kingdom had fallen upon the father’s death—were removed, her husband Joram was unexpectedly crowned King of Jerusalem. He also wished this wife of his to be queen. To this splendor, after her father Ahab had died, her brother Joram <note>Jehoram of Israel, not to be confused with her husband, Jehoram of Judah</note>, having succeeded to his father's throne, added no small amount of light. Indeed, as time passed and having been shaken by many previous misfortunes, she saw her son Ahaziah sitting on his father’s throne after her husband had died, so that the woman shone everywhere with royal honors. Clearly...

She had brought the small boy <note>Jehoash, the only survivor of the royal massacre</note>, who had been pulled away from the slaughter, into the house of Jehoiada the <term>high priest<note>original: "pontificis"</note></term>, her husband, to be kept safe and nourished. And so, through the blood of so many who were impiously murdered, this woman dared to ascend the royal throne and enter the court—as if it were a vacancy created by her own hand—and began to arrange all the royal affairs. 

Why should we wonder at <note>Atreus: a mythical Greek king known for killing his nephews and serving them to his brother</note> Atreus, or <note>Dionysius I: the notoriously cruel tyrant of Syracuse</note> Dionysius, or <note>Jugurtha: a King of Numidia who seized the throne by murdering his adoptive brothers</note> Jugurtha? Thus, Athaliah shone with the royal <term>diadem<note>a jeweled headband worn as a symbol of sovereignty</note></term>; however, she was more notable for being splattered with purple blood than for any mark of royalty. Indeed, just as she had grimly cut down the innocent souls of the <note>Davidic line: the descendants of King David, from whom the Messiah was promised to come</note> house of David with her sword, she was just as capable of venting her rage against both her own embittered relatives and total strangers.

<meta>This page continues a moral philosophical argument regarding the heavy spiritual and psychological price of seizing power through immoral means, likely part of a "Mirror for Princes" style text which warns rulers of the dangers of tyranny.</meta>

We take care and entangle ourselves in even greater crimes while we least expect it, swallowed up by the storm; when it is of no use, we wretches weep over what we have committed. Indeed, the desire for a kingdom against the law is a dreadful thing. For the most part, it is a savage occupation into which one rarely falls by chance; one must ascend either through fraud or through violence. 

If you are driven by fraud—by tricks, snares, perjuries, betrayals, and such things—it will occupy your every thought. If by violence—it weighs upon you with boastings, riots, rumors, cruelty, and ancient rage. And whichever path you intend to take, you must prepare your forces, all of which are managed by the work of wicked men; unless you become their slave, you cannot be the master of the kingdom. 

How, then, does one reach the throne? You must harden your ears to complaints. You must turn your eyes away from tears, crimes, and slaughters, and harden your heart into stone. Let cruelty be armed; let piety be shut out. Let reason be neglected; let injustice be cultivated. Let power be stripped from the sacred laws; let it serve lust. Let malice be summoned. Simplicity is mourned. Rapacity, luxury, and gluttony devour the provinces of the noble kingdom <note>original: "incliti reg̃ puincie"</note>; let it spare neither things divine nor human. Let sacred and profane things be mixed, and with life crushed by the highest wickedness, let one wade through blood. Let pious men be struck down; let the impious be raised up. Let virgins be defiled and boys dragged into abuse. Let the virtue of the mind be condemned, and vice forgiven; and everywhere, with no peace, let discord celebrate its triumphs. 

O, what a spectacle is the king’s accession! But why have we approached the throne through blood and shameful deeds? Would that, however it was gained, one could live innocently. Immediately, as suspicion grows urgent, the leading men are driven into exile. The rich are reduced to poverty. Old friends are banished. Brothers, sons, grandsons, and parents are imprisoned as if they were rivals, and they are killed. No faith, no holiness, no justice is preserved. One watches anxiously; one sleeps with difficulty; nor is food tasted without fear. And when the faithful have been driven away, all of life is handed over to the wicked. 

O beautiful, O desirable, O praiseworthy possession sought with such anxiety! It would indeed have been easier to enter a poor man’s little hut <note>The author uses the word "tuguriolum," meaning a very small, humble cottage or hut.</note>, full of peace, strong in security, and empty of worry. These heights <note>original: "hec ardua"</note> are sought with blood and held with such great fear. And we, who have cast out the faithful because of our suspicions, now <unclear>entrap</unclear> ourselves with snares as crime takes its toll...

<summary>This page provides a grim critique of the tyrant's rise to power, detailing the moral corruption, violence, and constant paranoia that accompany a throne seized through fraud or force. It contrasts the miserable, fearful life of a usurper with the peaceful security of a simple, poor life.</summary>
<keywords>tyranny, kingship, political morality, corruption, paranoia, Mirror for Princes, justice, violence</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the story of Athaliah, the Queen of Judah, and begins the biography of Cloelia, a legendary Roman heroine. The transition highlights the contrast between the "detestable" ends of the wicked and the courageous deeds of the virtuous.</meta>

...we commit. It is very often the case that by the same means through which we gained our entrance, we also find our exits—either similar or even more detestable. Together with our destruction, that hour is snatched away which had been wickedly amassed through many ill-omened days—a fact that **Athaliah** <note>Athaliah was a Queen of Judah in the 9th century BCE, known for her bloody ascent to the throne and eventual execution during a palace coup.</note> was only able to realize too late.

->### Concerning Cloelia, the Roman Virgin<-

**Cloelia** was a distinguished Roman virgin. From which parents she drew her origin, the ancestors either did not record, or the memory has been abolished by antiquity. However, she is judged to have been born of noble stock, as the generosity of her spirit testifies, and because she was given as a hostage for peace among other noblewomen of the Romans to **Porsenna**, the Etruscan king, during the war of **Tarquinius Superbus**.

To explain her praiseworthy audacity in fuller words, one must observe the context: after King Tarquinius the Proud had been expelled for the enormous crime perpetrated by his son **Sextus** against **Lucretia** <note>The rape of Lucretia led to the overthrow of the Roman monarchy and the establishment of the Republic.</note>, and when deceits failed to tempt his return, the situation turned to open war. **Porsenna**, the King of Chiusi, arrived, summoned by the prayers of Tarquinius. After the Etruscans were moved from their position by the integrity of **Horatius Cocles** <note>Horatius Cocles famously defended the Sublician Bridge alone against the Etruscan army.</note> while he defended the Sublician bridge, and after Porsenna had been terrified and moved by the audacity of **Mucius Scaevola** <note>Gaius Mucius Scaevola attempted to assassinate Porsenna and, when captured, thrust his right hand into a fire to show Roman resolve.</note>, he entered into an agreement with the Romans. To preserve the peace, he began taking hostages; thus it happened that **Cloelia** was sent along with many other virgins.

When she saw that so many virgins of the Republic were being detained by a foreign king, she armed her virginal breast with manly audacity. Having deceived the guards at night, she found a horse—which perhaps she had never mounted before—grazing near the **Tiber**. After she had led many of the girls out to the bank, she mounted the horse. Not terrified by the depth of the river or the rushing waters, she led them all safely to the opposite side and restored them to their own people.

When this was discovered in the morning, Porsenna was agitated. In a crowded Senate, it was decided that the leader of the fugitives should be restored to the king as he requested, with the condition that she should later be returned by the king to her own people, unharmed. The King, however, admiring the virtue of the virgin and delighted by her audacity, not only granted her return to her own family but gave her the power to lead away whichever of the remaining hostages she wished. From all of them, she chose only the <term>impuberes</term> <note>original: "impuberes" — children under the age of puberty.</note>, which was seen as commendable to virginal modesty and was most acceptable to the city, because she had primarily liberated those of an age most vulnerable to injury. For this cause, she was decorated by the grateful citizens with an unusual kind of honor: an equestrian statue was granted to her, which, placed at the top of the **Sacred Way**, remained intact for a long time.

<summary>This page recounts the legendary escape of the Roman hostage Cloelia from the camp of the Etruscan king Porsenna, leading her companions across the Tiber. Her bravery resulted in a peace agreement and the rare honor of an equestrian statue in ancient Rome.</summary>
<keywords>Cloelia, Porsenna, Tarquinius Superbus, Horatius Cocles, Mucius Scaevola, Tiber River, Roman Republic, hostages, equestrian statue, Sacred Way</keywords>
