<meta>This page continues the biographical sketches of famous women, likely from a Renaissance-era edition of Giovanni Boccaccio’s "Of Famous Women" (De Mulieribus Claris). The previous focus on Greek and Roman heroines continues here with the story of Hippo and the introduction of Megullia.</meta>

-># On Hippo, a Greek woman.<-

**Hippo** was a Greek woman, as is well enough established from the records of the ancients <note>original: "codicibus veterum"</note>. However, I do not believe she reached her high status by a single great deed, as we climb toward higher steps of virtue because no one becomes great suddenly. But since both her family and her homeland and her other deeds have been erased by the malice of time, it is my intention to bring into the light what has come down to us, lest it perish or her deserved honor be taken from her.

We have learned, therefore, that this Hippo was by chance captured by enemy sailors. Since she happened to be of great beauty and perceived that the pirates' intentions were set upon her and her chastity, she valued the honor of her purity so highly that she believed it could only be preserved through death. Without waiting for violence, she threw herself headlong into the waves, by which her life was taken and her chastity was saved. Who would not praise such a steadfast resolve <note>The Latin "consilium" here implies a deliberate, firm plan or moral decision.</note> in a woman? In exchange for the few years by which her life might perhaps have been extended, she redeemed her chastity and, through an untimely death, sought eternal glory for herself.

Neither the stormy sea could hide this work of virtue, nor the deserted shore take it away, without it being preserved in the light with its own honor by the perpetual monuments of literature. After her body had been tossed about for some time by the waves as if in mockery, it was driven by those same waves onto the Erythraean shore <note>Erythrae was an ancient city in Ionia, on the coast of modern-day Turkey.</note> and buried by the coastal dwellers in the manner of a shipwrecked person. At last, when her reputation and the cause of her death became known from the enemies, the site of her grave on the shore was raised up with the greatest veneration by the people of Erythrae into a massive and long-lasting mound, as a testimony of the honor she preserved. This was done so that we may know that the light of virtue cannot be darkened by any shadows of adverse fortune.

-># On Megullia Dotata.<-

I believe **Megullia** (whom the ancient Romans surnamed **Dotata** <note>The Latin "Dotata" means "The Well-Endowed" or "The Heavily-Dowered."</note>) was a noble Roman woman in that unrefined and—if I may say so—holy age. This was a time that had not yet broken out from the nurturing arms of poverty into Asiatic splendors and the luxury of great things. She attained this surname of "the Endowed," as I judge, because of the greatness of her ancestors...

<summary>This page recounts the legend of Hippo, a Greek woman who chose to drown herself rather than face sexual assault by pirates, and begins the entry for Megullia Dotata, a Roman woman famous for her massive dowry.</summary>
<keywords>Hippo, Erythrae, chastity, virtue, suicide, pirates, Megullia Dotata, Roman history, dowry, ancient morality</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the biography of Megullia Dotata from Giovanni Boccaccio's "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women). It concludes her story with a moralizing critique of contemporary greed before beginning the entry for Veturia.</meta>

...by her extravagance rather than by any merit of her own deeds. For when she gave five hundred thousand pieces of copper <note>original: *aeris*, referring to the Roman *as*, a bronze or copper coin. In early Rome, this was considered an astronomical sum.</note> to her husband as a dowry, it was seen as something so monstrous in that era that the surname "Dotata" <note>The Well-Dowered</note> was bestowed upon her because of the gift. This name persisted for a long time, to such an extent that if anything beyond the usual custom of the citizens was added to a maiden's dowry, she was immediately called a "Megullia Dotata" herself. 

O blessed simplicity! O praiseworthy poverty! You made that which you rightly considered monstrous seem a joke to the luxury of today. For we have so exceeded the measure of things in every direction that you will hardly find a cobbler, a carpenter, a hired laborer, a camp-follower, or a farm steward who is willing to bring a wife into his home for so small a pittance. Nor is it any wonder, when even common women of the lower classes have claimed for themselves the golden crowns, brooches, bracelets, and other regalia of queens, which they wear—I will not say shamelessly, but certainly with pride. 

Woe is me; I do not know whether to say our spirits have been so puffed up because we trust ourselves too much, or rather—as I believe to be most true—that through our own fault, the ambitions and insatiable desires of mortals have been thus uplifted.

## Concerning Veturia, a Roman Matron <note>Veturia was the mother of the Roman general Gaius Marcius Coriolanus; she is famous for pleading with her son to end his siege of Rome.</note>

--- Page 81 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Gnaeus Marcius Coriolanus, a Roman general who, after being exiled from Rome, defected to the Volscians (a neighboring tribe and rival of Rome).</meta>

...a little while before, he withdrew to the enemies of the Romans. By them, he was received both kindly and honorably; for everywhere, excellence <note>original: "virtus," referring here to military valor and character</note> is held in high value. This man, as much by his own trickery as by that of Attius Tullius the Volscian, drove them into war against the Romans. Having been made the leader of the war by them, he led his army to the Cluilian trenches, at the fourth milestone from Rome. He brought Roman affairs to such a desperate state that the Senate sent envoys to him—an exile—to beg for peace on fair terms. Marcius dismissed them back to their fatherland with a harsh response; because of this, they were sent a second time, but were hardly even received. A third time, the priests came, veiled with sacrificial fillets <note>The "infula" was a woolal band worn by priests and suppliants to signal their sacred status</note> and carrying their insignias as suppliants, but they returned in vain. 

And now, desperation had invaded the minds of the Romans on all sides. Then, a crowd of matrons came wailing to Veturia, Coriolanus's mother, and Volumnia, his wife. They prevailed upon the woman, now of great age, to go to the enemy camp with prayers and tears to appease her son (since it seemed the Republic could not be protected by the arms of men), and to go accompanied by his wife. A huge crowd of followers joined them. 

When Coriolanus learned of her arrival, although he was swollen with pride <note>original: "animo turgidus"</note>, he was nevertheless dismayed by his mother’s presence. Rising from his chair, he left his tent and went to meet her to receive her. Indeed, Veturia—holding Coriolanus’s wife on one side and his children on the other—did not even look at her son before she, setting aside maternal devotion <note>original: "pietate patria"</note>, kindled herself into a rage. Where she had left the city as a humble suppliant, she became a fierce rebuker upon entering the enemy camp. 

Stirring up the strength in her worn-out body, she said: "Halt your step, hostile youth! I wish to know, before I accept your embrace, whether you come to receive a mother or a captive enemy. An enemy, I think. Oh, wretched me! Has this long life, so desired by mortals, brought me to this: that I should see you condemned to exile and thereafter see you as an enemy of the Republic? 

Look around, I beg you, as you stand as an armed enemy upon your throne. Recognize the fatherland you have in your sight. You know indeed—even if you pretend not to—that this is the place where you were begotten, where you were born, and where you were raised with such great labor. With what spirit then, with what mind, with what impulse could you bring hostile arms against her? As you entered these lands, did not the honor due to a parent, the love of a sweet wife, the devotion of your sons, and the reverence for your native fatherland meet you..."

<summary>This page recounts the climax of the Coriolanus legend, where the exiled general's mother, Veturia, and wife, Volumnia, confront him at the gates of Rome to plead for peace. Veturia delivers a powerful, stinging rebuke, questioning how he could turn his weapons against the land of his birth.</summary>
<keywords>Coriolanus, Veturia, Volumnia, Volscians, Roman Republic, siege of Rome, maternal authority, exile, Attius Tullius</keywords>

--- Page 82 ---
<meta>This page continues the speech of Veturia, the mother of the Roman general Coriolanus, who has led an enemy army to the gates of Rome. On the previous page, she likely began her emotional appeal to his sense of duty and family.</meta>

...did not move your fierce heart; they could not crush your angers, however justly they were undertaken. Did it not first come to your mind, when you looked upon those walls: "There is my home and the **household gods** <note>original: "penates patrij mei" — the ancestral spirits and deities believed to protect a Roman home</note> of my fathers; there are my wife and children; there is my unhappy mother, suffering by her own misfortune and my own doing"? The **fathers** <note>original: "patres" — the Roman Senators</note> came; the priests came; but they could not move your stony heart to do upon request what you should have done of your own accord.

I realize I am wretched enough that my own fertility was a curse to my country and to myself; where I thought I had given birth to a son and a citizen, I see I have birthed an enemy—most hostile and inflexible. It would indeed have been better never to have conceived; through my barrenness Rome could have stood without siege, and I, a poor little old woman, could die in a free fatherland. But I can suffer nothing more miserable for me, nor more shameful for you; nor am I to be this wretched for long. As for these, your children, you must see that if you continue, either an untimely death or long servitude awaits them.

Tears followed these words, and then the prayers of his wife and children, along with mutual embraces and the cries of the weeping and praying **matrons** <note>respectable married women of high social standing</note>. By these words, groans, and prayers, it was brought about that—where the majesty of ambassadors and the reverence of priests had failed—the anger of the fiercest leader was broken by the veneration of his mother. His purpose was turned; with his forces dispersed and dismissed, the enemies' camp was moved back from the city.

From this it followed—lest ingratitude detract from the glory of the women—that by a **decree of the Senate** <note>original: "senatus consulto"</note>, in that place where Veturia had softened her son's anger, a temple to the perpetual memory of this deed and an altar to **Women's Fortune** <note>original: "fortune muliebri"</note> were built of cut stone. This temple, although it is most ancient, remains almost undiminished even into our own age. 

It was also ordained that when women passed by—to whom little or no honor had been shown by men until that age—men should rise and yield the way <note>a significant change in Roman social protocol, granting women public deference</note>, a ritual which is preserved in our country according to ancient custom even until now. Furthermore, it was permitted for them to wear the distinguished ear-ornaments of Eastern women, as well as purple clothing and golden brooches and bracelets. Nor are there those lacking who assert that by the same decree of the Senate, it was added that women could inherit from anyone, which was not permitted before.

<summary>This page concludes Veturia’s emotional plea to her son Coriolanus, which successfully moves him to spare Rome and retreat. In gratitude, the Roman Senate establishes the Temple of Fortuna Muliebris and grants women unprecedented social honors and legal rights.</summary>
<keywords>Coriolanus, Veturia, Rome, Fortuna Muliebris, Women's Rights, Senate Decree, Volscians, Matrons</keywords>

--- Page 83 ---
<meta>This page continues a discussion regarding the Roman matron Veturia and her influence on the rights and luxuries afforded to women, before transitioning to the biography of the Greek painter Thamyris.</meta>

Whether her merit <note>Referring to Veturia, the mother of the general Coriolanus, who successfully pleaded with her son to end his siege of Rome in the 5th century BCE.</note> should therefore be more hateful to men or more pleasing to women is a matter some think depends on a judgment that I consider most certain. For while these ornaments are displayed, the wealth of men is exhausted, and women walk about distinguished by royal attire. Men are impoverished; with the inheritances of their ancestors taken away, the women who follow them are enriched. The distinguished are honored, and even those who are not illustrious are honored. Many disadvantages for the former and advantages for the latter followed from these events. I would speak against Veturia with curses because of the pride that followed for women, had not Roman liberty stood on its own feet. But I cannot praise that generosity of the Senate, a custom so harmful and persevering through so many centuries. They would have been content with a smaller gift; it seemed a very great thing that the Temple of Womanly Fortune <note>original: "muliebri fortune"; This refers to the Temple of Fortuna Muliebris, established to honor the women who saved Rome.</note> was enriched. 

But as the world of women is, so are womanly men. Whatever time was adverse to men—though it has consumed many useful things—it could not consume or diminish the rights of women, who keep their status with tenacious perseverance. Therefore, let them applaud Veturia; let them honor her name and merit as often as they are adorned with dear gems, purple, and golden brooches, and as men rise for them as they walk, while at the same time the wealth of the dying is counted.

### Concerning Thamyris, daughter of Micon.

Thamyris was an outstanding female painter in her time. Although antiquity may have perhaps first taken away her name <note>The text suggests that while her specific fame might fade, her reputation as a pioneer remains.</note>, yet it has not been able to take away her excellence or her skill. They say, therefore, that in the ninetieth <term>Olympiad<note>A four-year period used by the ancient Greeks to track time; the 90th Olympiad would be around 420 BCE.</note></term>, she was the daughter of Micon the painter. Truly, since we read that there were two Micons, and both were painters who flourished at Athens at the same time, they do not distinguish between them except by these few words: that she was the daughter of the Micon to whom the surname "the Younger" <note>original: "mioris cognomē"; likely referring to Micon the Younger to distinguish him from the more famous Micon the Elder.</note> is said to have been added. 

Indeed, whoever her father was, she imitated his art with such marvelous genius—despising typical womanly duties—that while Archelaus reigned among the Macedonians, she achieved such singular glory in painting that at Ephesus, where Diana <note>The Roman name for the Greek goddess Artemis.</note> was worshipped with special honor, they for a long time preserved an image of that same Diana, painted by her hand on a certain panel, as a celebrated work. Which, since it had lasted into a very long age of craftsmanship...

...bore testimony of this so graciously that even to this day it seems memorable and indeed most praiseworthy, if we look upon the spindles and baskets <note>original: "hysos et calathos" – likely referring to "fusos," the tools of spinning and weaving which were symbols of domestic female virtue</note> of other women.

->**Of Queen Artemisia.**<-

Artemisia, Queen of Caria, was a woman of immense spirit and a perpetual example to posterity of the most sacred and rare love, and of unimpeachable widowhood. Although the parents from whom she descended or the country from which she came have not been sufficiently recorded for our times, it is enough for the praise of her nobility to know that she was the wife of Mausolus, the most powerful king of Caria. She loved him so much in life that, surviving him, she could not forget him in death; of which fact distinguished monuments stood for a long time. 

For (if faith is to be placed in famous writers), when her most beloved <note>The OCR reads "antiqssimus" (most ancient), but the context and historical source suggest "amantissimus" (most loving/beloved).</note> husband first closed his final day, she bore out his lifeless corpse with great honors. She did not allow his ashes, carefully collected after the funeral pyre, to be stored away in a golden urn; she judged every other vessel to be unsuitable for so beloved a spouse except for her own breast, in which the flames of her long-held love burned far more fiercely than usual after his death. Therefore, so that what was earthly of him might reside where the perpetual memory of his past life remained, she mixed the collected ashes little by little into her drinks and consumed them all until they were gone. She dedicated the remainder of her life to perpetual tears, and so, consumed by grief and believing she would soon go to her husband, she arrived joyfully at death.

Truly, as a widow she performed great deeds. It was an ancient custom for distinguished men to have notable tombs erected; so that her work might appear more in keeping with her love, Artemisia, setting aside all greed, devised an exceedingly marvelous and costly monument. Not content with just one or some common artisan, she ordered Scopas, Bryaxis, Timotheus, and Leochares <note>These were the four most famous sculptors of the 4th century BCE; each was said to have decorated one side of the tomb.</note> (whom Greece proclaimed the greatest masters of the whole world in that age) to be summoned. According to their judgment, she caused a magnificent "Mausoleum" <term>Mausoleum: Originally the specific name for the tomb of King Mausolus, this word became the general term for any grand, monumental tomb.</term> to be designed for Mausolus and constructed with prepared marbles. Through that marvelous work, she made her beloved husband's name eternal, even if it were not so otherwise. Indeed, because it excelled almost all buildings on earth in both art and expense, it was long remembered as one of the Seven Wonders of the World.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Virginia, a famous figure of Roman virtue, during the conflict between the plebeians and the Decemviri (the ten-man commission). The previous context established her father Verginius's military service and the beauty of Virginia that attracted the lustful eye of the ruler Appius Claudius.</meta>

...remained at Mount Algidus against the Aequi <note>The Latin reads "equos" (horses), but in the context of Roman history, this refers to the Aequi, a hostile neighboring tribe.</note>, and where Verginius was serving as a soldier. While things stood thus, it happened to the misfortune of Virginia that her beauty so pleased Appius Claudius—the <term>decemvir</term> <note>A member of a ten-man commission appointed to govern Rome and codify its laws.</note> who had remained alone to protect the city with Spurius Oppius while the others were away fighting—that he fell desperately in love with her. Since the girl, still in her tender years, frustrated his flatteries, and since her heart, imbued with <gloss>chastity</gloss>, could not be swayed by any great gifts, nor by prayers or threats, Appius was seized by such an insane fury that he turned his wavering mind to various schemes. Determining that it would be unwise to use public force openly, he turned his cleverness toward fraud. 

He arranged by a plot that Marcus Claudius—a man of his own household and of the utmost audacity—should, at the first opportunity, seize the girl as she was passing through the <term>forum</term> <note>The central public square of Rome, serving as a marketplace and the site of judicial business.</note>, claiming her as his own runaway slave <margin>The legal term used is *mancipium*.</margin>, and lead her to his house. If anything should obstruct the attempt, he was to immediately drag her before Appius for a legal hearing. When, a few days later, the client seized her with reckless daring as she passed, claiming her as his own, the girl cried out. She resisted the impure man with all her strength, and with the help of the matrons with whom she was walking, a crowd of people suddenly gathered. 

Among these people arrived Icilius <note>Virginia's betrothed and a former tribune.</note> and Numitor <note>Virginia's grandfather.</note>; after arguments were exchanged on both sides, the matter reached the point where she was led to the <term>praetorium</term> <note>The judicial seat or court.</note> before the "loving" judge. From the burning Appius, it was obtained that the judgment should be deferred until the following day. In this, the fraud of Appius did not succeed; though he had sent orders to the camp that Verginius should not be permitted by the commanders to come to Rome if summoned, the father—having already been called by his friends—arrived promptly. 

Verginius came into the <term>curia</term> <note>The assembly or court building.</note> with his daughter and other friends, dressed in <term>sordidatus</term> <note>Dressed in mourning or shabby clothes, a Roman custom intended to win the sympathy of a jury.</note> attire. On the other side stood Marcus Claudius, acting for Appius, seeking the slave. Despite hearing Verginius's plea, the lustful presiding official adjudged Virginia to be a runaway slave. When Marcus attempted to seize her, and Verginius had spoken many things against Appius in vain, the father finally obtained—while Appius ground his teeth in anger—permission to speak at least a little with the girl and her nurse. He claimed this was so that, perhaps by discovering the truth of this "old error" <note>Referring to the false claim that she was a slave swapped at birth.</note>, he might yield to his grief with less hesitation. 

And when he had gone aside with them near the Shops of Cloacina <note>The *Tabernae Lanistariae* near the Shrine of Venus Cloacina in the Forum.</note>, yet still in sight of the court, he seized a butcher’s knife and said: "In the only way I can, my beloved daughter, I give you your liberty..."

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Roman maiden Virginia and the corruption of the Decemvir Appius Claudius, following the moment her father Virginius decides to kill her to save her from slavery and dishonor.</meta>

"[I claim] your [freedom]," <note>original: "tuam vendico"; this refers to Virginius reclaiming his daughter's liberty through the only means left to him—death.</note> and he plunged [the knife] into the maiden's breast, to the immense grief of the onlookers. Whereupon the unhappy maiden, falling down in the sight of all those standing by, poured out her blood along with her life. And thus the most foul hope of the lustful Appius was emptied out through the slaughter of an innocent. 

By the act of Virginius and the resulting second secession of the common people, it was brought about that the **Decemvirs** <term>The Decemvirate was a ten-man commission in early Rome granted supreme power to draft the laws of the Twelve Tables.</term> were forced to abdicate their power and restore to the Roman people the liberty which they had seized. Not long after, at the petition of Virginius—now a tribune of the plebs—a trial date was set for **Appius Claudius**. When he arrived to plead his case, he was dragged to prison by order of Virginius. Trapped in that prison, so that he might escape his deserved disgrace and—guilty as he was—atone to the spirit of the innocent Virginia, he ended his life there by the noose, the sword, or by poison. 

However, his reckless client **Marcus Claudius** <note>The man Appius used to falsely claim Virginia was his runaway slave.</note> lamented his crime in a way he did not deserve (for he was sent into exile), with both his patron's goods and his own being confiscated for the public treasury. 

Nothing is more destructive than an unjust judge. Whenever such a man follows the command of a wicked mind, it is inevitable that the entire order of law is perverted. The power of the law is dissolved, the work of virtue is weakened, the reins are loosened for crime, and in short, every public good is dragged to ruin. 

If Appius’s wicked undertaking does not sufficiently appear so elsewhere, the events that followed bring it into the light. For while this powerful man failed to place a curb on his evil lust, he nearly succeeded—through a freedman steeped in fraud—in turning a free woman into a slave, a maiden into an adulteress, and a betrothed woman into a concubine. By his detestable decree, it came to pass that a father was armed against his daughter, fatherly love was turned into cruelty, and so that the incestuous man might not enjoy his fraud-won desire, an innocent girl was killed. Cries rose in the city, riots broke out in the camps, a separation of the commoners from the "fathers" <note>The Patricians or Senators.</note> arose, and nearly the entire Roman state was placed in peril. 

O famous leader and distinguished lawmaker! He did not fear to perpetrate himself what he should have punished in others with dire execution. Alas for me, how often we mortals are endangered by this plague! How often we are dragged to an undeserved end, and oppressed by a foul yoke, we are burdened, despoiled, and killed by pressing wickedness. What evil is this? Those in authority <note>original: "perfecti"; referring to those of high rank or "perfected" status.</note> do not fear to turn into a license for crimes that which was invented to temper lusts, terrified by no fear. And while it is necessary [for the eyes of a philosopher...] <note>Text cuts off at "oculos".</note>

->**Concerning Claudia the Vestal Virgin**<-

I would believe Claudia, the Vestal Virgin <term>Vestal Virgin: a priestess of the Roman goddess Vesta, bound by a vow of chastity and held in high public honor; their persons were considered sacred</term>, was worthily born of the noble blood of the Romans when I consider her remarkable devotion to her father. While her father was leading a triumph <term>Triumph: a grand civil ceremony and religious rite in ancient Rome to publicly celebrate and sanctify the success of a military commander</term> by decree of the Senate, with a great crowd of Romans watching, one of the tribunes of the plebs <term>Tribune of the plebs: a Roman official elected to protect the interests of the common people, possessing the power to veto or physically stop the actions of other officials</term> broke out in a personal feud against him as if he were a man of no merit. With insolent audacity, he cast violent hands upon the man in the triumph and attempted to pull him down from his chariot. When the virgin Claudia saw this, moved by urgent devotion and saddened by the sight, she forgot her sex and the dignity of her sacred headbands <note>The "vittae," or woolen headbands, which marked her status as a priestess and should have made her untouchable</note>. She could not endure it. Instead, she suddenly broke through the center of the crowd with great force. Forcing the mob to give way at the sight of her boldness, she placed herself between the arrogance of the tribune and the glory of her father. By her daring, the tribune was moved aside, and her father proceeded to his ascent to the Capitol. Oh, sweet love! Oh, unbroken devotion! What strength must we believe was granted to the weak body of a maiden? What else but her religious duty could have led her to see the injustice overwhelming the man who had been the nurturer of her infancy? He had been the one who soothed her with pious charms, the one who offered prayers for her safety and removed all harm, the instructor of her youth. But let enough be said of this; I ask who, because of this, among those burying their dead, would not honor a holy woman...

### On Virginia, the wife of Lucius Volumnius

<margin>v</margin> **Virginia** was indeed a famous matron among the Romans. She was, however, distinct from the earlier Virginia <note>The author distinguishes her from the more famous Virginia whose death led to the downfall of the Decemviri</note>, though she was born to a certain Aulus, who was likewise a patrician <term>Patrician: the hereditary land-owning noble class of ancient Rome</term>. Beyond the emblems of her nobility, she was considered superior to all others in her era due to the merits of her chastity. It will be enough to recount a single, most praiseworthy act of hers to reflect upon her whole life and grant her the renown she deserves.

As is well established, there was once in the city of Rome, in the Cattle Market <term>Forum Boarium: the primary market and religious center near the Tiber</term> near the round temple of Hercules, a famous shrine of Patrician Chastity. It had been holily dedicated long ago by noblewomen. In the year when Quintus Fabius and Quintus Publilius Decius Mus—serving his fourth consulship—were in office <note>approximately 296 BCE</note>, the Senate ordered that supplications <note>original: "supplicationes"; public days of prayer or thanksgiving</note> be held at this and other temples to atone for various omens. In that place, the patrician women were performing the sacred rites according to the ancient custom, with the utmost purity.

It happened that Virginia approached with the other women to participate in the sacrifice. However, she was haughtily removed by the ruling patrician matrons because she had married Lucius Volumnius, a man of plebeian <term>Plebeian: the general body of free Roman citizens, distinct from the patricians</term> rank, even though he had been consul the previous year. A brief quarrel broke out among the women at the sacred shrine, which finally, through womanly indignation, flared up into a great fire of tempers. 

But Virginia asserted that she was both chaste and a patrician, and that she should by no means be barred from the temple of Patrician Chastity simply because she had married a plebeian man as a virgin. After she had extolled the deeds of her husband with wonderful praises, she left the patrician women and returned home in indignation. There, she added a remarkable deed to her words. Since she possessed a large house on the Long Street <term>Vicus Longus: a street running between the Quirinal and Viminal hills</term> where she lived with her husband, she decided that a portion of it was sufficient for a modest shrine. She partitioned it off from the rest of the house, and there [established] an altar...

--- Page 95 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of Virginia, a plebeian woman of noble character, who establishes a new cult of modesty after being excluded from the patrician rites. It then transitions to the biography of Flora.</meta>

...instituted this altar and submitted it to them, complaining of the insult she had suffered. "I therefore pray and exhort you," she said, "that just as you see the men of this city engaged in a continuous contest of virtue, so you too should take up a contest for the sake of matronly modesty alone. Give your best efforts so that this altar, which I dedicate to **Plebeian Chastity** in your presence, may be believed to be tended more piously and by more chaste women than the other. Let it be clear through your actions that heavenly souls are infused not only into the hearts of patrician women." 

*O worthy and most holy words for a matron! O indignation worthy of praise, and a discovery to be raised to the stars with joyful applause!* This vow was not made by these women for the sake of men's wealth or for lewd ornaments, but rather to guard against the wanton and impudent eyes and desires of young men. They proceeded with the best of intentions and holy strength to earn their own glory of chastity. This was so well-observed then and for a long time afterward that no woman was granted the right to sacrifice in that temple unless she was of proven modesty and had been a <term>univira</term> <note>original: "uni tantum viro nuphisset"; a woman who had married only one husband, a high moral ideal in Roman society</note>. By such holiness, she equaled the sanctity of the patrician altar and shattered the lustful hopes of the "incestuous" eyes of onlookers. Nor would I doubt that many women, out of a desire for glory and to escape the shame of being barred from the sacrifice, applied themselves with great zeal to the preservation of their chastity.

## Concerning Flora the Prostitute, Goddess of Flowers and Wife of Zephyrus

Antiquity seems to testify that **Flora** was a Roman woman. To the same degree that her shameful profession <note>original: "ignominos' questus"; referring to her life as a courtesan</note> lacked honor, Fortune favored her fame. Now, she was (as everyone asserts) an extremely wealthy woman, though writers disagree on how she obtained her riches. 

Some say that she spent the entire "flower" of her youth and the beauty of her body as a public prostitute among the brothels, pimps, and wicked young men. By stripping various fools of their property with wanton flatteries—as is the custom of such women—and by gnawing and picking away at them from every side, she eventually amassed these vast riches. 

Others, however, preferring a more "honorable" or elegant explanation, relate a pleasant and humorous story about her. They assert that a certain temple-warden of **Hercules** in Rome, having nothing to do, began a game of dice. He played against himself, assigning his right hand to Hercules and his left hand to himself. He made a wager that if...

<meta>This page continues a discussion of Roman mythology, specifically focusing on the origins of the goddess Flora and the Floralia festival. The text likely draws from early Christian polemics (such as Lactantius) which sought to debunk pagan deities by claiming they were actually humans of ill-repute.</meta>

For a long time now, it has been told that there was a native nymph of marvelous beauty named Chloris. She was most ardently loved by the wind Zephyrus <note>The Greek god of the West Wind.</note> (whom in Latin we call Favonius <note>original: "fauoníū"</note>), and was eventually taken by him as his wife. In their foolishness, the people named him among the gods, and it was said that a certain gift of divinity was granted to her by him as a dowry or wedding gift (as is often the custom). 

This came with the duty that she should first adorn the trees, hills, and meadows with flowers and rule over them. For this reason, from "Chloris" she came to be called "Flora." <note>The author is explaining the transition from the Greek name Chloris (associated with the pale green of new growth) to the Latin Flora.</note> Furthermore, because fruit follows from flowers, they believed that if her divinity were appeased with games, she would grant those fruits with a certain great abundance and lead them to ripeness. Thus, sacred rites and games were granted to this goddess by the ancients.

Led astray by this deception, they believe that she—who while living had frequented the vaulted archways <term>fornices: the brick arches of Roman stadiums and public buildings where prostitutes worked; the root of the word "fornication."</term> and prostrated herself to anyone for even the smallest copper coin <note>original: "minima stipe"</note>—was carried into heaven on the wings of Zephyrus (as if on his own wings) to sit with Queen Juno and the other goddesses. They think <note>The text ends mid-word: "arbit-" likely for "arbitrantur" (they think/judge).</note>

<summary>This page recounts the mythological origin of the goddess Flora as the wife of the West Wind, Zephyrus, while simultaneously offering a cynical Christian critique that she was actually a common prostitute deified by the Romans.</summary>
<keywords>Flora, Chloris, Zephyrus, Favonius, Roman Mythology, Floralia, Etymology, Christian Polemic</keywords>

--- Page 98 ---
<meta>This page continues a series of "memorable deeds" regarding filial devotion. It concludes the famous story of "Roman Charity," where a daughter saves her parent from execution by starvation.</meta>

...extraordinarily, so that she might bring nothing inside with her, she obtained permission from the guard to enter the prison. She then helped her now-starving mother with milk (with which she was then overflowing from a recent birth). Finally, after several consecutive days, the guard began to wonder how the condemned woman had drawn breath for so long without food. Watching secretly to see what the daughter was doing with her mother, he noticed that she was offering her breasts for her mother to suckle. 

Marveling at this devotion <term>pietas; a Roman virtue encompassing duty to family, country, and the gods</term> and the unusual method of the daughter nourishing her mother, he reported his finding to the triumvir <term>one of three judicial officials in charge of prisons and executions</term>, and the triumvir reported it to the praetor <term>a high-ranking Roman magistrate</term>. The praetor then announced the matter to the public council. By common consent, it was decided that because of the daughter’s devotion, the mother’s deserved punishment should be waived as a gift. 

If antiquity bestowed an oak crown <note>original: "querneam coronam" — the Civic Crown, one of the highest military honors, awarded to a soldier who saved a fellow citizen's life</note> on a soldier who saved a citizen by force in battle, with what praise shall we decorate a child who saved her mother in prison? Indeed, you will find no wreath among the foliage worthy enough for such a devoted deed. This devotion was not only holy but admirable; nor should it merely be equaled to the gift of nature—by which we are taught to lead the weak age of infants with milk—but rather it should be preferred over it, as by this means parents are withdrawn from death. 

The powers of devotion are therefore marvelous. For it penetrates not only female hearts (which are easily drawn to compassion and tears) but sometimes even those hardened in unrestrained and adamantine stubbornness. Sitting at the very center of the soul, it first softens every hard thing with flexible humanity. As the best seeker of opportunities, it causes tears to mix with those in misery, making people undergo sicknesses and dangers at least in empathy, and sometimes, if remedies are lacking, even to undergo vicarious deaths. These effects are so great that we should wonder less if we, as devoted children, do something for our parents, since in doing so we seem rather to be returning a favor and paying back through a due restoration what we received from them in our youth.

# About Marcia, daughter of Varro <note>original: "De Marcia Varronis"</note>

<margin>A metal paperclip is attached to the left edge of the page. Some text from the reverse side of the page is visible through the paper.</margin>

<summary>This page recounts the legendary act of Pero, who breastfed her imprisoned mother to save her from starvation, leading to a legal pardon. The author reflects on the supreme power of filial duty (pietas) to soften even the hardest hearts.</summary>

<keywords>Roman Charity, Pero, Mycon, filial piety, breastfeeding, Roman justice, oak crown, Marcia daughter of Varro, caritas romana</keywords>

--- Page 99 ---
For I do not find her bound to the priesthood by her garment, nor devoted to the mark of Diana <note>The goddess of the hunt and virginity, often associated with female religious vows</note>, nor entangled in any other religious profession (by which many women are either coerced or restrained). Rather, having overcome the "sting of the flesh" <note>original: "carnis aculeo"; a common medieval metaphor for physical desire</note> through the integrity of her mind alone—a struggle to which even the most excellent men have occasionally succumbed—she preserved her body unstained by the "contagion of man" <note>referring to her lifelong vow of virginity</note> until her death.

Truly, while Marcia is to be greatly praised for this commendable constancy, she is no less deserving of praise for her manly intellect <note>original: "ingenij virilis"; in the author's time, "manly" was a high compliment for a woman's intellectual strength</note> and the skill of her hands. Whether she learned her craft from a master or if nature itself was her teacher remains uncertain to us. What seems most certain is that she rejected womanly duties <note>likely referring to domestic tasks like spinning or weaving</note> so as not to waste away in idleness, and instead devoted herself entirely to the study of painting and sculpture. In time, she painted so skillfully and elegantly with a brush, and carved images from ivory so well, that she surpassed Sopolis and Dionysius <note>Two famous Greek painters mentioned by Pliny the Elder</note>, the most celebrated artists of her era.

The most famous proof of this was that the panels she painted were valued more highly than all others. Furthermore—and this is far more remarkable—it is said that she not only painted exceptionally well (which others have occasionally done), but that her hands were so swift at painting that no one else ever possessed such speed. For a long time, many examples of her art survived, but the most notable among them was her own likeness. She captured her own features and soft colors on a panel so perfectly by consulting a mirror <note>This is one of the first historical mentions of a woman painting a self-portrait</note> that no contemporary who saw it could doubt who it was.

Among her other unique habits (to speak of her singular character), it is said that whether she was painting with a brush or carving with a chisel <term>celte: a specialized tool for carving stone or ivory</term>, she very frequently depicted women and rarely or never depicted men. I believe the strength of her modesty was the cause of this habit. Since the artists of antiquity usually depicted figures as naked or half-naked, she felt it was necessary either to make "imperfect" men <note>meaning anatomically vague or poorly defined</note>, or, if she made them "perfect" <note>anatomically accurate</note>, she would seem to have forgotten her virginal shame. To avoid falling into either trap, she decided it was easier to abstain from depicting men altogether.

<summary>This page describes the life and artistic career of Marcia (Iaia of Cyzicus), an ancient painter and sculptor praised for her lifelong virginity, her incredible speed in painting, and her famous self-portrait created using a mirror.</summary>
<keywords>Marcia, Iaia of Cyzicus, women artists, virginity, painting, sculpture, self-portrait, Sopolis, Dionysius, ancient art, modesty</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the biographical accounts of famous women from antiquity, focusing here on the Roman matron Sulpicia. The text emphasizes the public recognition of female virtue in the Roman Republic.</meta>

# Concerning Sulpicia, wife of Fulvius.

Sulpicia was once a most venerable woman who, by the testimony of the Roman matrons, attained no less praise for her strictly observed chastity than Lucretia did by ending her life with a blade <note>Lucretia was a legendary Roman heroine whose suicide after her honor was violated led to the founding of the Roman Republic; she was the classical archetype of female virtue.</note>. She was the daughter of Servius Paterculus and the wife of Fulvius Flaccus; both were noble men. 

When the Senate, following an ancient custom and the guidance of the sacred books <note>original: "libris"; likely referring to the Sibylline Books, a collection of oracular utterances consulted during Roman religious crises</note>, decreed that a statue of <term>Venus Verticordia</term> <note>literally "Venus the Changer of Hearts," a title given to the goddess to turn women's minds away from lust and toward virtue</note> should be consecrated in the city, they did so so that virgins and other women might not only abstain from lust but also more easily be turned toward commendable modesty. 

They sought—according to the command of the <term>decemviri</term> <note>a commission of ten men tasked with religious or legal duties</note> which stipulated that the most chaste of the Roman matrons should dedicate the image—the most virtuous woman from the vast multitude that then abounded in Rome. 

It was decided that first, a hundred women should be selected from the entire assembly who were considered more distinguished in their modesty. Sulpicia was chosen as one of these. Then, by order of the Senate and the judgment of those same women, ten even more illustrious women were set apart from the hundred, among whom Sulpicia was numbered. Finally, when one was sought from the ten, Sulpicia was chosen by the total consensus of everyone.

To her, it was a beautiful thing to have dedicated the statue of Venus Verticordia in that time. Yet her honor was far greater because she was preferred over others by the estimation of such a vast multitude; she was viewed by the eyes of those present as a kind of heavenly divinity of modesty, confirmed by the solemn oath of all. Indeed, her name and glory seem to have been carried down to every future age with a veneration that almost never fades. 

But someone might ask: "If a hundred chaste women were chosen, what more could be added to the modesty of this one woman so that she deservedly was preferred over the others?" It is clear that those men or women who think modesty consists solely in abstaining from sleeping with men other than one’s husband should consider this carefully <note>The author implies that Sulpicia's virtue was not just the absence of sin, but a superior purity of mind and spirit.</note>.

<summary>This page recounts the selection of Sulpicia as the most virtuous woman in Rome to dedicate the statue of Venus Verticordia, emphasizing her peerless reputation for chastity.</summary>
<keywords>Sulpicia, Venus Verticordia, Roman Matrons, Chastity, Lucretia, Servius Paterculus, Fulvius Flaccus, Decemviri</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Harmonia of Syracuse and begins the account of Busa of Canusium, both examples of extraordinary virtue and loyalty in the face of war.</meta>

She did not even accuse the hiding Harmonia <note>Harmonia was the daughter of Gelon of Syracuse; she was being hunted during a revolt</note> (in whose place she was being killed). But remaining silent and unmoved, receiving the death-bearing blows, she fell. Harmonia was both fortunate and unfortunate: fortunate in such loyalty, yet unfortunate in her situation. This faithful woman <note>original: "Fidelis"; referring to Harmonia</note>, however, when she saw from her hiding place the perseverance of the innocent girl and her brave spirit in the face of death, and the maidenly blood flowing from her wounds, stood amazed. Although the girl had been struck down and she could have escaped as the assassins were already leaving, she began to admire such loyalty with her whole heart. Seized by a noble <term>piety</term> <note>The Latin 'pietas' implies a deep sense of duty, devotion, and moral correctness toward family and country</note>, and with tears flowing, she felt it was wrong to let the shedding of innocent blood go unpunished. Judging it better to descend to the underworld in an early death with such a loyal youth than to prolong a life preserved at such a cost and by another’s faith—or to wait for old age among treacherous citizens—she cried out, "O devotion! O ancient loyalty!" She who had escaped stepped forward, and calling the blood-stained swords back upon herself, she confessed the nurse’s trick, the victim’s loyalty, and her own true identity. She willingly gave her own blood as a tribute to the slain girl, and, harassed by frequent wounds, she collapsed in death beside the corpse as quickly as she could. It was most worthy to restore through literature what her own devotion had taken from her. Truly, it is difficult to discern whose virtue was greater: the loyalty of the one who died first, or the devotion of the one who followed. The first makes the name of virtue eternal; the second does the same for the name of the princess.

[blank space]

-> ### Concerning Busa of Canusium, an Apulian woman. ### <-

Busa (whom some call Paulina, as if Busa were a family name) was an Apulian woman, originally from Canusium. That magnificent deed, which is the only thing antiquity has left to posterity regarding her, makes me believe she was born of noble blood and was splendid in other qualities of mind. For they say that when Hannibal was waging a hostile war of the Carthaginians against the Romans, devastating all of Italy with fire and sword and staining it with much blood—at the time when he had not only overcome his enemies in the great struggle at the Apulian village of Cannae <note>The Battle of Cannae (216 BCE) was one of the most disastrous defeats in Roman history</note> but had broken almost all the Italian forces—it happened that from that conflict and massive slaughter, about ten thousand men, scattered and wandering by night through out-of-the-way paths, came to Canusium. This city at that time remained faithful to its alliance with Rome.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Busa, a noblewoman of Canusium, following the Roman defeat at the Battle of Cannae. It highlights her exceptional virtue compared to male figures of antiquity.</meta>

All of these men—bloodless, weary, destitute, unarmed, naked, and afflicted with wounds—were received by Busa <note>Paulla Busa, a wealthy Apulian woman known for her relief efforts after the Battle of Cannae in 216 BCE</note> in hospitality within her own homes. She was neither terrified by the disaster nor by the power of the victor <note>original: "victoris potencia" — referring to Hannibal, who had just crushed the Roman army</note>. She commanded them, above all else, to be of good heart. By bringing in physicians, she ensured the wounded were cared for with a mother’s affection. With wonderful liberality, she first granted clothing to all who were naked, and then weapons to those without arms. She provided for the daily needs of everyone from her own personal wealth. Once these wretched men were revived with such devotion and restored to hope, she voluntarily granted travel expenses <term>viaticum</term> <note>viaticum: provision or money for a journey</note> to all who wished to depart. In no way did she withdraw her hand from the needs of those who continued to arrive in great numbers.

Indeed, it is a marvel to say, and in a woman it is far more praiseworthy than if it had been done by a man. For the ancients were accustomed to extol Alexander, King of the Macedonians and conqueror of the world, for his magnificent generosity among his other great honors. They asserted that he was accustomed to bestow not only precious jewels, huge sums of money, and similar gifts—which were considered far greater than the generosity of other princes—but truly he gave away excellent principalities, splendid kingdoms, and the most extensive empires...

...it was used most excellently. For Mother Nature <note>original: "natura parens"</note> did not bring gold out from the depths of the earth into the public eye so that it might be carried from the mother's womb into a tomb—which is what greedy men do when they bury it in a storehouse <note>original: "archiuo," referring here to a chest or secure repository</note> and watch over it with excessive care, as if it were to be born again. Rather, before all else, it was intended to be offered for the common good. From that wealth, we should minister aid with a generous spirit—first for our own honorable splendor and the fellowship of friends, and then, if any remains, for those cast down by the injury of fortune, those whom the wrath of heaven <note>original: "ira celi," a common metaphor for natural disasters or divine misfortune</note> exhausts with poverty, those weighed down by unworthy pressures, those shut in a foreign prison for another's crime, and whoever is worn down by anxious need. We do this not indeed so that we may appear great, but that we may be of benefit; not to gain, but to bestow. Such benefits are to be expended with a measure of reason, lest while we help others, we bring upon ourselves a want <note>original: "inopiam"</note> that might force us—I shall not say to lay violent hands on others' property—but even to look upon it with longing eyes.

## Of Sophonisba, Queen of Numidia.

<margin>s</margin>
**S**ophonisba <note>The large initial 'S' is preceded by a small guide letter 's' in the margin for the rubricator.</note> was indeed a queen of the Numidians, shining in power. Though she met the austerity of death with an intrepid spirit of her own choosing, she became far more illustrious through it. For she was the daughter of Hasdrubal, son of Gisco <note>Hasdrubal Gisco was a major Carthaginian general during the Second Punic War.</note> and the greatest prince of the Carthaginians, while Hannibal was harrying the battles. When she was in the flower of her age and of sufficient beauty, she was joined as a maiden in marriage by her father to Syphax, the most powerful king of the Numidians. Her father, a wise man, did not desire this royal alliance for its own sake alone; rather, with the Roman war looming, he hoped to withdraw the only "barbarian" king from the Roman side. Instead, by the help of his charming daughter, he hoped to turn Syphax to the side of the Carthaginians against the Romans. Nor was he deceived by his calculated scheme. For when Syphax celebrated the nuptials, drawn by the girl's beauty—of which he had been forewarned—he was pulled into such a heat of affection that Syphax considered nothing dear or delightful to him besides her. And while the unhappy man was thus inflamed, and it appeared that Cornelius Scipio had crossed from Sicily into Africa with his armies, Hasdrubal—prompted by Sophonisba's warnings, flatteries, and prayers—drew the mind of Syphax so much into his own desire that not only...

...he would abandon the Romans to whom he had pledged the faith of friendship <note>original: "amicitie fidem"</note> and join himself to Carthage and the Carthaginians. But Scipio, a youth of a great spirit, having condemned the wickedness of the barbarian king <note>Referring to King Syphax of western Numidia, who betrayed his alliance with Rome for Carthage</note> and having stationed his troops not far from Carthage, overcame him before all else through King Masinissa <note>A prince of eastern Numidia and a steadfast Roman ally</note>, his ally, and Laelius his legate. By their efforts, Syphax was captured with his army, defeated, and led to Cirta, the royal city of Numidia. Nor was he shown to the citizens laden with chains until the surrender of the city was made to Masinissa. 

When Masinissa entered the city (as Laelius had not yet arrived), amidst the sudden upheaval of events and a general riot, he entered the palace, where he was well-loved. There he was met by Sophonisba <note>The daughter of the Carthaginian general Hasdrubal Gisco and wife of Syphax</note>. She, conscious of her own fortunes, when she saw him entering the vestibule—distinguished by his armor beyond all others—and having recognized him as the king, prostrated herself at his knees. Retaining the spirit of her former fortune <note>Even in defeat, Sophonisba is portrayed with the dignity of her royal rank</note>, she said: 

"Thus, illustrious King, it has seemed good to God and to your good fortune <note>original: "felicitati tue"</note> that over us—who were kings but a moment ago—you may do all that you wish. But even if it is permitted that a captive may utter supplicant voices before her conqueror and the master of her life and death, and touch his knees and his victorious right hand, I pray you, cast down as I am, by your majesty—in which I also shared just a short while ago—and by all things through which our race, our kingdom, and the common Numidian name exist. And if you are received with a better omen than that with which Syphax departed from this place, I beg that you do with me (whom adverse fortune has newly made subject to your law) what seems pious and good in your eyes; provided that I am not handed over alive to the insolent and fastidious judgment of the Romans <note>original: "romanorum arbitrio"</note>. For you can easily perceive what I—a Carthaginian enemy of Rome and daughter of Hasdrubal, not to mention the wife of Syphax—might have to fear. And if every other way is removed, see to it that I die by your hand rather than fall alive into the power of the enemy. I pray and beseech you." 

Masinissa, who was himself a Numidian and (as they all are) prone to passion <note>original: "pronus libidinem"</note>, gazed upon the beauty of her face as she prayed; for indeed, her misfortune had added a certain pious and unusual grace...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Second Punic War, specifically the tragic episode involving Masinissa, a Numidian king allied with Rome, and Sophonisba, a Carthaginian noblewoman.</meta>

(The honor of a suppliant) <note>This fragment likely refers to Masinissa being moved by Sophonisba’s status as a desperate petitioner.</note> moved by humanity and drawn by passion—since Laelius <note>original: "lelius"; Gaius Laelius was a Roman general and close friend of Scipio Africanus.</note> had not yet arrived—while still in armor and having given his right hand, he raised her as she prayed amidst the wailing cries of women and the chaos of soldiers running everywhere. Finally, on the following day, he received Laelius upon his arrival. Returning to the camp with all the royal finery, the rest of the booty, and his new wife, they were first received kindly by Scipio <note>Scipio Africanus, the Roman commander.</note> for their success in battle. Then, after being amicably rebuked by him for celebrating a marriage with a captive of the Roman people, he withdrew into his tent. Having dismissed all witnesses, filled with sighs and tears, he groaned for so long that he was heard by those standing nearby. 

Pressed by necessity, he ordered the most faithful of his servants—to whom he had entrusted the keeping of poison for the uncertain turns of fortune—to be summoned to him. He commanded the servant to carry that poison, diluted in a cup, to Sophonisba, and to say that he would have gladly kept the promise <note>original: "fidem"; refers to his pledge to protect her from the Romans.</note> he had freely given her, if he were able. But since his power of choice was being taken away by those who had the authority, he was fulfilling the promise she herself had requested (not without his own grief): namely, that she should not fall alive into the power of the Romans. Let her, mindful of her father, her country, and the two kings to whom she had been married shortly before, take whatever counsel seemed best to her.

Indeed, having heard the messenger, she said with a steady face: "I accept this wedding gift, and if nothing else could be given by a husband to a wife, I am grateful for it. But report back that I would have died more honorably if I had not married at the time of my own funeral." She spoke these sharp words and took the cup; showing no sign of trembling, she immediately drank it all. Not long after, she collapsed in the death she had sought, piteous. 

Truly, for an aged man for whom life is a burden and there is no hope except death, it would be a great thing—how much more so for a young royal girl, just entering into the world and beginning to perceive what sweetness life holds, it was a magnificent and admirable act, worthy of note, to have met death so fearlessly.

# Concerning Theoxena, Daughter of Prince Herodicus

<margin>t</margin> **Theoxena**, a Thessalian woman of noble birth, left behind a famous testimony of herself for future generations, characterized on one hand by tender devotion and on the other by a stern, even fierce, resolve. She was the daughter of **Herodicus**, a prince of the Thessalians, during the time when **Philip** <note>Philip V of Macedon (r. 221–179 BCE), known for his struggles against the rising power of Rome.</note>, the son of Demetrius, ruled over the Macedonians. She had a sister from the same parents named **Archo**. 

Initially, through the wickedness of this same Philip, their father was killed; as time passed, through the same treacherous cruelty, their husbands were also deprived of life by a heinous death <note>original: *nepharia morte*</note>. Each woman was left with a single surviving son. After they were widowed, Archo first married a certain **Poris** <note>original: *Porridi*</note>, a leading man of their nation, and bore him many children. Theoxena, however, possessed a more steadfast spirit and, though many nobles unsuccessfully sought her hand in marriage, she remained a widow for a long time. 

But when death took Archo, Theoxena was moved by compassion for her nephews; she did not want them to fall into the hands of another stepmother or be raised less carefully by their father. To ensure she could raise them as if they were her own, she joined herself in marriage to the same Poris—a practice not prohibited by any law at that time. She began to raise them with such devoted care, as if she had given birth to them herself, that it became clear she had married Poris more for their service than for her own convenience.

While things were in this state, it happened that Philip, King of the Macedonians (being of a restless disposition), was again planning war in his mind against the **Romans**, who were then flourishing in great prosperity. For this reason, in a massive upheaval of his kingdom, he emptied nearly all the maritime cities of Thessaly of their old inhabitants. He ordered them to migrate in crowds to the inland region of **Paeonia** <note>original: *Poemiam*; an ancient region north of Macedon.</note> (which was later called Emathia), and he granted the vacant towns on the coast to his Thracian allies, whom he considered more suitable and faithful for the coming war. 

When he heard the curses of those who were departing, he realized he would never be safe from the children of those nobles he had brutally killed long ago, and thus he decided to destroy them all. When he ordered them to be seized and kept in custody—not to kill them all in one blow, but to eliminate them one by one over time—it happened that Theoxena heard the edict of the wicked king, and in fear for her and her sister’s...

For this reason, some were consumed by one death and others by another; with Theoxena's help, they threw themselves from the ship, half-dead and still gasping. 

When she had driven those she had persuaded into death for the sake of liberty, she did not wish to appear to have reserved for herself the very slavery she had dissuaded others from. With an extraordinary spirit, she embraced her husband—who was still praying—as a companion in death, and dragged him headlong with her into the stormy sea. She judged it better to die freely than to waste away in foul slavery while living. Thus, leaving the ship empty for the enemies, she deprived Philip of the satisfaction of his cruelty, and this austere woman produced a monument for herself worthy of memory.

<margin>a.</margin>
### Concerning Berenice, Queen of Cappadocia.

<margin>b.</margin> **Berenice of Pontus** (who was also named **Laodice**), even if she might seem to have sought a place among famous women by the brilliance of her blood, is judged to have occupied it far more not because of a fervent love for her son (which mothers most commonly display), but because of the merit of her remarkable audacity in taking vengeance for him. Lest this should seem a slip of the pen, it must be explained in a few words. 

She was the daughter of Mithridates, King of Pontus <note>original: "mitridatis regis ponti"; this refers to Mithridates V Euergetes.</note>—the one who had waged war with the Romans against Aristonicus a little while before and had finally been snatched away by a sudden death. She married Mithridates <note>This refers to Ariarathes VI of Cappadocia, who was sometimes associated with the name Mithridates in various regional records, though history primarily knows him as Ariarathes.</note>, the son of the previous King Mithridates and a long-time enemy. When her husband was killed by a certain Gordius through the treachery of Mithridates (Berenice's own brother), two sons survived him. 

But when Nicomedes, the King of Bithynia at that time, occupied Cappadocia—viewing it as vacant after the king's murder—the greedy Mithridates feigned family devotion <term>piety (pietas): the Roman virtue of duty to family, country, and gods</term>. Proclaiming that he would recover the kingdom for his nephews, he took up arms against Nicomedes. Indeed, when he discovered that the widow Laodice <note>Berenice is referred to here by her other name, Laodice.</note> had married Nicomedes, his feigned devotion turned to true [action]. Having driven Nicomedes out of Cappadocia by force of arms, he restored the ancestral kingdom to Ariarathes, the elder of the brothers.

<summary>This page describes the final suicide of Theoxena and her family to avoid King Philip's capture, then introduces Berenice (Laodice) of Cappadocia, caught in a power struggle between her brother Mithridates and Nicomedes of Bithynia.</summary>
<keywords>Theoxena, King Philip V, Berenice of Cappadocia, Laodice, Mithridates of Pontus, Nicomedes of Bithynia, Ariarathes, Roman history, female martyrs, political intrigue</keywords>

--- Page 110 ---
<meta>This page continues the narrative of the tragic dynastic struggles in Cappadocia, focusing on the aftermath of the assassinations ordered by Mithridates VI of Pontus and the desperate vengeance of the victims' mother.</meta>

When he <note>original: "Quem"; referring to Mithridates VI of Pontus.</note> later regretted the deed and had him killed through deception, and the other younger son—whose name was **Ariarathes**—had been recalled from Asia by friends and appeared to rule (as some believe), he too was slaughtered through the treachery of that same Mithridates. 

The unhappy mother <note>Likely Laodice, the sister of Mithridates and mother of the Cappadocian princes.</note>, robbed of both her children, took this so bitterly that, driven by grief and forgetting the perceived limitations of her sex, she seized weapons in a fury and mounted a chariot with yoked horses. She did not stop pursuing the royal attendant Ceneus—the executor of that wicked crime—who fled in headlong haste, until she struck him down with a stone, having been unable to reach him with her spear. 

Then, filled with indignation, she drove her chariot over his fallen corpse. Amidst the weapons of the enemy, and showing no fear of her brother <note>Mithridates, who was her own brother, had now become her mortal enemy.</note>, she made her way to the house where she believed the body of her murdered boy was being kept. There, the pitiable woman offered her maternal tears and performed the final funeral rites. 

O good God! O the impregnable forces of nature and the strength of unconquered love! What greater or more wonderful thing could you have done? Whom all...

...impelled an honest breast to such great strength that she did not fear to undergo chains again if necessary, nor to be dragged into captivity, nor to enter a foul prison, and even to offer her neck to the executioner's axes. Where indeed will you find a sharper man, a more spirited leader, or a harsher commander against those who deserve ill? To whom could you go for a wiser or more daring woman, or a more watchful servant of matronly honor? 

This woman saw, with wonderful mental perspicacity, that it would be better to face certain death than to return to her husband's house with the uncertainty of disgrace. She believed that only through the greatest daring and danger could she testify that, although her body had been violated, her mind remained unsullied. Thus is feminine honor preserved; thus, once taken, it is recovered; thus is the chaste testimony of the heart returned. Therefore, let those who care for renowned modesty <term>pudicicia: the Roman virtue of feminine chastity and sexual integrity</term> observe this: it is not enough to testify to the sincerity of the heart with tears and complaints, saying one has suffered violence, unless one can proceed with extraordinary effort toward vengeance.

->## Concerning Tertia Aemilia, wife of the first Scipio Africanus.<-

**Tertia Aemilia**, although she was esteemed both for the fame of the Aemilian family (from which she drew her splendid origin) and for her marriage to the most serene man, Scipio the first Africanus <note>Scipio Africanus the Elder, the Roman general who defeated Hannibal.</note>, she nevertheless shone with a much brighter light through her own actions. 

For when Scipio—who as a younger man had once restored a virgin betrothed to the prince Lucceius <note>original: "luceio"; historically Allucius, a Celtiberian prince. Scipio famously returned his captive bride untouched.</note> in the first flower of her youth and remarkable beauty, along with the treasures offered by her parents for her ransom—had become an older man, he was unable to snatch himself away from the enticements of forbidden lust. Instead, he fell into an attraction for and cohabitation with his own maidservant. Since it is most difficult to deceive the watchful eye of an honest love, this could not remain hidden from Tertia. Indeed, in the course of time, she learned everything. And who could doubt that she bore it most bitterly? For some assert (putting aside all blushing) that nothing is more injurious or more intolerable to a bride...

# Concerning Oripetrua, Queen of Laodicea

We read that **Oripetrua** <note>original: "O Ripetruā"; this is a variant of the name Hypsicrateia, the sixth wife of King Mithridates VI of Pontus</note> was the Queen of Laodicea and the daughter of the great **Mithridates**. While the loyalty she showed to her household gods <note>original: "penatibus"; refers here to her familial duty and her father’s throne</note> made her commendable, in my judgment, something else made her even more memorable: an unheard-of physical trait she possessed from birth.

For (if faith is to be placed in the ancient books), she was born with a **double row of teeth**. This presented a monstrous spectacle to all the people of Asia in her time. Although she suffered no impediment in chewing from such an unusual quantity of teeth, she was nonetheless marked by this notable deformity. Yet, as has been mentioned, she pursued a path of laudable loyalty. When her father, Mithridates, was defeated by **Pompey the Great**, she followed him through every danger and hardship. By her faithful service, she testified that the failures or "crimes" of parents should not be held against their children <note>The author suggests her virtuous service redeemed her father’s reputation or proved her own worth despite his defeat</note>.

# Concerning Sempronia, sister of the Gracchi

**Sempronia** was the daughter of **Tiberius Sempronius Gracchus**, a man most renowned in his time. She was born to **Cornelia**, who was herself the daughter of the elder **Scipio Africanus**. Furthermore, she was the wife of the splendid **Scipio Aemilianus**, who later earned his surname for the destruction of **Carthage**.

She was also the sister of **Tiberius and Gaius Gracchus**, and in her greatness and constancy of spirit, she did not degenerate from her ancestors. It is said that after her brothers were killed due to their political seditions, she was dragged by a <term>Tribune of the Plebs</term> <note>A powerful Roman official elected to protect the interests of the common people</note> before a public trial. This was a situation of the greatest mental strain. There, with the crowd favoring the official, the Tribune pressured her to publicly kiss a man named **Equicius**. 

This Equicius, a man from Firmum in the region of Picenum, was pretending to be her nephew—the son of her deceased brother Tiberius Gracchus. She was being coerced to accept him into the **Sempronian family**. Even though she stood in a place where even great princes were accustomed to tremble, and though she was beset by the discordant shouts of an impetuous mob and threatened by the grim face of the Tribune's high authority, her **womanly constancy** was in no way broken. On the contrary, she remained mindful that her brother Tiberius had only three sons: one of whom had died as a young man while serving in the military in Sardinia, and the others [had likewise perished] <note>The text cuts off mid-explanation of the Gracchi genealogy</note>.

...brought it about that no matter what anxieties he was driven into, he felt as though he were being refreshed in the marriage chamber. O heart, the sanctuary of marital sweetness! When Mithridates saw this and found his son to be inexorable, judging it to be the final ruin of his affairs, he took poison. He also gave it to the rest of his wives, concubines, and daughters—and to Hypsicratea (who had provided so many helps for his life through her labor)—lest she should survive him. Indeed, the ungrateful act of Mithridates <note>The author considers it "ungrateful" that Mithridates forced her to die with him rather than allowing her to live.</note> could not diminish the well-deserved glory of Hypsicratea. Her mortal body was taken away by poison in an untimely death, but her name will live on through the monuments of venerable literature until our time and forever in celebrated fame; and it certainly cannot be cheated of a long posterity.

### Concerning Sempronia the Roman.

[Space for a large initial letter] We remember having read often that there was another Sempronia <note>Boccaccio distinguishes this Sempronia, a conspirator with Catiline, from the more virtuous Sempronia, sister of the Gracchi.</note> of celebrated talent. She was, therefore, of a talent so ready and so versatile that she immediately understood and carried out—even by imitation—whatever she had seen someone saying or doing at any time. Hence, since she had learned not only Latin but also Greek literature, she dared... and not in a womanly fashion <note>In the Roman context, a woman being "too" educated or bold was often seen as a transgression of social norms.</note>

While they stubbornly escaped slavery and shame by means of the noose <note>original: "laqueo"; referring to death by hanging.</note>, they demonstrated that their men had been overcome not by a lack of strength, but by the crime of Fortune. By casting aside those few years of life during which they might have survived their hanging, they sought instead the longest possible life for their reputation of purity. They left behind a cause of wonder for posterity: that so great a multitude of women—not through a formal assembly or public decree, but within the span of a single night—reached the same resolution of death, as if one and the same spirit had possessed them all.

# Concerning Porcia, daughter of Cato of Utica

Porcia was the daughter of that Marcus Cato <note>original: "Cathonis vticensis"; Cato the Younger (95–46 BCE), a Roman statesman and Stoic philosopher known for his moral integrity and his opposition to Julius Caesar.</note> who, after leading the remnants of Pompey’s army from Egypt through the burning deserts of Libya into Africa, killed himself at Utica, being unable to endure the victory of Caesar. Indeed, this distinguished woman did not seem to have degenerated in any way from her father's courage and perseverance. 

Moreover—to set aside other noble examples of her virtue—even while her father was still alive, she married Brutus <note>Marcus Junius Brutus, one of the primary assassins of Julius Caesar.</note> as his third wife, and she loved him with such integrity and purity that among the many concerns of other women, his well-being was by far her first and foremost priority. Nor could she hide the honorable flames of her love within her chaste breast at the critical moment. Since these actions have resulted in her eternal praise, they offer themselves freely to enhance her fame. 

The pestilential tumult of the civil wars had already quieted, with Caesar having everywhere crushed the followers of Pompey. However, when it was clearly perceived that Caesar was aiming for kingship as "perpetual dictator," the saner part of the Senate conspired against him; Brutus himself was among them. Because Brutus knew of Porcia’s integrity, the secret of this wicked deed <note>The plot to assassinate Julius Caesar.</note> was revealed to her. 

It happened on that night before the day dawned when Caesar was removed from human affairs by the help of the conspirators. As Brutus was leaving the bedroom, Porcia took a barber’s razor, pretending she was going to trim her excess nails, and intentionally wounded herself, though she made it appear that the razor had fallen by accident. Indeed, when her maidservants standing nearby saw the blood flow, they suspected something more serious and raised a cry. Brutus, who was leaving, was called back to the bedroom and scolded Porcia for usurping the barber’s duty. 

Porcia, however, having sent away the maidservants, said: "What you think was done recklessly was not so at all. I did it to test with what spirit I could kill myself with a blade and endure death, if your undertaking <note>The assassination attempt.</note> does not succeed according to your wishes." 

O, love of inexhaustible vigor! O, husband happy in such a life-partner! But the conspirators proceeded further into the crime; and once Caesar was killed, the killers escaped, though not with impunity. But since everything turned out contrary to expectation, and they were condemned as <term>patricides</term> <note>While "patricide" usually means killing a father, here it refers to the "Father of the Fatherland" (Pater Patriae), a title held by Caesar.</note> by the Senate, they fled in different directions. Brutus and Cassius, heading to the East, gathered no small forces against Octavian Caesar and Antony, Caesar’s heirs. Octavian and Antony led an army against them, and the battle was fought at Philippi.

<summary>This page describes the character and courage of Porcia, daughter of Cato, highlighting a famous incident where she self-inflicted a wound to prove her Stoic fortitude to her husband Brutus before the assassination of Julius Caesar.</summary>
<keywords>Porcia, Marcus Cato, Brutus, Julius Caesar, Stoicism, Roman Civil War, Assassination, Philippi, Roman Women</keywords>
