<meta>This page continues the narrative of famous Roman women, specifically focusing on the tragic end of Portia following the defeat of Brutus and Cassius, and beginning the account of Curia’s devotion to her husband during the Roman proscriptions.</meta>

...it was so. And when the parties of Cassius and Brutus were defeated and put to flight, and Brutus himself was also killed, Portia heard the news. Thinking that with her husband taken away no joy would remain for her, she judged that she would undergo death with no different spirit than when she once immediately applied the wound of a barber’s knife to her thigh <note>original: "cultri tonsorij vuln⁹". Portia famously stabbed herself in the thigh to prove to her husband, Brutus, that she was brave enough to keep the secret of the conspiracy against Julius Caesar.</note>. 

And when she saw that no instrument for a voluntary death was provided to her as quickly as her impulse demanded, she unhesitatingly swallowed glowing coals—which she happened to have nearby—having cast them into her throat with her hands. From these, as her vitals burned, her life force was forced to depart into death. There is no doubt that the more unusual the kind of death she brought upon herself, the more brilliance of marital devotion she bestowed upon those who encountered the story. Even the wound inflicted by her father’s hands <note>Portia was the daughter of Cato the Younger, a famous Roman statesman who also committed suicide for his principles.</note> could not take away any of the deserved praise for her bravery.

### Concerning Curia, the wife of Quintus Lucretius

<margin>[A metal clip is attached to the left margin of the page]</margin>
<margin>c</margin>

Curia was a Roman woman and, if we give credit to her name, she was from the lineage of the Curiones. She was a splendid example of ancient times, possessing wonderful constancy and the purest faith. For when, in the turmoil of affairs which occurred by the order of the <term>Triumvirs</term> <note>The Second Triumvirate of Octavian, Mark Antony, and Lepidus (43 BCE), who consolidated power by executing their political enemies.</note>, new <term>proscription</term> lists <note>A "proscription" was a public list of "enemies of the state" whose property was confiscated and who could be killed for a reward.</note> were posted against the lives of citizens, her husband Quintus Lucretius was found among those listed. 

While many others left their native soil in swift flight, seeking safety in wild caves and mountain solitudes, or finding hiding places among the enemies of the Roman name, he alone, by the advice and help of his most loving wife, hid fearlessly within the Roman walls. He remained within the walls of his domestic hearth and the secret of the marriage chamber, in the bosom of his wife. And he was saved by such skill from his wife, such sagacious industry, and such integrity of faith, that besides one little servant girl who was in on the secret, no one—even among his close friends—could have known or even suspected. 

How often, to artfully conceal the deed, we can believe this woman went out in public in worn-out clothing, a dirty dress, a mournful face, weeping eyes, and neglected hair. She wore none of the customary ornaments or veils, her breast was anxious with sighs, and she moved with a certain feigned stupor of madness. As if she were out of her mind, she ran about the streets, entered temples, and walked through the squares. And with a trembling and broken voice, while she seemed to honor the gods with prayers and vows, she asked friends she met if they had ever seen her Lucretius, or if they knew where he was...

<summary>This page recounts the dramatic suicide of Portia by swallowing hot coals and begins the story of Curia, who cleverly hid her husband Quintus Lucretius within Rome to save him from political execution.</summary>

<meta>Continuing the accounts of virtuous women from the Roman era, specifically those who showed extraordinary loyalty and intellect during the civil wars of the Second Triumvirate.</meta>

...whether he might live; and if he were living, where he had taken flight, with what companions, and with what hope? Furthermore, she <note>This refers to Curia, the wife of Quintus Lucretius Vespillo, though she is named only at the end of the paragraph</note> desired for herself to become a companion of his flight, his exile, and his miseries <note>original: "miseriarum"</note>. She acted in several ways as the unhappy are accustomed to do: by providing strong coverings for the man's hiding places. By what charms, what allurements, and what suggestions she had strengthened the mind of a little maidservant who was aware of the secret <note>original: "ancillule secreti conscie"</note> and made her act as a wife! By what consolations, finally, she raised his hope and encouraged the anxious heart of her trembling husband, and turned his sorrow into some kind of joy. And so, while the rest were suffering under the same plague <note>referring to the proscriptions—state-sanctioned murders—of the Triumvirs</note> and were miserably perishing among the mountain crags, the swells of the sea, the storms of the sky, the betrayals and hatreds of enemies, and the hands of those pursuing them, Lucretius alone was kept safe and secure in the lap of his most pious wife. By which most clever deed, Curia sought for herself a not-undeserved eternal fame.

### Concerning Hortensia, daughter of Quintus Hortensius

Hortensia, the daughter of the excellent orator Quintus Hortensius <note>Quintus Hortensius Hortalus was a contemporary and rival of Cicero, known for his "Asianic" style of oratory</note>, is to be extolled with worthy praises. She not only embraced the eloquence of her father Hortensius with a lively heart, but she also preserved the vigor of his public speaking which the opportunity demanded—a quality which very often is found lacking even in the most learned men. 

In the time of the Triumvirs <note>The rule of Octavian, Mark Antony, and Lepidus around 42 BCE</note>, when a multitude of matrons seemed burdened by a heavy and almost intolerable weight of money to be paid, necessitated by the needs of the Republic, not one of the men could be found who dared to provide patronage in such an unsuitable matter. She alone dared, with a constant mind, to take up the cause of the women before the Triumvirs. She pleaded the case so effectively with inexhaustible eloquence that, to the great admiration of the audience, it was believed that Hortensius had returned to life with his gender changed <note>original: "mutato sexu redivivus Hortensius"</note>. 

Nor was this extraordinary work, undertaken and executed by a woman, unsuccessful. For just as she had not failed in any part of her speech, whether in its structure or in the commendable demonstration of her rights, so too was the tax diminished by the Triumvirs according to her wish. Indeed, it was freely granted that a much larger part of the imposed money be removed. To the same extent that silence is seen as a praiseworthy thing for a woman in public under her matronly robe, so too should her eloquence be extolled when it is made beautiful by its own ornament as the opportunity demands. When this was finally done, not without the greatest brilliance for Hortensia, the remainder of what was required was easily obtained from the matrons. What more shall I say?

## Concerning Sulpitia, the wife of Lentulus Crustellio

**Sulpitia**, the wife of Lentulus Crustellio, earned for herself an indelible fame through a nearly equal measure of devotion. For this was the same Lentulus who was proscribed <term>Proscribed: Officially declared an enemy of the state, meaning his life was forfeit and his property was to be seized.</term> by the Triumvirs <term>The Triumvirs: The three-man political alliance of Octavian, Mark Antony, and Lepidus who ruled Rome and ordered the execution of their political rivals.</term> during the political storm mentioned above. 

When he had secured his safety by swift flight to Sicily and was living there as an exile, Sulpitia—having been informed of this and finding herself in a state of want—came to the resolution that she wished to endure his hardships together with her husband. She believed it was indecent for wives to share only the joyful hours and bright fortunes of their husbands, while shrinking from their troubles if they were forced into flight. 

Yet, it was not easy for Sulpitia to set out to her husband. Indeed, she was watched with the utmost diligence by her mother, **Julia**, specifically to prevent her from following her husband into exile. But what guards does true love not outwit? Therefore, having seized a favorable moment and dressed herself in the clothes of a slave <note>original: "servili sumpta veste"</note>, she deceived her mother and the guards and fled...

->### On Cornificia the Poet<-

**Cornificia**, whether she was Roman or rather from foreign lands, I do not recall. <note>The author, Giovanni Boccaccio, frequently uses this first-person "I" to admit where historical sources are thin.</note> However, by the testimony of the ancients, she was most worthy of being remembered. During the reign of **Octavian Caesar** <note>Augustus, the first Roman Emperor</note>, she shone with such great poetic skill that she seemed to have been nourished not by Italian milk, but by the **Castalian spring** <note>original: "castalio"; a spring on Mount Parnassus sacred to the Muses and a metaphor for poetic inspiration</note>. She was the sister of **Cornificius**, a man of the same name and an eminent poet himself, in whose glory she was equally illustrious. Not content with having possessed such a splendid faculty for words, and with the **Muses** <note>The Greek goddesses of inspiration</note> urging her to descend to the songs of **Helicon** <note>The mountain home of the Muses</note>, she very often applied her learned hands to the pen, having **cast aside the distaff** <note>original: "colo reiecta"; the distaff was the symbol of traditional female domestic labor like spinning wool</note>. With her pen and her own wit, she wrote epigrams which, in the times of the most holy and illustrious man **Jerome** <note>Saint Jerome, the 4th-century scholar who translated the Bible into Latin</note> (as he himself testifies), were still highly esteemed. Whether they have survived into later ages, I am not entirely certain.

O, feminine glory! To have neglected womanly trifles and applied her genius to the studies of the great prophets. Let the sluggish be ashamed, those who miserably distrust themselves and persuade themselves that they were born only for leisure and the marriage bed—as if they were not capable of anything more than receiving the embraces of men and bearing and nursing children. For all things that make men glorious are held in common with women, if only they are willing to labor in study. This woman, being born with strength of mind and through her vigils, was able to surpass the female sex and seek an eternal name for herself through honorable labor—a name not without glory, but one which remains very rare and excellent even among a few men.

->### On Mariamne, Queen of the Jews<-

**Mariamne**, a Hebrew woman more fortunate in her lineage than in her husband, was the daughter of **Alexander** <note>original: "Alexandria"; likely a transcription error for Alexander, son of King Aristobulus II</note> and the daughter of King **Hyrcanus**. She was famous for such great and unseen beauty that she was believed not only to surpass all other women of that time in loveliness, but was judged to be a **heavenly image** rather than a mortal one. Testimony for this belief was not lacking from **Mark Antony**, the **Triumvir** <note>One of the three rulers of Rome alongside Octavian and Lepidus</note>. Now, Mariamne had a brother from the same parents named **Aristobulus**, who was equal to her in beauty and age. When his mother, **Alexandra**, desired that the high priesthood be given to him...

<meta>This page continues the historical account of the Ptolemaic dynasty in Egypt, specifically focusing on the transition of power from Ptolemy XII Auletes to his children, leading into the reign of Cleopatra VII and her interactions with Julius Caesar.</meta>

Let us take our beginning from Dionysius, also called Mineus <note>original: "dyonisius seu mineus" — likely referring to Ptolemy XII Auletes</note>, a very great friend of the Roman people. As he approached death during the first consulship of Julius Caesar, he left behind signed wills <note>original: "tabulis" — refers to wax tablets used for legal documents</note> decreeing that his eldest son (whom some believe was named Lpsania <note>A potential scribal error or variant for Ptolemy XIII</note>) and his eldest daughter, Cleopatra, should reign after his death as husband and wife. This was carried out because the custom of marrying sisters was very familiar among the Egyptians, though mothers were excluded from such marriages.

Furthermore, as Cleopatra was burning with a lust for power—or so it seemed to some—she did away with that innocent youth, her own brother and husband, who was then in his fifteenth year, by means of poison. Thus, she gained possession of the kingdom alone. From this point, they assert that when Pompey the Great had occupied nearly all of Asia with his armies and was heading toward Egypt, he substituted a surviving boy for the dead brother and made him King of Egypt. Because of this, the indignant Cleopatra took up arms against him. 

And so, with affairs in this state, after Pompey was defeated by Caesar in Thessaly <note>The Battle of Pharsalus, 48 BCE</note> and the boy king he had established was slain on the Egyptian shore, Caesar arrived after him and found the factions there waging war among themselves. When Caesar ordered them to be summoned before him to plead their case—so that we might know the fate of the young Ptolemy—the woman Cleopatra, unbridled in her malice, trusting first in herself, approached with the royal insignia. She envisioned a kingdom for herself if she could join the conqueror of the world in her own <term>lasciviousness</term> <note>refers to sexual intimacy or seduction used as a political tool</note>. Since she was most beautiful and, through the art of her sparkling eyes and the eloquence of her speech, could capture almost anyone she wished, with little effort she drew the lustful prince into her company. 

She spent several nights with him in the midst of the <term>Alexandrian tumult</term> <note>The Siege of Alexandria, 48–47 BCE</note>, and she conceived (as almost everyone agrees) a son whom she afterwards named Caesarion <note>The "Little Caesar," the only biological son of Julius Caesar</note> after his father's name. Finally, when the boy Ptolemy, having been released by Caesar, turned his wars against his own liberator at the urging of his followers, he encountered Mithridates of Pergamum, who was coming with an army to Caesar's aid. There, having been overcome by Caesar (who had reached the location by another route), the boy attempted flight in a boat. However, being weighed down by the press of many men rushing aboard, he was drowned. 

And so, with affairs settled and the surrender of the Alexandrians completed, as Caesar was about to set out against Pharnaces, the King of Pontus <note>Pharnaces II, who Caesar famously defeated with the phrase "Veni, vidi, vici"</note>—who had been, as it were, a reward for his nights with Cleopatra—the kingdom of Egypt fell to her. She desired nothing else, and because she had remained faithful, she received it. Her sister Arsinoe was led away, lest perhaps under her leadership some new plot might be attempted against her. Thus, having already obtained the kingdom through a double crime, Cleopatra...

### Concerning Antonia, daughter of Antony.

Antonia the Younger left behind for posterity an indelible example of most illustrious widowhood. She was also, it is believed, the daughter of the Triumvir Mark Antony by Octavia <note>Octavia was the sister of Augustus</note>. For that reason she was nicknamed "the Younger," because she had an older sister of the same name. Indeed, she married Drusus, the brother of Tiberius Nero and the stepson of Octavian Augustus. By him, she gave birth to Germanicus, to Claudius (who was later Emperor), and to Livilla.

While Drusus was engaged in the Germanic expedition, he perished by poison (as some believe) through the help of his brother Tiberius. After his death, although she was flourishing in the flower of her age and was of conspicuous beauty, Antonia decided that she had married honorably enough for a woman to have done so once; no one could provoke her to a second marriage. On the contrary, she spent the remaining space of her life under her mother-in-law Livia <note>Empress Livia, wife of Augustus</note>, within the limits of her husband's bedchamber, so chastely and so holily that she surpassed the praises of all past matrons by her celebrated widowhood.

Truly, among the likes of the Cincinnatuses, the Fabriciuses, the Curiuses, the Lucretiuses, and the Sulpiciuses <note>Names of famous families from the early Roman Republic known for their strict morality</note>, it is most holy and most splendid for even elderly women and the daughters of the Catos to have led a life without the stain of wantonness, and they should be extolled with much praise.

If this is so, with what proclamations shall we honor this young woman? She was distinguished by beauty and was the daughter of Mark Antony—that most foul man—yet she lived not in forests or solitudes, but amidst imperial leisure and delights. She lived among Julia, the daughter of Octavia, and Julia, the daughter of Marcus Agrippa <note>Both women were infamous in Rome for their scandalous affairs</note>—those most fervent fires of lust and wantonness. She lived amidst the obscenities and disgraces of her father Mark Antony and later of the Emperor Tiberius; in a fatherland once frugal but now given over to every foulness; and amidst a thousand examples of concupiscence. Yet, with a constant mind and strong heart, she preserved her chastity—not for a brief time or in hope of a future marriage, but in obedience to virtue, even unto old age and death.

And by Pollux! <note>original: "Et pol", a common Roman oath</note> nothing truly worthy enough is left to words; perhaps something remains for contemplation. Because this indeed exceeds the powers of writers, let it be enough to have left her merits to be considered and extolled by the contemplation of sacred minds.

<summary>This page concludes the account of Cleopatra's burial and begins a biography of Antonia the Younger, praising her for maintaining her chastity and widowhood despite the corruption of the Roman imperial court.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the biographies of famous women, transitioning to the Roman era with the story of Agrippina the Elder. It follows the common medieval and Renaissance tradition of focusing on the moral character and tragic ends of Roman noblewomen.</meta>

# Concerning Agrippina, the wife of Germanicus

<margin>Agrippina was born to Marcus Agrippa and Julia, the daughter of Octavian Caesar.</margin>

Even though Gaius Caligula—the son of this same Agrippina and later the ruler of the world—despised the low-born status of his maternal grandfather Agrippa, he used to claim that his mother was not fathered by Agrippa at all. Instead, he claimed she was the result of an act of debauchery <note>original: "stupro"</note> committed by Octavian upon his own daughter, Julia. He foolishly wished it to be believed that he was of nobler birth, having been conceived by an incestuous mother, rather than being fathered by a low-born man in a legal marriage.

But regardless of whose daughter she was, she was married to Germanicus, an outstanding young man of his age and a great asset to the Republic, who was also the adoptive son of Tiberius Caesar Augustus. She was illustrious enough for this marriage, but she became even more illustrious because she resisted the treachery of a most insolent prince <note>Referring to the Emperor Tiberius, who was her father-in-law and later her persecutor.</note> with an unwavering resolve. She had already given birth to three sons by her husband Germanicus—including the Gaius Caligula who later ruled the Romans—and as many daughters, one of whom was the Agrippina who became the mother of the Emperor Nero. 

When she mourned the death of her most famous husband—who was widely believed to have been removed by poison with the help of his father, Tiberius—and lamented his murder with a great display of feminine grief, she fell into such favor-less hatred with Tiberius. He would grab her by the arm and harass her with rebukes until she wept, as she could not bear the fact that she was prevented from holding power. After being provoked by many accusations that arose in the Emperor's mind, he ordered her, though innocent, to be kept under guard.

Furthermore, this remarkable woman, considering the treatment she received from Caesar to be beneath her dignity, decided to escape or end the disdain of the spiteful prince through death. Since no other convenient means were available, she determined with a noble spirit to summon death through hunger and immediately began to abstain from all food. When this was reported to Tiberius, and that heartless man <note>original: "ignarus homo" refers here to his lack of empathy or moral sense regarding her suffering.</note> realized where the woman's fasting was leading, he feared she would escape his abuses by such a certain path in so short a time.

When threats and beatings proved useless in making her eat, he went so far—simply so he would not be deprived of someone to torment—as to order food to be violently forced down her throat. He intended that, by whatever means it was shoved into her stomach, it would provide nourishment against her will. Agrippina, however, became all the more determined in her purpose the more she was provoked by these injuries. Persevering in what she had begun, she triumphed over the wicked prince's insolence by dying. She proved that while he could easily kill many people whenever he wished, he could not, with all the power of his empire, save a single person who was determined to die.

<summary>This page recounts the life of Agrippina the Elder, highlighting her noble marriage to Germanicus and her defiant suicide by starvation to escape the tyranny and forced feeding of the Emperor Tiberius.</summary>

...could not. Although through this death Agrippina gained a great deal of favor among her own people, she left behind far more disgrace for Tiberius <note>Tiberius was the second Roman Emperor; Agrippina the Elder, his rival's widow, died of starvation while in his custody, which the public saw as a cruel martyrdom.</note>.

## Concerning Paulina, a Roman woman.

Paulina, a certain Roman woman, achieved an enduring name for herself through her ridiculous simplicity <note>The author uses "simplicity" here to mean a naive gullibility that led to her being easily deceived.</note>. While Tiberius Caesar Augustus was ruling the Romans, she was considered remarkable; as a married woman, she was regarded by everyone as a model of illustrious chastity. She cared for nothing else besides devotion to her husband, except that she might be able to serve Anubis <note>The jackal-headed god of the Egyptian pantheon, whose cult was popular in Rome at this time despite official disapproval.</note>, the god of the Egyptians, whom she worshipped with total devotion so that she might earn his favor.

Truly, since beautiful women are everywhere loved by young men with hope—and especially those women who possess a careful reputation for chastity—a certain young man of great beauty named Mundus <note>original: "Mundus"; his name means "World" or "Pure" in Latin, which may be a play on his character.</note> [pursued] her intensely, now with glances and gestures, now with jokes, and now with pleas...

<summary>This page concludes the tragic account of Agrippina the Elder and begins the story of Paulina, a Roman noblewoman whose extreme religious devotion to the god Anubis made her vulnerable to deception.</summary>
<keywords>Agrippina the Elder, Tiberius, Paulina, Anubis, Mundus, Roman chastity, Egyptian religion, naivety</keywords>

--- Page 137 ---
<meta>This page concludes the story of Paulina’s deception by Decius Mundus—a famous scandal from the reign of Tiberius—and begins the biography of Agrippina the Younger, mother of the emperor Nero.</meta>

...had descended and come into her embrace, so that a god similar to himself might be born from him and her. Above all other things, she asked the "god," her lover, whether the heavenly beings were able or were accustomed to mingle with mortals. To this, Mundus <note>Decius Mundus was a Roman knight who had fallen in love with Paulina, a noblewoman of high reputation.</note> immediately replied that they could. He then entered through the barriers <note>original: "per regulas"; referring to the gates or restricted areas of the Temple of Isis.</note> and was later received into heaven <note>Mundus is pretending that his departure is an ascension back to the divine realm.</note>. Having heard these things, Paulina joyfully agreed to the request. Mundus entered the bed stripped of his Anubis disguise <note>Anubis was the jackal-headed Egyptian god; Mundus had bribed the priests to let him impersonate the deity.</note> and enjoyed the desired embrace and union.

But when night was already turning into day, as he left at dawn, he told her that she had conceived a son. In the morning, after the bed had been removed from the temple by the priests, Paulina told her husband what had happened. The foolish man believed it and rejoiced that his wife was about to give birth to a god. Nor is there any doubt that the time of birth would have been expected by both of them, had not the young Mundus, burning with too much passion, revealed the trick with too little caution. 

He, perhaps conscious that she had come eagerly into his embrace and union, thought that if he showed her that her modesty had been overcome by his own cleverness, she would be more flexible and equally eager for another night; and thus, by an easier path, he could return again and more often to the desired embraces. He met Paulina as she was going to the temple and said in a low voice: "You are blessed, Paulina, since you have received a child from me as the god Anubis." 

But the result of his words was far different than he expected. For when Paulina was stunned, and recalled in her mind many of the things she had heard and experienced, she immediately sensed the fraud. Disturbed, she returned to her husband and revealed to him the trick of Mundus and the priests as she understood it. It followed from this that the husband complained to **Tiberius** <note>Tiberius was the Roman Emperor from 14 to 37 AD.</note>. Once the fraud was discovered, the Emperor arranged for the priests to be executed, Mundus to be punished with exile, and the mocked Paulina became a cautionary tale <note>original: "fabulam"; a story or fable</note> among the Roman commoners. She was made more famous by her own simplicity and Mundus’s trickery than by her devotion to Anubis or her chastity, which she had tried so skillfully to preserve.

### About Agrippina, mother of Nero Caesar.

**Agrippina**, the mother of Nero Caesar, was distinguished by her wicked lineage and the monstrosity of her son and herself, no less than by her notorious deeds. Indeed, she was the daughter of **Germanicus Caesar** <note>Germanicus was a beloved Roman general and the intended heir of Tiberius before his early death.</note>—a young man of the finest and most praiseworthy character—and the elder Agrippina; she was named **Julia Agrippina** and was the sister of the prince **Gaius Caligula**. She married **Gnaeus Domitius**, a man from the Ahenobarbus family who was most disdainful and harsh.

<meta>This page continues the biography of Agrippina the Younger, focusing on her transition from the reign of her brother Caligula to her marriage to her uncle, the Emperor Claudius.</meta>

From her, she brought forth Nero—a beast <note>original: "beluam"</note> famous to the whole world—who was birthed feet-first from his mother’s womb. However, after Domitius <note>Gnaeus Domitius Ahenobarbus, Nero’s father</note> died in the interim and Nero was taken up while still a small child, her brother Gaius <note>The Emperor Caligula</note>—a most filthy man—abused her with shameful debauchery because she was so very beautiful. 

Later, having become embittered <note>original: "limatus," perhaps meaning provoked or sharpened in her resolve</note> against the prince, or perhaps because he did not approve of her conduct—either because she had involved herself with Lepidus in the hope of seizing power, or by the instigation of some rival—he deprived her of almost all her possessions and banished her to an island. When he was finally slaughtered by his own soldiers and Claudius was substituted in his place, she was recalled by him. 

In the course of time, when she heard that Valeria Messalina <note>Claudius's third wife, executed for conspiracy and adultery</note> had died through various deserving causes, she immediately entered into the single hope of securing the world for herself and her son. She drew the widowed prince toward a desire for marriage with her—even though he was the brother of her father, Germanicus—using the grace of her own beauty and the help of the freedman Pallas. This was done in opposition to Lollia Paulina (who was aided by the freedman Callistus) and Aelia Paetina (who was favored by the efforts of Narcissus). 

But morality seemed to stand entirely in the way, because she was his niece and the daughter of his brother. However, by a speech from a suborned Vitellius, it was brought about that the prince was compelled toward his own desire by the prayers of the senators. At Vitellius's pleading, a decree was made by the Senate that uncles were permitted to marry their nieces. And so, with Claudius willing and the Senate entreating, Agrippina entered into marriage with him. She was eventually titled "Augusta" and was carried to the Capitol in a <term>carpento</term> <note>A two-wheeled ceremonial carriage, a high honor usually reserved for priests and sacred objects</note>, a privilege previously granted only to the priesthood. She then began to rage against those who opposed her with various punishments. Indeed, she was a most cunning woman of the time...

<meta>This page continues the narrative of Nero’s reign, specifically detailing the execution of his mother, Agrippina, and the emergence of the Pisonian conspiracy.</meta>

...in which Agrippina, having been received and unaware of the trickery, might be put in danger. Agrippina, however, with a wounded shoulder, was finally cast into the sea; she was rescued by the aid of those on the shore and brought to the Lucrine Lake and her own villa. 

From there, by her order, it was announced to Nero by the freedman Agermus that she had escaped. Nero ordered the man to be detained as if he had come to plot against the Emperor’s safety. Then Anicetus, Herculeius the trierarch <term>trierarch: a commander of a galley or ship</term>, and Obaritus, a centurion of the fleet, were sent to finish her off. When the house had been surrounded by Anicetus, and the little maidservant—Agrippina's only companion—had fled, the executioners entered. First, Herculeius struck her head with a club. Then, when she saw the centurion drawing his sword for her death, she stretched out her womb and cried out that they should strike her belly <note>This famous gesture was meant to emphasize that she was being killed by the very son she had carried</note>. Thus she was killed and cremated that same night. She was covered only by a lowly mound of earth, though finally a light tomb was placed for her on the road near Misenum and the villa of Julius Caesar by those who attended her. Others claim that she was viewed by Nero after her death, and that he condemned some of her limbs while praising others, before she was finally buried.

->### Concerning Epicharis, the freedwoman.<-

Epicharis is believed to have been a foreign woman rather than a Roman. Nor was she distinguished by any brilliance of lineage, being the daughter of a freedman and a freedwoman herself. And what is far more shameful, she delighted in no honorable arts. However, toward the end of her life, she revealed that she possessed a noble spirit with manly strength. Indeed, as the insolence and debaucheries of Nero, the prince of the Romans, grew among all the Romans and Italians, matters reached such a state that several senators and other citizens conspired against him under the leadership of Gaius Piso. While they were attempting to bring the matter to a conclusion through various discussions, I know not by what means the entire plan and the names of the conspirators came to the knowledge of Epicharis. But because the work was being drawn out too long in her judgment, and being affected by weariness, she withdrew into Campania...

<meta>This page continues a moralizing discussion on the virtues of widowhood and the sanctity of marriage, likely contrasting a virtuous woman from the previous page with the "modern" women the author now criticizes. The text transitions into the biography of Poppaea Sabina.</meta>

...honestly (if she had been able) she would have preferred to die with her aged husband, rather than to preserve her life—as most women do—by entering into second marriages, which should not be done without a sense of shame.

Indeed, to the greatest disgrace of <term>matronly modesty<note>Latin: "matronalis pudicitiae." In the Roman world, pudicitia was the ideal of feminine virtue and sexual fidelity.</note></term>, there are some in these days for whom—I will not say a second or third marriage (which is common to almost everyone)—but a sixth, seventh, or eighth marriage (if the opportunity arises) is so familiar. They carry the <term>nuptial torches<note>In traditional Roman weddings, torches were carried in a procession to the groom's house to signify the start of the marriage.</note></term> into the bedrooms of new husbands so frequently that they seem to have snatched the habit of death from <term>common harlots<note>Latin: "meretriculas." The author is using a derogatory comparison to suggest that women who marry many times are no better than prostitutes who change partners nightly.</note></term>, whose custom it is to change bedfellows very often throughout the night. 

Nor do they enter into these frequently repeated marriage rites with any different expression than if they were performing the most holy service of honor. Indeed, it is not quite certain whether such women should be said to emerge from a <term>brothel cell<note>Latin: "lupanaris cellula."</note></term> or from the bedchamber of a deceased husband. Nor should I doubt that one must suspect either a burning dishonor in the woman entering the room, or a great foolishness in the man introducing her there. 

Alas! Our morals have deservedly collapsed from those the ancients used to keep. For them, in the holiness of their souls, it was previously considered shameful to have entered not just a seventh marriage, but even a second <note>original: "secundas misse nuptias"</note>. Nor could such women thereafter be rightfully mixed with honorable matrons. 

Today’s women are far different. For, keeping silent about their own lustful itch, they consider themselves more beautiful and dearer to others because, through frequent weddings, they have overcome the "misfortune" of widowhood and have so often pleased various husbands.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Triaria, the wife of Lucius Vitellius (previously mentioned as a woman of fierce and perhaps excessive character), before transitioning to the life of Faltonia Betitia Proba, a celebrated Roman Christian poet.</meta>

...omitting no extreme of military severity, she rushed upon the wretched. Indeed, it is reported that she acted with excessive cruelty and arrogance toward the enemy. There is no fear in them, provided the husband’s glory is extolled; there is no memory of piety, no shame for the female sex, and no consideration for the nature of the times. Triaria was able to undergo all things with easy labor for her husband’s honor—she who could strike horror not only into women <note>The author here reflects a common medieval/Renaissance stereotype that women were naturally timid.</note> (who, as is the custom, are often terrified even by the daily murmur of walls <note>original: "diurno muris murmure"</note> in the arms of their husbands) but even into robust and warlike young men. And if this woman threw herself into these nocturnal arms with such force, who will believe she was notable only for this deed, since it is not customary (whether they be rare or frequent) for only the sun-lit virtues to enter the hearts of mortals? Indeed, I believe that although they have been removed from memory, Triaria was remarkable for many other merits.

-># Concerning Proba, the wife of Adelphus<-

**Proba**, both by her deeds and her name <note>The name "Proba" in Latin means "upright," "virtuous," or "worthy."</note>, was a woman most worthy of remembrance in the history of letters. Although her noble lineage and origin are unknown to some, I believe by conjecture that she was Roman. Other most distinguished men assert that she originated from a certain town and was the wife of a man named **Adelphus**, and of the **Christian** religion. It can be clearly perceived that, under whatever teacher it occurred, she was highly proficient in the liberal arts. 

Indeed, among her other studies, she became so learned and familiar with the poetry of **Virgil** <note>The Roman epic poet Publius Vergilius Maro.</note> through such watchful care that she seemed to have his work almost entirely within her sight and memory. While perhaps reading with a more sharp-sighted attentiveness, it occurred to her that the entire history of the **Old Testament** and the sequence of the **New** could be described using Virgil's verse in a calm, ready, and substantial manner. Truly, it does not lack for wonder that such a sublime concept entered a woman’s brain; but it was far more wonderful to have carried it out to execution. 

Therefore, applying herself to this pious undertaking, running now here and now there through the verses of the *Bucolics*, the *Georgics*, and the *Aeneid* <note>The three major works of Virgil: poems on pastoral life, farming, and the founding of Rome.</note>, sometimes plucking whole verses from this part and sometimes fragments of meters from that, she reduced them to her own purpose with marvelous craftsmanship. She arranged the whole lines and connected the fragments so aptly—preserving the laws of the <term>feet</term> <note>Metrical "feet" are the rhythmic units of classical poetry; she maintained the strict dactylic hexameter of the original lines while changing their meaning.</note> and the dignity of the poem—that no one, however much of a critic, could find fault with them. And starting from the beginning of the world...

<summary>This page concludes a description of the fierce Triaria and introduces Proba, a Roman Christian poet famous for creating a "Cento" (a poem composed of fragments of Virgil's work) that retells the Bible.</summary>
<keywords>Triaria, Lucius Vitellius, Proba, Adelphus, Virgil, Cento, Christian poetry, Old Testament, New Testament, Liberal Arts</keywords>

--- Page 147 ---
<meta>Continuing the biography of the Roman poet Proba, likely from Giovanni Boccaccio’s "On Famous Women." This section discusses her mastery of the "Cento" technique and her use of Virgil to tell Christian stories.</meta>

Making a beginning of what history is read in the Old and New Testaments, she composed the work so aptly that a person unaware of how it was put together might easily believe that Virgil had been both a prophet and an evangelist. From this, it is no less commendable to note that this woman possessed a complete knowledge of the sacred volumes and historical records <note>The Latin 'fastis' refers to chronological records or calendars of events.</note>, something which we sadly know happens only to rare men in our own age. 

This excellent woman moreover wished for the work composed by her labor to be called a **Cento** <term>Cento: derived from the Latin for 'patchwork,' this is a poetic technique where a new poem is created by sewing together lines or fragments from existing works—in this case, the works of Virgil.</term>, which we ourselves have often seen. And the more we think it worthy of careful memory, the less we believe the talent of such a famous woman rested only on this one labor. Indeed, I believe that if her life had been extended for more years, she would have composed other praiseworthy things which, through the neglect of librarians, have not reached us in our own time. 

Among these (though it is not certain) was a Homeric Cento, edited with the same art and from the same material as she had taken from Virgil, but using verses taken from Homer. From this (if it is so), we may conclude with even greater praise that she knew Greek literature as most learnedly as she did Latin. I ask now: what could be more desirable than to have heard a woman scanning the songs of Maro <note>Publius Vergilius Maro, the Roman poet Virgil.</note> and Homer, setting aside selected parts for her work and weaving them together with a skillful arrangement? 

Let most learned men look on, for whom the distinguished profession of sacred letters is nevertheless arduous and difficult: to draw out parts from the vast heart of the sacred volume, now from here and now from there, and to force them into a single sequence of the life of Christ with scattered words and patches, just as she did from a pagan poem. 

For her (if we consider feminine customs), the distaff and the needle and the weaving-loom would have been enough, if she had wished to be idle like so many others. But because she was diligent in sacred studies, she wiped away all the rust of laziness from her spirit and escaped into eternal light. I wish those who are slaves to pleasures and idleness would fear this with a good heart—those for whom it is a great thing to sit in their bedrooms, wasting irrecoverable time on frivolous tales, and often spending the whole day from early morning until night babbling in speeches that are either harmful or empty, or devoting themselves only to wantonness. By God, they should notice how great a difference there is between seeking fame through praiseworthy works and burying one's name together with one's corpse, departing from life as if they had never lived.

<summary>This page describes the literary achievements of Proba, a Roman poet who cleverly repurposed the verses of Virgil and Homer to tell the story of the Bible. The author praises her immense learning and contrasts her intellectual diligence with the perceived idleness of other women of the era.</summary>
<keywords>Proba, Cento, Virgil, Homer, Christian poetry, female education, literary labor, patchwork poetry</keywords>

# ->Of Faustina Augusta<-

**F**austina Augusta <note>This is Faustina the Younger (c. 130–175 AD), daughter of Antoninus Pius and wife of Marcus Aurelius.</note> (who was also afterwards enrolled among the **deified** <note>original: "inter divos"; referring to the Roman practice of consecrating deceased emperors and empresses as gods.</note>) achieved a great deal of glory during both her life and her death, though she followed her husband’s kindness more than her own merit. She was the daughter of **Antoninus Pius**, the Caesar Augustus, by his wife Faustina <note>Faustina the Elder.</note>, and was joined in marriage to **Marcus Antoninus Pius** <note>Marcus Aurelius, who took the name Antoninus upon his accession.</note> after he had been adopted as a son. Upon the death of his father, she ruled together with her husband and was called **Augusta** by a decree of the Senate. This was no small glory for a woman at that time; for although the title of "Augusta" had been granted to the wives of emperors by their husbands in the past, I find that to no one before her had it been granted by a formal **decree of the Senate**.

Furthermore, she was of such exquisite beauty that it was believed something divine was mixed with her mortality. Lest this beauty be consumed by old age or death, it was arranged that her image be sculpted on gold, silver, and bronze coins—both as a young girl and in her more mature age—and these endure even to this day. In these coins, although the expression of the mouth, the movement of the eyes, the vivid color, and the cheerfulness of the face are lacking, the facial features nevertheless bear witness to her beauty in the highest degree.

Truly, as much as she was celebrated by the fame of the whole world, she was equally stained by the mark of shameful **unchastity**. Indeed, it was believed that she was not content with only one lover besides her husband, but rather that she came into the embraces of many. Among these, the names of certain men are preserved by infamy: for a certain old man was considered among her adulterers, as was **Orphicus**, and after him, **Moderatius**. But the one who surpassed the rest was named **Tertullus** <note>The OCR reads "terculus." Historically, this refers to the lover Tertullus mentioned in the *Historia Augusta*.</note>, whom they even say was seen by Antoninus <note>Marcus Aurelius.</note> dining with her. Added to these was **Marcus Verus**, despite the fact that he was her son-in-law by her daughter **Lucilla**.

And (what is more shameful than all these things), they say she once loved a certain **gladiator** so much that on account of her longing for him, she fell into a nearly fatal illness. In her desire for health, she revealed her lust to Antoninus. He, following the advice of a physician to soothe the passion of the ailing woman, had the gladiator killed and then had the entire body of the sick woman smeared with his **fresh blood**. And thus he freed his wife from the impetuous heat of love and even from the disease.

But wise men believed this remedy was a fiction, since in the course of time, **Commodus Antoninus**—conceived at that time—provided testimony to the truth by his wicked deeds. For he was believed to be the son of a gladiator rather than of Antoninus, as he was better suited to the habits of a gladiator than the Emperor. While these things were ringing out to the shame of Faustina...

# Of Joan the English Pope

Joan, though appearing to be a man by name, was nonetheless female in sex. Her hidden audacity <note>original: "temeritas"</note> was such that she became known to the entire world and ensured that she would be recognized by posterity. Although some say her homeland was Mainz <note>original: "maguntinam"</note>, her true name is scarcely known. Let there be those who say she was called Giliberta. 

This much is established by the claims of some: that as a young girl, she was loved by a young scholar. They say she loved him so much that—casting aside her maidenly modesty and womanly fear—she fled her father’s house in secret. Changing her name and her dress <term>habit: the distinctive clothing of a monk, cleric, or student</term>, she followed the young man she loved. 

While he was a student in England <note>While many versions of the Pope Joan legend place her studies in Athens, this specific manuscript tradition identifies England as the location</note>, she was thought by everyone there to be a male cleric. There, she devoted herself both to "Venus" <note>A classical reference to romantic love and physical intimacy</note> and to the study of letters. Later, after the young man was taken by death, she realized she possessed great talent and was drawn by the sweetness of knowledge. Keeping her male clothing, she neither wished to join herself to another man nor to reveal herself as a woman. Instead, applying herself to her studies with great vigilance, she became so [proficient] in the liberal arts and sac[red]... <note>The text breaks off here; it likely intended to say "sacred scriptures"</note>

To show detestation for her filthiness and to strike her name from memory, to this very day the High Pontiffs <note>The Popes.</note>, when performing holy litanies <note>Processional prayers.</note> with the clergy and people, abhor that spot in the middle of their route. Avoiding it, they take a detour through side streets; and having bypassed this detestable place, they complete the journey they began.

<meta>This page continues the narrative of the Byzantine Empress Irene and her son Constantine VI, detailing the brutal internal politics of the 8th-century Eastern Roman Empire and the eventual transition of power. It then begins a new biographical entry for a local figure from Ravenna named Enguldruda.</meta>

...tore out the tongues of the brothers **Nicephorus** and **Christopher**. <note>original: "nycepharo & xpoforo fratribus"</note> This man <note>referring to Emperor Constantine VI</note> blinded **Alexios**, the patrician of Armenia, and forced his wife **Maria** to take the monastic habit <note>The "monastic habit" refers to the clothing and lifestyle of a nun, a common way to forcibly retire political rivals or unwanted spouses</note>. In her place, he brought in **Theodote**, a chambermaid, whom he immediately crowned. Because of these atrocities, the woman **Irene**, who was hidden away and had been forced by circumstance to set aside her power, nevertheless preserved her noble spirit in the hope of reclaiming the imperial rule. By bestowing gold profusely upon the nobles and using the abundant treasures she had hidden in the palace where she lived in seclusion, she made the minds of the leading men favorable to her.

And when she had won them over to her view with ample gifts, she arranged for them to capture her son **Constantine** and deprive him of his eyes. <note>Blinding was a common Byzantine practice to render a person ineligible for the throne without necessarily killing them.</note> Thus, this spirited woman retook the empire that had once been taken from her. Constantine, however, died after being seized by disease. Finally, when she had ruled for another five years, she was besieged in the **Palace of Eleutherios** <note>A palace in Constantinople built by Irene</note> by the rebelling **Nicephorus**. 

This Nicephorus, having received the imperial crown from **Tarasios**, the Patriarch of Constantinople—with the support of the legion and the patricians **Trophilos** and **Staurakios** the steward (who had both been recently enriched by Irene)—acted with humility to enter Irene’s presence and used flatteries to deceive her. She, noticing nothing, asked for no more than the palace in which she resided; she promised that once her petitions were granted, she would open all the treasures to him. Once he obtained these, the wicked man broke his word and exiled her to the island of **Lesbos**. There, the famous woman ended her life in old age. 

Others, however, seem to hold a different view of her end. They say that while the mother and son were in discord and alternately depriving each other of the empire, the Romans revolted from them and transferred the empire to **Charlemagne**, then King of the Franks. <note>original: "karoluz magnū francoorum"</note> They say he attempted to reunite the empire, which appeared divided, through a permanent marriage alliance, and that Irene had agreed to this. When the patrician **Aetius** <note>original: "euticius"</note> heard of this, he immediately elevated **Nicephorus**, forced Irene to give up the empire and enter a monastery, and there she eventually grew old.

When the town had finally surrendered, having been driven about by storms during long wanderings at sea, the men were dragged to Naples laden with chains and kept in prisons. Among these captives was Roland, the son of King Frederick <note>King Frederick III of Sicily (r. 1296–1337)</note> born of a concubine <note>original: "ex cubina," referring to an illegitimate or "natural" son</note>, a young man of striking appearance and great physical prowess. While the ransom for the other prisoners was being sought, he alone remained sadly among the captives, while the others departed fearlessly once their own ransoms were paid. 

For King Peter <note>Peter II of Sicily, Roland's half-brother</note>, who was responsible for his brother’s affairs, held both Roland and all the others who had been in that naval battle in utter loathing; this was because the engagement had been poorly handled and fought against his express commands. <meta>This page continues the speech of Canniola, a noble widow, as she confronts Roland (Rolandus), the man she rescued from debt and potential death. In the previous section, she likely established how she used her own wealth to elevate his status, only for him to deny their secret marriage once he regained his standing.</meta>

...and made you a beautiful young man. I recall this to your mind, which you ought to remember and cannot deny. You, indeed, in return for such memorable benefits, have repaid me with these thanks: that you dared to deny a noble marriage—fortified by honest and holy testimonies and protected by such seals—and to despise your redeemer <note>original: "redemptricem"; Canniola refers to herself as his savior because she paid the massive debts that would have seen him imprisoned or executed.</note> and treat her as worthless, and were even able to stain her with base suspicion. 

You were ashamed, O man of insane mind, to have a widow born of a knightly man as a wife. Oh, how much better it would have been to be ashamed of having broken the faith you pledged! You have despised the holy and terrible name of God and shown yourself to be overflowing with these vices through your execrable ingratitude. 

I confess that I am not a royal woman. But since from my cradle I have been conversant among royal virgins, daughters-in-law, and wives, it is no wonder that my manners and spirit have surpassed those of royalty—which is enough for nobility and for assuming a royal station. 

But why say more? I will be easy with you in this, though you were difficult for me with all your strength: you denied being mine when you were. But now that I have proven you are mine <note>This likely refers to a legal victory where she proved the validity of their marriage through witnesses.</note>, I grant that you need not be mine of your own accord. Let yours be that royal brilliance—though it is now stained with the mark of infidelity; let yours be your youthful strength and your fleeting beauty. 

I, for my part, will remain content in my widowhood, and the fortunes God has granted me, I will leave to heirs more honorable than those of your race. Go then, unfortunate young man; learn at your own expense with what arts and deceits you may play with other women. It is enough for me that I have been deceived by you once; for which reason it is never my intention to be with you. Indeed, I judge that I should keep a celibate life, which is to be preferred to your embraces.

And having said these things, she withdrew herself from his sight, nor could she thereafter be moved from her laudable purpose by prayers or warnings. Roland, however, confused and late repenting of his cowardice, despised by all, with a dejected face, fleeing the sight not only of brothers but even of common men, departed into a miserable fortune, not daring to seek her again through fraud. 

The king and the other nobles marveled at the generous spirit <note>original: "Gerenofum... animum"; likely "generosum," meaning noble-minded or magnanimous.</note> of the woman, and they extolled her with wonderful praises, uncertain which was more commendable: that Canniola, contrary to feminine greed <note>A common medieval trope suggesting women were naturally miserly; the author highlights her behavior as exceptional.</note>, had ransomed the young man with so much money, or that, once he was ransomed and convicted, she had spiritedly despised and rejected him as unworthy.

She chooses a life of noble widowhood over a marriage to a man who shamed her, earning the admiration of the royal court for her magnanimity and self-respect.</summary>

# Concerning Joanna, Queen of Jerusalem and Sicily

Joanna, Queen of Jerusalem and Sicily, is certainly distinguished among other women of our era by her noble birth, her power, and her character. <note>Joanna I (1326–1382) was the ruling Queen of Naples, a title which traditionally included the claims to the kingdoms of Sicily and Jerusalem.</note> Concerning her, it seemed to me that to have left her out would be a cause for resentment; it would have been better to remain silent than to have written only a few words. 

She was the first daughter of the most Serene prince Charles, the renowned Duke of Calabria—himself the first-born son of Robert of famous memory, King of Jerusalem and Sicily—and of Mary, the sister of Philip, King of the Franks <note>King Philip VI of France.</note>. If we wished to trace the ancestors and great-grandparents of her parents to the very end, we would not stop until, through countless preceding kings, we reached Dardanus, the first founder of Troy <note>original: "Ilionis"</note>. Ancient tradition claims his father was Jove <note>The Roman god Jupiter. This reflects the medieval practice of tracing royal lineages back to classical mythology.</note>. From this so ancient and noble a lineage have flowed so many princes, world-renowned, that no Christian...

<summary>This page introduces Queen Joanna I of Naples, establishing her prestigious lineage through the French and Neapolitan royal houses and tracing her mythical ancestry back to Troy.</summary>
<keywords>Joanna I of Naples, Queen of Sicily, Robert of Naples, House of Anjou, Dardanus, royal genealogy, Trojan origins</keywords>

<meta>The previous page likely introduced the king or ruler being described, establishing his lineage and the state of the kingdom before his reforms.</meta>

...he still preserves the bright character of his ancestors. After his head was marked with the royal crown <note>original: "regio diademate insignita"</note>, rising up with such powerful virtue, he purged not only the cities and inhabited places, but also the mountain passes of the Alps, the remote forests, and the dens of wild beasts. He did this so that the hands of wicked men <note>original: "scelera hominum manus"; implying bandits or outlaws</note> either fled entirely in terror or shut themselves within high fortresses. Against these, he sent forth a column of armed men under an excellent leader; he would not lift the siege of such places until, once the fortifications were captured, he had punished those unspeakable men with the death penalty <note>original: "supplicio"; often referring to capital punishment or severe judicial execution</note>.

This was a feat that none of the preceding kings had either the will or the ability to accomplish. He brought the lands he possessed into such a state of order that not only the poor, but even the wealthy, could set out on a journey safely to wherever they wished, singing by day and night.

And what is no less beneficial, he restrained the distinguished men and the nobles <term>proceres|The high-ranking nobility or leading men of a realm</term> of the kingdom with such moderation, and reformed their loose morals for the better, so that they set aside that old pride by which they once held kings in little regard... <note>The OCR ends with "puipendeb," a truncation of "parvipendebant," meaning they previously looked down upon royal authority.</note>

--- Page 167 ---
<meta>This page contains the "Conclusio" or formal conclusion of Giovanni Boccaccio's famous work, "De Mulieribus Claris" (Of Famous Women). It follows the final biography—that of Queen Joanna I of Naples—and serves as an authorial afterword addressing his audience and potential critics.</meta>

### Conclusion

We have come down at last (as is clear enough) to the women of our own time. Among these, the number of "shining" <note>original: "rutilantium"; literally "glowing" or "reddish-gold," here used to mean illustrious or brilliant.</note> examples is so very rare that I believe it more honorable to bring my undertaking to an end here, rather than to proceed further through these modern times, which lead us into more corrupt ways. This is especially true since a most illustrious queen <note>This refers to Queen Joanna I of Naples, to whom Boccaccio dedicated the work. He views her as the pinnacle of the sequence he began with Eve.</note> has concluded what Eve, the first mother of all, began.

Yet I know there will be no shortage of people who will say that many women have been omitted. There will also be others who raise different objections, which perhaps could be rightly refuted. As for me, so that I may respond with humility to those first critics, I will freely admit that I have omitted many. Indeed, I could not have reached every woman before all others, because time—the vanquisher of fame—has consumed a great many of them. Nor was it granted to me to be able to see all those who still survive, and memory, when searched, does not always provide everything a writer wishes.

Indeed, lest they think me entirely forgetful, I want them to believe that it was not without my noticing that many women—barbarian <note>In this context, "barbarous" refers to non-Greek and non-Roman cultures, such as Germanic or Middle Eastern peoples.</note> as well as Greek and Latin, including the wives of Emperors and kings—were passed over. I have truly seen countless women and I know of their deeds, but when I took up my pen, it was not my intention to describe them all. Rather (as I testified at the beginning of this little work), my intent was to select and set down certain ones from the great multitude. Since I believe this has been done appropriately enough, any further objection is superfluous.

As for the rest, let it be said that I easily believe it possible—and that it may have happened—that some things in this book are less than perfectly correct. For quite often, not only does ignorance of facts deceive a writer, but even an excessive affection of the laborer for his own work. If that has happened here, I regret it. I ask, by the venerable glory of honorable studies, that wiser men bear with equanimity those parts that are less well-crafted. And if there is any spirit of pious charity in them, let them correct and amend what is improperly written—adding or taking away as needed—so that the work may thrive for the benefit of someone, rather than perish, torn by the teeth of the envious to no one's advantage.

->**Here ends the compendium of Giovanni Boccaccio of Certaldo, which he successfully published concerning famous women and their perpetual fame.**<-

<summary>In this concluding passage, Boccaccio justifies his selection of women, admits to potential errors or omissions due to the passage of time and human fallibility, and asks for scholarly charity from his readers.</summary>
<keywords>Giovanni Boccaccio, conclusion, Queen Joanna I of Naples, Eve, literary criticism, historiography, memory, Certaldo, famous women</keywords>

<meta>This page serves as the concluding summary of the preceding theological arguments regarding the Eucharist. It transitions from specific doctrinal points to a final appeal to the reader and a solemn warning to critics.</meta>

# Conclusion.

It is not, therefore, concerning a vain or futile matter (as some may think) that
this case is being conducted between us and our adversaries,
but concerning the highest and chief doctrine of light,
grace, and our salvation; in which, if any error is 
made, the whole Christian religion is put at risk. Therefore, while
there are many causes which impel us to make this 
confession <note>In this context, a "confession" is a formal statement of religious belief or a "confession of faith," rather than a confession of sins.</note>,
this is indeed the greatest:
that we recognize this doctrine to be true, and
confirmed by divine testimony. If we were to deny it,
we would bind ourselves in the greatest of crimes and
subject ourselves to eternal damnation. Because these things are so,
we exhort all pious people and lovers of truth to
read this confession of ours diligently, to
compare it with the sacred scriptures, and then to judge whether
we or our adversaries wander from the truth.
For we do not doubt that all who weigh this cause without 
prejudice will agree with us.
(May God, who is the author of peace and 
harmony, so enlighten our minds that we may finally 
meet in one and the same truth, to the glory
of His name and the salvation of His church. Amen.)
But if there should be anyone who wishes to slander this 
confession of ours, or interpret it in a <term>sinister way</term> <note>original: "in sinistram partem"; literally meaning "to the left side," but here implying a malicious or unfavorable interpretation.</note>, 
let him know that he declares war not against us, but against 
truth itself and God, who is the author of truth, whose 
judgment no mortal will be able to escape.

->Here ends the review of the doctrine of the Lord's Supper <note>original: "Coena Domini"; a common Latin term for the Eucharist or Holy Communion.</note>
collected from the lectures and writings of Master Philip Melanchthon.<-

<summary>This concluding page asserts that the theological debate over the Lord's Supper is a matter of salvation and truth rather than triviality. It ends with a prayer for Christian unity and a warning that those who maliciously misinterpret the text are opposing God Himself.</summary>

--- Page 169 ---
<meta>This page appears to be the closing statement or "Conclusio" of a biographical work, likely a collection of lives of famous women (resembling the style of Boccaccio’s *De Mulieribus Claris*). It follows the tradition of the "author's apology," where the writer justifies their selection and asks for the reader's leniency regarding omissions.</meta>

**Conclusion.**

Up to our own day, women among the ancients shine so rarely <note>original: "rarus rutil" likely from "raro rutilant," meaning they rarely glow or stand out in the historical record</note> that I might be considered more honest than brief were I to proceed. And especially since Nature, the mother of all, first began her work, it is certain that many women will have been omitted. For this, I could indeed be justly rebuked. I might respond that I have omitted many, even though I could have touched upon them all. However, time having been consumed <note>original: "absumptis," referring to time or resources being spent</note>, it was not possible for me to do so, nor could everything be recalled from memory. 

Indeed, lest I be entirely blamed for being inattentive, I acknowledge that there were very many wives of the August emperors and various queens whose deeds—and crimes <note>original: "facinoza" from "facinora," meaning bold deeds or, more often, notorious crimes</note>—I knew of, but did not wish to describe. I believe it was done appropriately because, from the great multitude of those I have already included, I think adding more would be superfluous, even if it were possible and if I had the leisure to persist. For a man often diminishes his own work by laboring over it excessively. 

I ask, therefore, through this venerable work, that where it is less well-made, you grant me pardon. Where the spirit of truth is lacking <note>A common humble trope where the author asks the reader to fix mistakes</note>, let others correct and emend it, so that the benefit of the work is not torn away from anyone.

<summary>In this concluding passage, the author defends their decision to omit certain famous women and queens from the text, arguing that brevity and focus are preferable to an exhaustive but tedious history. The author concludes with a humble request for the reader to correct any errors they might find.</summary>

<keywords>Conclusion, historiography, famous women, Nature, emperors’ wives, authorship, literary apology, editing</keywords>
